# THE BOOK OF CEREMONIAL MAGIC

->The Secret Tradition in <term>Goëtia</term><note>Goëtia: A term derived from the Greek word for "howling" or "moaning," used in Western magic to describe the invocation of spirits or demons.</note>, including the rites and mysteries of Goëtic <term>theurgy</term><note>Theurgy: Literally "god-working," this refers to high magic practiced with the intention of invoking divine or angelic help to achieve spiritual growth.</note>, sorcery, and infernal <term>necromancy</term><note>Necromancy: Historically, the practice of communicating with the dead to predict the future; in this context, it refers more broadly to dark or "black" magic.</note>.<-

## By ARTHUR EDWARD WAITE

> "Others are accustomed to call upon evil demons, bound by the power of divine names, and this is that type of Necromancy which is called malicious; or they turn to Theurgy, which is governed (as some believe) by good Angels and the divine power—yet most often it is carried out under the names of God and Angels through the evil deceptions of Demons."<note>original: "Alii damones malos virtute divinorum nominum adjuratos, advocare solent, atque hæc est illa Necromantiæ species quæ dicitur malefica: vel in Theurgiam, quæ quasi bonis Angelis, divinoque numine regitur (ut nonnulli putant) cum sapissime tamen sub Dei, et Angelorum nominibus malis Dæmonum illusionibus peragitur."</note> — ROBERT FLUDD<note>Robert Fludd (1574–1637) was a prominent English physician and occultist who sought to reconcile science and mysticism.</note>.

->London<-

->[1913]<-

->Scanned at sacred-texts.com, December 2001 – November 2002. J.B. Hare, Editor<-

->Bibliographic note: This is the second edition of this book; the first edition was titled *The Book of Black Magic* and published in 1898. The second edition contains substantially the same material as the first, along with some additions. — JBH<-

## PLATE I

FRONTISPIECE—The witch has shot a peasant in the foot with a magic hazel wand. The victim takes off his shoe when he feels his foot beginning to swell. If the peasant yields to the witch, the spell will be lifted. (Ulrich Molitor <note>A 15th-century lawyer and author of a famous treatise on witchcraft, *De Lamiis et Pythonicis Mulieribus*.</note> was a medieval demographer.)

## PLATE III

->Page 49<-

### Mystic Figures of the Enchiridion.
<note>The *Enchiridion of Pope Leo III* is a collection of prayers and charms, traditionally said to have been gifted to Charlemagne for protection and success.</note>

**Figure I.**, the mystic symbol of the **Tau** <note>A T-shaped cross.</note>, converted into a monogram which has been supposed to signify the word *Taro* or *Tora* <note>Words often associated with the Tarot or the Hebrew Torah.</note>. **Figure II.**, the triple **Tau**. **Figure III.**, an arbitrary figure supposed to represent the fortieth part of the height of Jesus Christ. **Figure IV.**, the Labarum <note>A military standard used by Roman emperors.</note> of Constantine, with the usual inscription, "In this sign you shall conquer" <note>original: "In hoc signo vinces."</note>, and the emblems of the Passion of Christ. **Figure V.**, a double door connected by a bar and inscribed with the first seven letters of the Latin alphabet. **Figure VI.**, a composite symbol of unknown meaning. The second circle contains twenty-two letters, which recall the keys of the **Tarot** <note>The twenty-two Major Arcana cards.</note>. **Figure VII.** represents the dimensions of the wound produced by the centurion’s spear in the side of Jesus Christ. **Figure VIII.**, a two-edged sword, for which various simple meanings may be guessed. Its inscription has been adopted by alchemists.

### Mystic Figures of the Enchiridion.

It was possibly misplaced through the ignorance of the printer, but it appears this way in many books that do not seem to be connected to Black Magic. **Figure V.**, a

The Magical circle used in Goëtic Theurgy <note>Goëtic Theurgy refers to a system of magic involving the summoning of spirits, specifically those found in the *Goetia*, the first book of the *Lesser Key of Solomon*.</note>, according to the *Lesser Key of Solomon the King* <note>A famous 17th-century grimoire or book of magic spells.</note>, showing the position of the operator, the divine names and symbols to be inscribed within and around the double circle, and the placement of the candles <note>original: "lights"</note>.

## PLATE IX

->Page 259<-

The Goëtic Circle of Black Evocations <note>The act of calling forth spirits or demons.</note> and Pacts, according to Éliphas Lévi.

The circle is formed from the skin of a sacrificial animal <note>original: "victim"</note>, fastened to the ground by four nails taken from the coffin of an executed criminal. The skull is that of a parricide <note>Someone who has murdered their parent or a close relative.</note>; the horns are those of a goat; the male bat opposite the skull must have been drowned in blood; and the black cat, whose head forms the fourth object on the circumference of the circle, must have been fed on human flesh. <note>The author notes that Lévi—a 19th-century occultist—likely invented these macabre details himself.</note> There is no historical authority for any of these requirements <note>original: "stipulations"</note>. The small circles within the triangle show the place where the operator and his assistants stand.

# PREFACE

The art known as "Goëtic"—being the art of incantation, sorcery, enchantment <note>original: "fascination"</note>, and the illusions and deceptions connected with them—has come, somewhat randomly, to represent the final result in devil-worship <note>original: "diabolism"</note> of the more universal <note>original: "catholic"</note> and general art termed "Practical Magic." This latter name implies that there is also a theoretical side to Magic, or perhaps a philosophy of the subject. This theory is itself divided into two kinds: in modern times, it has involved various attempts to provide an explanation or a working hypothesis for the alleged supernatural phenomena of the past; in ancient times, it presented itself with an air of authority, backed by the credentials of a specialized and secret knowledge. It sought to teach rather than to explain. 

Behind this, based on a specific assumption, stood the source of such authority: the school or schools that issued the "titles of expertise" which the records of the teaching master are supposed to show that he possessed. Within this tradition resided what was presumably "Higher Magic," which justified the original meaning of the word "Magic." This was the science of wisdom—a wisdom that resulted from the experience and knowledge unique to sacred sanctuaries during the era of the Magi <note>Ancient Persian priests and astronomers from whom the word "magic" is derived.</note>. 

In this way, a distant and abstract grandeur has been assigned to these practical magical works. However, between the reality of these practices as we know them and the "dream" of magic as it has been exaggerated in writing, there is the same relationship that exists between a great reputation and the failure to live up to it. If "Magic," in its proper and original meaning, is a synonym for wisdom; and if that wisdom—by virtue of the assumption I mentioned—were something unimaginably great, then it is certain that it had no causal connection to...

<summary>This page describes the ritual setup for Goëtic magic and introduces the preface, which distinguishes between "Goëtic" sorcery, "Practical Magic," and the idealized "Higher Magic" of antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Goëtic Theurgy, Lesser Key of Solomon, Éliphas Lévi, Black Evocations, Practical Magic, Magi, ritual circle, sorcery</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory critique of "Practical Magic" and its relationship to genuine mystical traditions. It transitions from discussing the nature of magical texts to critiquing a specific 19th-century English translation of the *Key of Solomon*.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>xxv</page-num>

...connection with the collection <note>original: "congeries"</note> of arts and processes which are understood as Practical Magic. As a mystic, I am certain that there was—as there still is—a true science belonging to the ancient sanctuaries. I am also certain that this science resulted in that experience which imparts wisdom. However, it did not correspond to any of the arts and processes to which I refer here, nor to anything that the mind can receive by attempting to elevate them. 

The consideration of such a possibility—which I have already dismissed—is therefore excluded from the inquiry I have attempted in this work. As will be seen, I have also excluded the distinctions that have existed between the "good" and "evil" sides of these arts. This is not because such a distinction does not exist on the surface, but because the two aspects dissolve into one another; they belong together in a root common to both. 

The actual question before us is in what way, if any, magical procedure draws from the secret traditions of the past, and thus how it fits into the general subject of such traditions, whether in Christian or earlier times. If it does draw from them, it could only be from the worthless side of tradition. It will not elevate a subject which the records of centuries have shown to be incapable of being raised; it will, however, let us know where we stand. Looking at the question directly, a tradition consisting of all kinds of rubbish is very likely to have been handed down from antiquity. In respect to occultism, the final scraps and fragments <note>original: "drift and scattermeal"</note> have passed into the Grimoires <note>Textbooks of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects like talismans and amulets, how to perform magical spells, and how to summon entities.</note>, *Keys of Solomon*, and other innumerable rituals by which "Art Magic" has been preserved in written records.

As this book represents a work originally issued in 1898—now under a new title and with many additions—I have taken the opportunity to indicate its position in relation to my far more important works which explain my interpretation of the Secret Tradition in Christian Times. I have achieved this goal (which is quite clear and simple) not by making a regrettable comparison between my other writings and this one, but by showing in a brief introduction the proper sense in which phenomenal occultism <note>Magic focused on producing observable physical or psychic effects.</note> and all its various arts connect to the tradition of the mystics. They are the path of illusion. It is through this path that the psychic nature of man enters that other path which descends into the abyss. 

In its current revision, this book necessarily remains a presentation of old texts in the form of a digest. I have added some new sections so that it may be more representative of the field. If the reader will pardon a touch of fantasy at the beginning—which is not entirely lacking in seriousness—the work itself is now an appendix to my introductory thesis. It provides the textual, historical, and other evidence by which that thesis is supported.

In the year 1889, a commentator on the more dry <note>original: "arid"</note> and unprofitable side of Kabbalistic doctrine <note>A form of Jewish mysticism.</note> edited an English version of a text on Ceremonial Magic entitled *Clavicula Salomonis*, or, the *Key of Solomon the King*. In an introduction added to the work, he stated that he saw no reason to doubt—and therefore presumably accepted—the tradition of its authorship. <gloss>1</gloss> Regarding his critical judgment, this statement summarizes his qualifications to be a "teacher of fools" <note>original: *mentor stultorum*</note>. It should be added, as further evidence, that he undertook his translation specifically for the use of "occult students"—that is, for those people who believe in the effectiveness of magical rites and may, as an expression of their faith, desire to put them into practice. With this...

<summary>The author argues that ceremonial magic is a "worthless" fragment of ancient tradition and clarifies that this book serves as a historical digest and an appendix to his broader works on Christian mysticism. He specifically critiques S.L. MacGregor Mathers' 1889 edition of the *Key of Solomon* for its lack of critical scholarship.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's introductory critique of magical literature. It completes the sentence from the previous page, noting that with few exceptions, the primary texts of ceremonial magic have remained hidden from the public in rare books and foreign manuscripts.</meta>

<page-num>xxvi</page-num>

With this exception, the large body of literature that deals with <term>Theurgic Ceremonial</term> <note>original: "Theurgic Ceremonial"; rituals intended to invoke divine or supernatural powers, often distinguished from "goetic" or dark magic</note> in its various branches has remained inaccessible to most readers. It has been hidden in rare printed books and even rarer manuscripts, both of which are mostly in foreign languages. There is likely a significant group of people—beyond those who actively study the occult—who might welcome a systematic account of magical procedures. They might view it primarily as a curiosity of ancient belief <note>original: "old-world credulity"</note>, but also as a valuable contribution to certain niche areas of historical research. However, a typical edition designed for occult students would likely discourage rather than attract these historical researchers. 

In this work, I have tried to consider several different interests. I approach the subject from the perspectives of bibliography and criticism. I have consulted every source of information known to me through many years of inquiry to ensure this account is complete. At the same time, I recognize there is a group of readers who do not mind being called "professed occultists." Regardless of my own view of their commitments, I am dealing with texts in which they have a primary interest. I have therefore addressed their needs in two important ways, which I believe will not offend those who are purely historical students. 

First, I have maintained strict technical accuracy. The ceremonial rituals described in this book are absolutely faithful to the original texts. This removes any need for a reader to consult the originals to clarify a doubtful point of historical magical procedure. For ease of reference—if I may modestly suggest it to such a circle—this version is actually superior to the originals. This is because I have organized the material systematically, whereas the original texts are often incomprehensible <note>original: "exceed understanding"</note> due to the errors of scribes, the misprints of early publishers, the sloppy methods of early translators, and, it must be added, the confused minds of the 

<page-num>xxvii</page-num>

original compilers—even the legendary "Solomon" himself was no exception. Those who are interested in the countless rituals of useless observance <note>original: "vain observance"; rituals considered to have no real spiritual efficacy</note> that make up <term>Ceremonial Magic</term> <note>original: "Ceremonial Magic"; the formal, ritualistic practice of magic found in grimoires</note> as it appears in books will, therefore, find them substantially intact here.

The second way I have considered the interests of the occult student is much more important, though they may not be as ready to accept the suggestion, given everything it implies. Robert Turner <note>an English translator active in the mid-17th century</note>, the translator of the *Magical Elements* <note>original: "Magical Elements"; the *Heptameron*, a famous book of rituals</note>—written, or more accurately, supposed to be written, by the ill-fated Peter of Abano <note>a 13th-century Italian physician and philosopher who was accused of magic</note>—describes that treatise as an introduction to "magical vanity." This term was perhaps used in a symbolic or specialized way to suggest that most things concerning the material world are equally trivial. 

Now, the deeper purpose of this present investigation is to provide those interested in the subject with the clearest evidence of the uselessness of Ceremonial Magic as it is found in books. I also intend to show the imaginary nature of the distinction between <term>White and Black Magic</term>—at least as far as the literature of either side is concerned. As for the things implied within or lying behind this literature, that is a different matter. Regarding that, I will only say for now that, judging by the results they have produced, they are not unlike the "second death" beyond the gates of hell <note>original: "perdition"</note>. It would be inappropriate for a writer with my known commitments to deny that there is a Magic which is...

<summary>The author explains that this book provides technically accurate versions of magical rituals to prove their ultimate futility. He argues that the traditional distinction between "White" and "Black" magic is largely a fantasy within the literature.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgic Ceremonial, Ceremonial Magic, Peter of Abano, Robert Turner, White and Black Magic, magical literature, ritual accuracy, occultism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of the "secret schools" of magic. He argues that the rituals found in books are often seen as inferior by self-proclaimed occultists, but he contends that the "higher" secrets they claim to possess are likely just more extreme versions of the same psychological delusions.</meta>

<page-num>xxvii, xxviii, xxix</page-num>

...behind Magic, or that even the occult sanctuaries possess their own secrets and mysteries. Their self-proclaimed <note>original: "self-imputed"</note> proponents claim that the written rituals we have are either a corrupt and scandalous mockery, a trivial and misunderstood version, or—in a more mild view—merely "as moonlight is to sunlight and as water is to wine." These proponents withhold their authorizations <note>original: "warrants"</note>. 

But whether they have these authorizations or not, it should be said at the outset: if these secrets and mysteries focus on psychic powers and wonders rather than spiritual grace, then God is not present in those sanctuaries. Let a mystic assure the occult student that because they are dealing here with the sickening follies of a distracted inner world, they would encounter those same follies carried to the absolute extreme <note>original: "ne plus ultra"; Latin for "nothing further beyond," meaning the highest possible point</note> in those alleged "secret schools"—assuming they could even gain entry. 

For this reason, these texts might actually be more harmless because they are so ridiculous and, for the most part, impossible to actually perform. This explains why it is acceptable to bring these various rituals out from centuries of obscurity. They would be horrifying <note>original: "abominable"</note> if we thought they were meant to be taken seriously. This criticism applies to all surviving <note>original: "extant"</note> rituals, regardless of their claims or outward appearance. Some are more absurd than others, and some are perhaps more wicked <note>original: "iniquitous"</note>, but they are all tainted by <term>Black Magic</term> in the same way that every "idle word" is said to be tainted by sin. The difference between White and Black Magic is simply the difference between a thoughtless word and an evil one.

Naturally, it would be unwise to claim that everyone who tries to perform these ceremonies would fail to get any results. In the past, the majority of such experiments likely produced results of some kind. Entering the path of hallucination is likely to guarantee hallucination. Given what we know about hypnosis, <term>clairvoyance</term> <note>original: "clairvoyant"; the alleged ability to gain information about an object, person, location, or physical event through extrasensory perception</note>, and a thousand similar phenomena, it would be absurd to think that the many "seeing" processes of <term>Ancient Magic</term> did not produce some form of <term>seership</term>. Similarly, the self-hypnotic state that many magical rituals tend to create likely induced such states in people who were susceptible—and not always only in them. To this degree, some of these processes are "practical," and to that same degree, they are dangerous.

For the sake of clarity, this book is divided into two parts. The first part provides an analytical and critical account of the main magical rituals known to me. The second part forms a complete <term>Grimoire</term> <note>original: "Grimoire"; a textbook of magic, typically including instructions on how to summon supernatural entities or create magical objects</note> of Black Magic. It must be remembered that these are the very practices that provided evidence for the <term>Inquisitors</term> <note>original: "Inquisitors"; officials of the Catholic Church responsible for finding and punishing heresy</note> of the past. These rituals justified the bloody <note>original: "sanguinary"</note> decrees that civil courts of that era issued against witches, warlocks, and magicians. It is, truly, a very strange and unsettling page of history.

# INTRODUCTION

THE mystic tradition in Christian times is preserved, apart from all questions and traces of formal secret societies <note>original: "Instituted Mysteries"</note>, in the literature of Christian Mystical Theology. It is a large and exceedingly scattered body of work; some of its most important texts are not available in any modern language. They stand in serious need of being organized <note>original: "codification"</note> and—if I may be so frank—even of being rewritten for a modern audience. 

However, if the entire collection of these works is a study that must be left to the experts, there is no person now living in Europe who does not have close at hand specific, simple, and separate texts—far too many to name—which are sufficient to give a general idea of the scope and goals of the tradition. If I were asked to define this literature briefly and comprehensively as a whole, I would call it "the texts of the way, the truth, and the life" regarding the ultimate mystic goal <note>original: "mystic term"</note>. 

This literature is not only full but exhaustive regarding "the way"—which is the path of the inner world, mental focus <note>original: "recollection"</note>, meditation, contemplation, and the letting go of everything lower in the search for everything higher. Perhaps the most universal <note>original: "catholic"</note> word for this process would be "centralization." The literature is also very detailed regarding the fundamental truth out of which it grows: the truth that a way does exist and that the way is open. This truth is formulated in all simplicity by the *Letter to the Hebrews*—that God exists and that He rewards <note>original: "recompenses"</note> those who seek Him out. I have cited this testimony on several occasions in this same context, and I do so here and now without apology or any sense of repetition. It can never be redundant to remember how the Divine ways are justified to humanity, specifically when humanity is seeking the Divine. The literature,

<summary>The author introduces the vast literature of Christian Mystical Theology, describing it as a guide to the "way, truth, and life" for those seeking a direct connection with God. He emphasizes that while the literature is complex, its core message—that a path to God exists and is open—is simple and accessible.</summary>

<keywords>mystic tradition, Christian Mystical Theology, Cædmon, Letter to the Hebrews, contemplation, meditation, spiritual centralization</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Christian mystical tradition, moving from the definition of its literature to the specific theological states of the "Divine Life" and the union of the soul with God.</meta>

In short, it is fully complete in what it understands regarding life—but this is the Divine Life. It is grace that fills the heart; it is the Holy Spirit of God that makes the spirit of human beings holy; it is life lived in God. There is no doubt that in its formulation, it was presented to the mind of Christian Mysticism as the "life which was hidden with Christ in God" <note>This is a reference to Colossians 3:3.</note>. This unspeakable concealment was equivalent to the open teaching of that mystery of symbolic <note>original: "emblematic"</note> death which lies behind all the rituals <note>original: "pageants"</note> of initiation. This was the state of being, and the doctrine upon which that state depends is defined by the First Letter of John <note>original: "Johannine Epistle"</note>, which affirms:

> (1) That God has given to us eternal life; (2) That this life is His Son; (3) That whoever has the Son has life; (4) That whoever is without the Son is also without life.

These points follow naturally enough from the testimony of the Gospel of John <note>original: "the Fourth Gospel"</note>:

> (1) In the words of the Divine Voice, saying: "I am the Way, the Truth, and the Life"; "I am the Resurrection and the Life"; "I am the Bread of Life." (2) In the words of the witness, saying: "In Him was life, and the life was the light of men."

There is no doubt, secondly, that the Divine Voice took human form <note>original: "was incarnate"</note> for Christian Mysticism in Jesus of Nazareth. We must reject the claims of those false witnesses who, from time to time, have pretended that the masters of the hidden life during the Christian centuries had become far too spiritually enlightened to tolerate the outward shell <note>original: "external cortex"</note> of their faith and creed. This point is much more important than it may appear in this context, for I am establishing a standard for judgment <note>original: "canon of criticism"</note>. 

I will take two typical examples, one of which is moderately early and the other late enough to serve as a point of comparison. The anonymous book *The Cloud of Unknowing* belongs, I believe, to the early part of the fifteenth century. It should be ranked among the most notable presentations of the conditions and methods of the "Union" with God that I have encountered in Christian literature. It offers an experiment in spiritual integration that seems more practical to me because it is more specific <note>original: "express"</note> than the great insights of Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite (c. 5th–6th century), an influential Christian theologian and philosopher.</note>. This integration is grounded on the identity of our essential nature with the Divine Nature and our eternal existence within it:

> "What you are, you have from Him, and He is that very thing"; and again: "Yet your being has always been in Him, without any beginning, from all beginning, from all eternity, and ever shall be, without end, just as He Himself is."

On the surface of these statements, there is enough similarity for a casual and careless reader to describe them as a simple presentation of the pantheistic <term>pantheism</term><note>The belief that God and the universe are the same thing, or that God is everything.</note> doctrine of identity. However, they are saved from this by an important qualification: that—despite this state of eternal Divine indwelling—man had "a beginning in the substantial creation, which was at one time nothing." 

This "beginning" signifies the emergence of the human spirit into a state of self-knowledge in "separateness," or some even more internal condition that we cannot accurately describe in language—at least not in language that would satisfy the higher part of our understanding. If it is conceivable that there is a possible state of distinction within Divine Consciousness by which the true self of our spirit became self-knowing, but not "separate," then it is this state which is called in *The Cloud of Unknowing* "a beginning in the substantial creation." It will be seen that I am implicitly setting aside the suggestion that this passage is a simple reference to the soul at physical birth. I do not think that a mystic whose greatest insights are so otherworldly <note>original: "exotic"</note> would offer a distinction like this as a...

<summary>The author explores the theological foundations of Christian Mysticism, citing the Gospel of John and "The Cloud of Unknowing" to explain the union between the human spirit and the Divine Nature.</summary>
<keywords>Christian Mysticism, The Cloud of Unknowing, Divine Life, Pseudo-Dionysius, Johannine theology, spiritual union, pantheism, substantial creation</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Christian mysticism, specifically exploring the theological concept of the soul's pre-existence in God and the mystical process of returning to Him as described in the 14th-century classic, *The Cloud of Unknowing*.</meta>

...qualification of the soul's eternity by integration in the essential nature of God <note>original: "Godhead"</note>, or, more correctly, by a unity of substance. What I believe the writer had in mind was, first, the implicit pre-existence of all souls within the Divine Being forever, and secondly, their unfolding—as if a living thought became a living word. However, there are no perfectly matching analogies for this. In this manner, there arose "the substantial 

<page-num>xxxiv</page-num>

creation, which at one time was nothing," and we know all that has followed throughout the past and the continuing ages of our separation from God. This state of separation is our sickness, and the way of return is our healing. That return, according to *The Cloud of Unknowing* and related works in this great literature, is 

> "the high wisdom of God's essence ... descending into the human soul ... and uniting it to God Himself."

The path is one of "undoing," though it is at this point that many mystics fear the unavoidable consequences of their own teachings: it is the return of the created substance into nothingness; it is an entrance into the darkness; an act of "unknowing" wherein the soul is completely stripped and unclothed of all sensory awareness of itself, so that it may be reclothed in the awareness of God.

It may well seem that in this "Mystical House" of unspeakable symbols <note>original: "ineffable typology"</note>, all the old order has passed away. The secret of reaching this state does not lie in meditation or in thinking, in realizing Divine qualities, or in calling upon <note>original: "invocation"</note> saints or angels. It is a work between the naked soul and God in His absolute essence—an essence so ultimate that 

> "it is of little or no benefit to think upon the loving-kindness of God, or upon the holy angels and saints, or even upon the glory and joys of heaven." 

That, and all of that, is honorable, honest, and good work, but it is not the material for building the Most Secret, Most Holy Temple, into which God and the soul enter and only one comes out. Yet, the old doctrine remains the true doctrine; nothing has been canceled and nothing has been diminished. In all but the deepest paths, it is appropriate, right, and healthy to seek the help of interceding angels and the communion of saints, to dwell upon the Passion <note>The suffering and crucifixion of Jesus.</note> of Christ, and so forth. The ancient histories are also understood in the traditional way: the Passion was no shadowy theatrical display; Christ died and rose in a physical body; in that body He ascended into Heaven, and no less truly in that same body He sits at the right hand of God the Father Almighty.

---

<page-num>xxxv</page-num>

And yet, these references to doctrine and practice—to symbol, rite, and ceremony—are only like the hills standing around Jerusalem. Into the "mystic city," the central place of this spiritual journey, they do not enter in any way. They have not been expelled; they are simply not there, because they do not belong there. Once more, it is a matter between God and the soul alone. It is as if the outer roads were filled with the grand processions of the Heavenly and Church Hierarchies; as if the Masses, Matins, and Vespers <note>Traditional morning and evening prayer services.</note> celebrated in marvelous and stately rhythms the Holy Trinity, the clear <note>original: "dilucid"</note> contemplation of the Divine Persons, the unspeakable secrets of the union of Christ's natures <note>original: "hypostatic state"</note>, and the mutual indwelling of the Trinity <note>original: "super-incession"; the theological term is *perichoresis* or *circumincession*, referring to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit living within one another.</note>. But after all these wonders, after rank upon rank of the Blessed Angels and all visions of the Great White Throne, it is as if a quiet center opened unexpectedly and through an...

<summary>This page discusses the mystical journey described in "The Cloud of Unknowing," where the soul must move beyond traditional religious imagery and doctrines to achieve a direct, "naked" union with the essence of God. --- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s ultimate journey toward God, moving from the vision of the divine to a total union with it. It compares the scholastic theology of St. Thomas Aquinas with the more radical mystical experiences described in *The Cloud of Unknowing* and the works of Jan van Ruysbroeck and Miguel de Molinos.</meta>

An immeasurable silence drew the soul down—from the many splendors into the one splendor, from the crowded cities of the blessed, from the things that exist outside in the realm of transcendence into the essence that is within all things—as if the soul saw there the one God and itself as the one worshiper. But after a little while, the worshiper itself has dissolved, and from then on and forever, it possesses only the consciousness of God. This is the knowledge of self, no longer attained by a self-reflective act of consciousness, but by a direct act within the unity of the infinite consciousness. In this mode of knowledge, there is that which knows even as it is known, but such a mode exists by virtue of such a union that the self does not remain, because there is no longer any separateness. 

It follows that the Divine Union—as I have sought to express it, set apart from all previous examples and the justifications of predecessors (of which I think, indeed, there are none)—is something much deeper and higher than what is understood by the **Beatific Vision**, which shines with all the lights of noon and sunrise and sunset at the summit of the mountain of theology. That Vision is more especially associated with **St. Thomas**, the **Angelic Doctor** <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the most influential philosopher and theologian of the Catholic Church.</note>, the mighty Angel of the 

<page-num>xxxvi</page-num>

**Schools** <note>The "Schools" refers to Scholasticism, the medieval system of theology and philosophy taught in European universities.</note>, expounding the Transcendence to himself in the most resplendent and spiritual terms of the logical understanding. The distinction between the Beatific Vision and the ultimate goal is that the one is the state of *beholding* and the other is the state of *being*; the one is seeing the Vision and the other is becoming it. Blessed and holy are those who receive the experience of God in that clear and bright <note>original: "dilucid"</note> contemplation; but sanctity, blessing, and the "all in all" is that state wherein contemplation is ineffably unified—by a supreme leap of love—with its object. In that love and in that joining together, there is no longer a passage from the subject <note>the person perceiving</note> to the object <note>God</note>. But this is the **Godhead**.

These considerations have moved so far beyond even ***The Cloud of Unknowing*** <note>A 14th-century English mystical text advising a "darkness" or "unknowing" of the self to reach God.</note> that it seems almost a descent into material concerns to speak, as I had intended, of **Molinos** <note>Miguel de Molinos (1628–1696), a Spanish mystic and founder of Quietism, who emphasized internal passivity.</note> and his ***Spiritual Guide***, which is in no sense truly comparable to the older work. It is a more ascetic treatise, and it is somewhat hindered by its asceticism; it is a less universal <note>original: "catholic"</note> treatise, and it suffers here and there from a narrow perspective. Yet it bears the same testimony of a full and complete intention—much too complete and full to feel like a staged performance—to maintain the veils of doctrine and to speak the high and orthodox language of the official Church. But again, it is like a moving, yet distant, echo from a world which has almost passed out of knowledge. 

What is there left for the soul to say of the **Holy Humanity**, of the **Precious Blood**, of the **five wounds**, or of the painful death and passion of Christ? It is not that all this has been swallowed up in the glories of the resurrection, but that those who have entered "where God keeps His Throne and communicates Himself with incredible intensity"—and those who have obeyed the last precept "to be lost in God"—have entered into a new order. The ships that carried them have dropped out of sight with the tide, with the breeze, and in the sunshine.

Now, the secret of this is—not that **Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-6th century theologian whose "Mystical Theology" deeply influenced later mystics.</note> and **Ruysbroeck** <note>Jan van Ruysbroeck (1293–1381), a Flemish mystic known as "The Divine Doctor."</note>, with all their fellow heirs and companions <note>original: "cohæredes et sodales"</note>, had become unitarians <note>meaning those who reject the Trinity in favor of God as a single person.</note>, but that the purpose of the Christian dispensation had been personally fulfilled in each of them. Christ had been born and lived, had taught 

<page-num>xxxvii</page-num>

<summary>This page discusses the distinction between the "Beatific Vision" (seeing God) and "Divine Union" (becoming one with God), arguing that true mystical union transcends even the standard theological teachings of St. Thomas Aquinas and the physical life of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Union, Beatific Vision, Godhead, St. Thomas Aquinas, The Cloud of Unknowing, Molinos, Ruysbroeck, Dionysius, contemplation, Christian mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Christ-life within the soul, explaining the "Secret Tradition" of Christian mysticism and the "Great Work" of attaining union with God, before briefly mentioning the existence of a "backward tradition" or dark path.</meta>

<page-num>xxxviii</page-num>

and suffered and died, had risen and ascended and reigned in them. So that Divine life, finally <note>original: "in fine"</note>, carried them to its last stage. It was not Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century Christian theologian and philosopher.</note> or Ruysbroeck <note>Jan van Ruysbroeck (1293–1381), a Flemish mystic.</note>, the author of *The Cloud of Unknowing* <note>An anonymous 14th-century Middle English book on contemplative prayer.</note>, or the soul of the poor imprisoned Jesuit Molinos <note>Miguel de Molinos (1628–1696), a Spanish mystic who was imprisoned for his "Quietist" teachings.</note>, but the Christ-nature within each and all of these—within ten thousand times ten thousand of their peers, in all ages and nations and faiths and climates—which entered into that incredible intensity. That which is termed the act or state of being lost in God is what I have elsewhere described using a perfection of all metaphors—which is my adaptation but not of my making—when Christ delivers up the Kingdom of each soul to His Father, and God is all in all.

This is the state which is beyond the state where it is said that "they shall see His face."

Of this is the mystic tradition in the Christian era; it has been perpetuated in an unbroken line from the beginnings of the new dispensation <note>The "new dispensation" refers to the period of the New Testament or the Christian era.</note> until this moment. It is, of course, in itself the most secret, exotic, and incomprehensible of all languages, though at the same time it is the most open, universal, and simple. The understanding of it is a question of experience, and that experience is attained in sanctity—though, as I have said elsewhere, the intellectual light concerning it belongs more to the dedication out of which sanctity may eventually emerge than to the state of sainthood itself. The technicalities of the occult sciences may seem hard to the beginner, and they are actually hard like the wilderness because they are barren wastes; but they are written in words of one syllable if compared with the little catechisms of eternal life, which are exclusive to the children of God.

Behind this *Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King* <note>The title of a famous 17th-century alchemical text by Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>—which is so like the eye of the needle—there is the concealed tradition in and behind the mysticism of Christian times. It is scarcely possible to speak about this here, and it will require some care not to confuse the image with which I have opened my statement. The Open Entrance, of course, leads to the Palace, but at a certain point, one finds an exceedingly hidden postern <note>A back door or private entrance.</note> and a path beyond. This path is absolutely unattainable except through the lawful entrance because, although the Kingdom of Heaven tolerates a certain quality of enlightened and loving violence, the sanctuary of all its sanctuaries responds only to the violence of that man who knows how to lay hands on himself, so that he may carry none of his external traits to the most innermost <note>original: *intrinsecus*</note> place in all the world of God. 

This postern is hidden deeply on the deepest side of tradition. However, by what can be traced concerning it, I think there has been such a going to and fro upon the Ladder of Jacob <note>A reference to Genesis 28:12, symbolizing the connection between Earth and Heaven.</note> that something more of those states—which are not the end, but perhaps the second-to-last step toward it—has been brought back by those who have accomplished all but the final stage of the Great Work <note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) is an alchemical term for the process of spiritual transformation and union with the Divine.</note>. I think further that they have gone so far that they have seen with their own eyes some intimacies of the end itself—the state of those who go in and never come back.

These are aspects of the Secret Tradition in so far as it has declared itself on the side of God. It remains now to be said that there is a backward tradition <note>original: *à rebours*, meaning "against the grain" or in reverse.</note>, and though it may seem very hard to put it so roughly and frankly, I have not taken all the consciousness of the inner man as my subject just to smooth over or reduce any of the distinctions between the loss and gain of the soul. The backward tradition is definitely and clearly that of miraculous...

<summary>The author describes the ultimate mystical union with God as the "Christ-nature" within the soul returning to the Father. This "Secret Tradition" is an unbroken line in Christianity, leading to a hidden sanctuary that requires total self-surrender to enter.</summary>
<keywords>Mysticism, Christ-nature, Secret Tradition, Great Work, Ruysbroeck, Molinos, Pseudo-Dionysius, Jacob's Ladder, Quietism, Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Secret Tradition," shifting from the heights of mystic union with God to the dangers of the "backward path" pursued by those seeking personal power rather than divine grace.</meta>

<page-num>xxxix</page-num>

power in the quest and attainment of it. It is summarized by the ambition of the <term>Magus</term> <note>original: "Magus"; a practitioner of magic or a sorcerer, often distinguished from a mystic by their search for power over the natural or supernatural world.</note> in contrast with the superficial desires and the hope which fills the heart of the true mystic. I do not mean to suggest that the <term>Magus</term> as such is necessarily in conflict with the <term>Decalogue</term> <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>, or that he is being judged solely by that standard for punishment or reward. Since this position can be stated as a fixed principle, and as such is not subject to change, I will express it as a dogma as follows: Whoever looks inward to find anything other than the Divine at their center is working toward their own loss. 

Just as there is the height of <term>Kether</term> <note>The highest point on the Kabbalistic Tree of Life, representing the "Crown" or Divine Will.</note> in <term>Kabalism</term> <note>A school of Jewish mysticism; also spelled Kabbalah.</note>, so there is the abyss which is below <term>Malkuth</term> <note>The lowest point on the Tree of Life, representing the physical world or the "Kingdom."</note>, and those who are seeking to exercise the powers of the soul apart from its spiritual graces are walking the downward path. The movement of grace is so entirely universal <note>original: "catholic"; here meaning all-encompassing or universal rather than referring to the Roman Catholic Church.</note>, and there is correspondingly so much divine protection <note>original: "prevention"; in traditional theology, "prevenient grace" refers to God's grace acting on a soul before any human effort.</note> operating everywhere, that in most instances these experiments result in very little. The wasting of effort usually stops simply because the seeker grows weary of the business. However, the quest for miraculous power—and I use this unscientific phrase intentionally, because I am now dealing with the most inexact of all subjects—is what is generally meant by the term <term>occult science</term>. Generally speaking, the <term>occult sciences</term> are the sciences of the abyss. 

I exclude <term>astrology</term> from this, which—only through the accident of many historical associations—has been forced into this category. It is not an <term>occult science</term>, and despite a few insignificant claims from certain schools, it has no secret method of operation whatsoever. It is simply a calculation of probabilities based on experience regarding observable things. Setting that aside, on the fringe of the whole circle there are also dozens of other foolish practices which I would not even call the stages of preparation for the abyss unless I had a reason to be unnaturally serious. I have described these enough in the main text, and here I will say only that all paths of folly lead to the Houses of Sin.

There remains the question of <term>Magic</term>. Regarding this, I am aware that the practitioners, who are many, and the

<page-num>xl</page-num>

amateurs, who are many more, may be inclined to interrupt at this point and call attention to the ancient and honorable distinction between <term>White and Black Magic</term>. But I have dealt with this so fully in the text that I wonder if the entire work is not an illustration of my argument: that, except in a very slight, verbal, and superficial sense, no such distinction exists—I mean to say that it has no roots in the deeper reality of the subject. 

However, so that I do not seem uncritical regarding things important enough to be viewed from different angles, I must make two further distinctions of my own. One of the secret sciences is, of course, <term>Alchemy</term>. Insofar as this was the method, mystery, or art of transforming metals, of healing physical human disease, or of extending human life by certain physical methods—to this extent it is, as it always was, a matter of scholarly research. Though I would not say that students of the old literature are in the least bit likely to discover these secrets from books, there is such an excusable and pleasant air...

<summary>The author distinguishes between the true mystic's search for God and the "Magus's" search for power, arguing that occult sciences seeking power apart from grace lead to a spiritual "abyss." He further dismisses the distinction between White and Black Magic and categorizes physical alchemy as a matter of historical research rather than spiritual progression.</summary>
<keywords>Magus, mysticism, Kabbalah, Kether, Malkuth, occult science, astrology, White Magic, Black Magic, Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "Secret Tradition," contrasting the spiritual side of alchemy with the dangers of the "abyss" or the "recremental deeps" of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>xli</page-num>

Regarding the quest and its enthusiasm, it is almost a relief to know that it poses more danger to one's finances than it ever will to the human soul.

Alchemy has, however, another and perhaps even more secret side, where it enters the "science of the soul." I distinguish this completely and entirely from occultism and all its methods; it is nearly and almost literally identical to that <term>postern</term> <note>original: "postern"; a back door or private side entrance, used here as a metaphor for a hidden path within the soul.</note> inside the first entrance of the "Closed Palace" <note>A common alchemical metaphor for the inner spiritual state or the hidden secrets of the craft.</note> which I have already mentioned. This back door, however, represents several methods of research that do not compete with and do not interfere with one another.

We shall soon come to a third distinction that is much closer to our daily lives than the other two; it will require some courage on my part to address. For this reason, I will save it for when the necessity arises at the end—which is, in fact, where it belongs.

Just as there is a door in the soul that opens toward God, so there is another door that opens into the <term>recremental</term> <note>original: "recremental"; related to waste matter or dross; here used to describe the "spiritual garbage" or waste products of the soul's depths.</note> deeps. There is no doubt that these depths flood in when that door is opened effectively. There are also the "powers of the abyss," and this is why it has been worthwhile to examine this subject seriously. I am thankful to say—and hope, under God, to continue—that I have no firsthand experience in these areas. Therefore, it must be understood that I am speaking here based on secondhand knowledge. I believe it should be understood that there is no sublimity in those depths; they are the cesspools of spiritual life and the pit of the "second death." Their powers are like those of a <term>pesthouse</term> <note>original: "pesthouse"; a hospital for people suffering from infectious diseases, such as the plague.</note>. They are as far removed from the dark terrors and splendors of Dante's *Inferno* as the gold bars of heaven and the stars and lilies of the *Blessed Damozel* <note>A reference to the poem "The Blessed Damozel" by Dante Gabriel Rossetti, which depicts a woman leaning out from heaven.</note> are far—and how far!—away from the divine Vision and the Union with God.

There is no specific reason to suppose that there is a "Black Sanctuary" or a "Hidden Church of Hell" opened to Christians. However, it may be—and by analogy, it seems there must be—a communion of "self-lost" souls, just as there is a "communion of saints." I imagine that the "Lords of its Convention" are to be feared in a certain way, like the Red and Black Death. But the short prayers, aspirations, and formulas which must be powerful enough at any moment to unlock all the gates of hell and tear down all its fortresses were taught to us almost at our mothers' knees. I believe that the prayer, "May the almighty Lord grant us a quiet night and a perfect end" <note>original: "*Noctem quietam et finem perfectum concedat nobis Dominus omnipotens*"; a traditional blessing from the prayer service of Compline.</note> would be enough to scatter whole armies, and not just the isolated "plague that stalks in darkness" <note>original: "*negotium perambulans in tenebris*"; a reference to Psalm 91:6.</note>. Furthermore, the *Pater noster* <note>The "Our Father" or Lord's Prayer.</note> is worth more than all the *Golden Verses* of Pythagoras, all the *Commentary* of Hierocles, and every oracle of Zoroaster—including the forged citations. In short, I do not think there is any power of the abyss, any "thrice-great" Magus, or any sorcerer in a state of final refusal to repent who possesses any charm, talisman, or spell that could look upon that one loving prayer without perishing:

<page-num>xlii</page-num>

> "Keep us, O Lord, as the apple of your eye; protect us under the shadow of your wings." <note>original: "*Custodi nos, Domine, ut pupillam oculi; sub umbra alarum tuarum, protege nos.*"</note>

<summary>This page distinguishes spiritual alchemy from mere occultism, warning that while the soul has a door to God, it also has a door to a "spiritual cesspool" of the abyss. The author emphasizes that simple Christian prayers are far more powerful against these dark forces than any ancient magical oracles.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the "Secret Tradition," specifically distinguishing between true spiritual growth and the various forms of magic and occultism.</meta>

<page-num>xliii</page-num>

It is unlikely that there is any secret science behind Magic, whether it is "Black" or "White." However, it should be noted that the occult sciences I am discussing here can be categorized under this specific heading, as the greater category always includes the lesser. The methods of magic are obvious to see, and the so-called "sanctuaries" of occultism might expand the collection of books, but they are unlikely to make the magic more effective. Regarding Black Magic—so far as there is a written reason for separating it from its sister <note>The author calls Black and White Magic "uterine sisters," meaning they come from the same source or mother.</note> who was raised on the same milk—I have shown that there is nothing to suggest even a touch of the sublime in devil-worship. In its pure—or I should say undiluted (forgive the term <note>original: *absit verbum*</note>)—state, it is simply the ambition and attempt to command demons. Note here that it is Satanism to deal, by definition <note>original: *ex hypothesi*</note>, with the abyss for any purpose whatsoever. In its worst state, it consists of <term>Grimoires</term> <note>original: "Grimoires"; textbooks of magic, typically providing instructions on how to summon spirits or demons.</note> and little books of wicked and incredibly foolish secrets. The difference between the *Grimorium Verum* <note>A 18th-century grimoire allegedly translated from Hebrew; it is notorious for its focus on black magic.</note> and the *Key of Solomon* <note>One of the most famous grimoires of the Renaissance, attributed to King Solomon.</note> is that the first deals openly with the devil and his messengers, while the other deals with spirits that are clearly in the same category but are addressed by kinder names. If it were possible to describe the motive of Black Magic in terms of a supposed spiritual height, it would be the hunger and thirst of a soul seeking to satisfy its cravings in the trash heaps of filth, greed, hatred, and malice. It is exactly comparable to the life of the character "Le Chourineur" <note>A character from Eugène Sue's 1842 novel *The Mysteries of Paris*; he was a violent but redeemable man who lived in extreme poverty.</note> in the novel *The Mysteries of Paris*, who lived on diseased scraps and grew to be satisfied with them. But that unfortunate man could not help his situation, while the soul of the black magician has usually sought out evil for its own sake.

I return, then, for a moment to that door of the soul which, as I have said, opens toward God; by a necessary but somewhat arbitrary distinction, this must be called the "door to the heights." In a proper understanding, the depths are as holy as the heights, and of course, in any true philosophical sense, there is neither height nor depth, for these are not journeys made through space and time. However, that symbolic door is the golden path to fulfillment. But this path does not consist of magic, divination, clairvoyance, or communication with spirits of any kind. It does not offer the legendary power over Nature that the <term>Magus</term> <note>original: "Magus"; a practitioner of magic or a sorcerer.</note> is said to seek, and which dishonest rituals have always claimed he could achieve. Instead, it is the hunger and thirst for holiness and the filling of the soul with it.

The word "clairvoyance" brings me to my last point and to the third distinction which I promised to mention.

The practice of occultism is, of course, comparable to the experimental science of the "psychic" side of things, which is being studied today with caution and intensity all over Europe and America. In one way, it is a poor compliment to make this comparison, because what has been distilled through the <term>alembics</term> <note>original: "alembics"; equipment used in alchemy for distilling liquids; here used metaphorically for the process of occult study.</note> of occultism is merely the waste and dregs of thought, intelligence, and motive. Psychical research, on the other hand, has been driven throughout by an honorable—often even a religious—motive. It has adopted a scientific method as far as the subject allows; it has made no grand claims and lets itself be judged by its results. From my perspective, of course, it is still very far from the final goal. I do not believe for one moment...

<summary>Waite argues that Black Magic is a debased pursuit of the "abyss" rather than a true science, contrasting the empty promises of magical rituals with the genuine spiritual path toward holiness. He also begins to distinguish between the "dregs" of historical occultism and the modern, more scientific approach of psychical research.</summary>
<keywords>Black Magic, White Magic, Grimoires, Satanism, Grimorium Verum, Key of Solomon, sanctity, clairvoyance, psychical research, spiritual heights, A.E. Waite</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the author's introductory argument, contrasting the "inward path" of true mysticism with the external methods of both ancient magic and modern psychical research.</meta>

...that anything responds to its methods from the unseen side of things which can bring good to humanity through such interaction. However, it must be remembered that every supernatural <note>original: "supramundane"</note> or abnormal fact recorded by this kind of research is simply more evidence added to the file of occult science. If the phenomena of psychism are as "psychical research" <note>A 19th-century movement, led by groups like the Society for Psychical Research, which sought to investigate paranormal phenomena like telepathy and mediumship using scientific methods.</note> has recorded them, then the old processes of Magic may be unquestionably authentic <note>original: "veridic"</note> processes within their own limits. Yet, they did not put the practitioner in communication with the archangels Raphael, Gabriel, and Uriel—at the highest level—nor with the demons Astaroth, Belial, and Lucifer—at the lowest—any more than

<page-num>xliv</page-num>

psychical research and spiritism have ever truly established contact with the souls of the faithful who have died. But both methods have produced extraordinary psychological states and the phenomena of the soul manifesting itself. 

The distinction between the two methods is that one—Magic—was usually the result of personally induced hallucinations, complicated by the frequent intervention of abnormal psychic facts, all following a more or less manic ceremonial. The other—Psychical Research—is the scientific investigation of similar and related states in subjects who are naturally sensitive to them, whom the researcher seeks to control. I have no reason to believe that the sober, ordered, and well-judged methods of such experimental research will succeed in bringing anyone to a level of certainty that will be of permanent value to humanity; as far as I am concerned, the matter is settled. The evidence, such as it is, points in the opposite direction. The path to certainty is found within the person, as reason dictates it must be, if God and His Kingdom are truly within us. Therefore, from the best and most balanced perspective, there is no reason to look in any other direction than toward that place which contains everything <note>original: "the All"</note>.

Two things only now remain to be said: First, it will be seen that from that part of the "Secret Tradition in Christian Times"—the details of which I used to open this discussion—there could never have been any connection to occult tradition and so-called occult science. Secondly, the work that follows will now allow the Rituals of White and Black Magic to speak for themselves regarding their own traditions and their value.

## THE LITERATURE OF CEREMONIAL MAGIC

# CHAPTER I

## The Antiquity of Magical Rituals

### § 1. *The Importance of Ceremonial Magic*

The standard fields of psychological study, which are mostly the domain of the pathologist <note>original: "pathologist"; here referring to early psychiatrists or those studying "abnormal" mental states</note>, are bordered by a fringe of hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> and questionable experimentation. While psychologists might occasionally venture there, it is mostly left to unofficial explorers. Beyond these fields and this border region lies the legendary world of wonders known as Theurgy <term>Theurgy: A type of magic intended to invoke the presence of gods or divine spirits, often for the purpose of self-perfection or achieving supernatural effects.</term>, Magic, and Sorcery. It is a world that inspires either fascination or terror, depending on the mindset of the observer, but in either case, it is the exact opposite of what is considered possible.

In that world, all paradoxes seem to actually occur, and contradictions exist together logically; the effect is greater than the cause, and the shadow carries more weight than the physical object. There, the visible dissolves into the unseen, and the invisible is openly revealed. Traveling from place to place happens without crossing the space in between, and solid matter passes through other matter. In this realm, two straight lines can enclose a space; space has a fourth dimension with unexplored regions beyond it. Literally and without exaggeration, the circle is mathematically squared <note>original: "the circle is mathematically squared"; squaring the circle is a famously impossible problem in ancient geometry, used here to illustrate the suspension of natural laws</note>. In this world, life is extended, youth is restored, and physical immortality is achieved. There, common earth turns into gold, and...

<summary>This page introduces the concept of Ceremonial Magic as a "wonder-world" where the laws of physics and logic—such as the impossibility of squaring a circle or matter passing through matter—are bypassed.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, Ceremonial Magic, Sorcery, Paradox, Fourth Dimension, Alchemy, Hesperides</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of the magical world as a place of wonder and creative power to a critique of its inconsistencies. It then outlines various perspectives on magic—believing, skeptical, religious, and occult—before focusing on the historical and literary aspects of the subject.</meta>

gold earth. In that world, words and wishes possess creative power; thoughts are things, and desire realizes its goal. There, also, the dead live, and the hierarchies of extra-worldly <note>original: "extra-mundane"</note> intelligence are in easy communication, becoming ministers or tormentors, guides or destroyers, of mankind. There, the Law of Continuity is suspended by the interference of the higher Law of Fantasy <note>original: "Fantasia"</note>.

<page-num>4</page-num>

But, unfortunately, this domain of enchantment is in every way comparable to "fairy gold" <note>In folklore, fairy gold is treasure that looks real but turns into leaves or dust when brought into the human world.</note>, which is presumably its currency. It cannot withstand daylight, the scrutiny of the human eye, or the balance of reason. When these are applied, its paradoxes become anticlimactic and its contradictions ludicrous. Its inconsistencies lack brilliance; its mathematical wonders end in mere wordplay. Its elixirs fail even as simple laxatives <note>original: "purges"</note>, and its transmutations do not even need a professional assayer to be proven false. Its magical words prove to be crude corruptions of dead languages, losing all power the moment they are understood. Even departed friends and planetary spirits must not be grabbed by their robes, for they are likely to slip out of them, and their garments are nothing like the miraculous mantle of Elijah <note>In the Bible (2 Kings 2), the prophet Elijah's mantle fell to Elisha, granting him Elijah's spirit and power.</note>.

This brief contrast shows that there are at least two points of view regarding Magic and its mysteries. First, there is the simple, unified view held by the few remaining believers within a "charmed circle" whom reason has not discouraged. Second, there is the view of the outside world, which is more complex and varied, though sometimes it is only considered more "reasonable" by assumption. There is also a third view, where legend is measured against legend and one wonder is simply replaced by another. In this view, it is not the persistence of natural laws against the Law of Fantasy; rather, it is the "bogeyman" <note>original: "Croquemetaine"</note> explained by the Devil <note>original: "Diabolus"</note>, or the runes of Fairyland interpreted through the lens of Hell <note>original: "Infernus"</note>. This is the perspective of the Church's rituals—the "Bell, Book, and Candle" <note>Referring to the rite of excommunication.</note> and the Law of Exorcism—and its ultimate expression is the "act of faith" <note>original: *auto-da-fé*; the public penance or execution of heretics during the Inquisition.</note>. For those who hold this view, the world of wonders definitely exists, but it is judged by the Holy Tribunal of the Church. It is not what

<page-num>5</page-num>

it appears to be, but is reinterpreted by the eye of faith through religious dogma <note>original: "light from the Lamp of the Sanctuaries"</note>. In short: its angels are actually demons, its water-spirits <note>original: "Melusines"</note> are blood-sucking monsters <note>original: "stryges"; mythical hags or birds of ill omen that fed on human flesh and blood.</note>, its phantoms are vampires, and its spells and mysteries are the "Black Science." 

At this point, Magic itself responds that there is both a Black and a White Art: an Art of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy and "high" magic.</note> and an Art of Canidia <note>A famous Neapolitan sorceress mentioned by the Roman poet Horace; representing "low" or malevolent magic.</note>, a science of the Heavens and a science of the Abyss, belonging to either the archangel Metatron <note>In Jewish mystical tradition, the highest of the angels.</note> or the demon Belial <note>A term used in the Hebrew Bible to personify wickedness, later identified as a demon or Satan.</note>. This creates a fourth point of view. However, all of these are in some way deceptive. There is the "positive illusion" of the legend itself, believed literally by its followers. There is the "negative illusion" which bluntly denies the legend without considering any underlying truth. There is the illusion that explains the legend away using an opposing theory. Finally, there is the illusion of the legend that tries to save itself by making a distinction between "good" and "bad" magic. Once these views are set aside, two truly important questions remain: the question of the Mystics and the question of history and literature. To a great extent, the first question is closed to discussion, because the issues it involves cannot be presented...

<summary>This section critiques the internal logic of magical traditions, contrasting the believer's "charmed circle" with skeptical, religious, and occultist viewpoints. It ultimately categorizes magic into Black and White Arts while preparing to examine the subject through history and mysticism.</summary>
<keywords>magic, folklore, Black Art, White Art, Law of Fantasia, superstition, religious perspective, mysticism, Hermes, Metatron, Belial</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Magic, transitioning from a critique of its logical inconsistencies to an analysis of the historical motives and literature behind it. The author identifies "Ceremonial Magic"—the art of communicating with spirits—as the central pillar of the subject's history.</meta>

with benefit to both sides in the public world of readers. As far as possible, this has already been addressed. Regarding the second point, it is the primary focus and goal of this investigation, and the extent of its importance can therefore be summarized briefly.

There can be no vast body of literature without significant motivations to account for it. If we examine the magical literature of Western Europe from the Middle Ages onward, we find that it is quite extensive. Now, the active principles that created that literature will also be found to govern its history. What is unclear in the literature can be understood with the help of the history, and vice versa; each influenced the other. As the literature grew, it helped shape history, and new historical events provided additional material for further literature.

<page-num>6</page-num>

There were, of course, many motivating principles at work, for both the literature and history of Magic are extremely complex. There are many interpretations of these principles that are often confused with the principles themselves—for example, the influence of what is loosely called superstition on the ignorant. These and similar interpretations must be excluded from an inquiry like this one. The main principles are summarized in the idea that there are a number of supposedly mysterious forces in the universe that could be controlled by humans, or at least followed in their secret processes. Ultimately, however, all these forces could be made secondary, or even subservient, to the human will. Even in astrology, which was the study of forces regarded as uniquely inescapable <note>original: "fatal"</note>, there was an art of management, and "The wise man will rule the stars" <note>original: *sapiens dominabitur astris*</note> became a fundamental principle of the science. This concept reached its peak or center in the doctrine of unseen, intelligent powers with whom prepared individuals could communicate. The methods by which this communication was attempted are the most important processes of Magic, and the books that contain these methods—called Ceremonial Magic—are the most important part of the literature. 

That is to say, this is the only branch of the subject that one must understand in order to understand its history. If Magic had been focused only on reading the stars, it would have had no significant history to speak of, because astrology required intellectual skills that were possible for only a few people. If Magic had centered on the transformation <note>original: "transmutation"</note> of metals, it would never have moved masses of people; instead, it would have remained what it still is today: an idealistic <note>original: "quixotic"</note> hope that exists far removed from the actual science of chemistry. We may look at the remaining occult sciences together, but there is nothing in them alone that would create history. By virtue of the integrated theory that has already been stated, they

<page-num>7</page-num>

were all magically possible, but they were all secondary to what was the pinnacle of it all: the art of dealing with spirits. The supposed possession of the secret of this art made Magic seem formidable, and therefore created its history. There was a time, indeed, when Ceremonial Magic threatened to absorb the entire circle of the occult sciences; it was the superior method, the "royal road." It achieved immediately what the others accomplished only through long and difficult labor. It had, moreover, the chief <note>original: "palmary"</note>...

<summary>The author argues that the historical significance of magic stems from the human desire to control mysterious forces, specifically through "Ceremonial Magic" or the summoning of spirits. This branch of the occult is identified as the primary driver of magical literature and history, outweighing astrology or alchemy in its social impact.</summary>
<keywords>Ceremonial Magic, Astrology, Alchemy, occult history, spirit communication, Western European literature, Middle Ages</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Ceremonial Magic as a "conventional art" based on specific formulas and linguistic processes. It discusses the social and religious impact of magic, contrasting it with astrology and alchemy, and argues for its importance as a subject of historical study despite its often irrational or "bizarre" nature.</meta>

<page-num>8, 9</page-num>

recommendation that it was a formal art, operating through definite formulas; above all, it was a process centered on words.

It was the fascination of this process that brought men and women—of all social classes and backgrounds—to the Black Sabbath and to the White Sabbath <note>The "Black Sabbath" refers to the legendary secret gatherings of witches for devil worship; the "White Sabbath" refers to similar rites intended for benevolent or "white" magic.</note>, and blinded them to the danger of the stake. It was the full and clear acceptance of this process as effective by both Church and State that lit the fires <note>original: "kindled the faggots," referring to bundles of sticks used as fuel for executions.</note> for the magician in every Christian land. Astrology was hardly discouraged, and if the alchemist was occasionally tortured, it was only to extract his secrets. There was no perceived danger in these practices, and therefore there was no judgment against them, except by their association with other arts. But Magic—specifically dealing with spirits—was what made even the peasant tremble. When the peasant shakes by his own fireplace, the king is not secure in his palace nor the Pope at St. Peter's, unless both can protect their own authority. Moreover, in the very claim of Ceremonial Magic, there was an implied competition with the essential claims of the Church.

Once the importance of Ceremonial Magic and the literature that embodies it is admitted as part of the history of the occult sciences, there is no need to argue that this history is a legitimate and reasonable study. In such a case, knowledge is its own reward, and there can certainly be no question regarding the significant influence exercised by the belief in Magic throughout the ages. However, in order to understand the literature of Magic, it is necessary to first obtain a clear principle for viewing it. It would be unnecessary to say that we must leave the legends as they are to those who work with folklore and dispute their fundamental value. We do not need to debate whether Magic, for example, can really "square the circle" <note>An ancient mathematical problem—proven impossible—of constructing a square with the same area as a circle; here used as a metaphor for performing the impossible.</note>, as magicians claim, or whether such an operation is impossible even for Magic, as would commonly be argued by those who deny the art entirely.

We do not need to seriously discuss the proposition that the devil assists magicians in performing a mathematical impossibility, nor its more moderate version: that the circle can be squared equally by those who invoke the angel Cassiel <note>An angel in Jewish and Christian mysticism often associated with the planet Saturn.</note> of the hierarchy of Uriel <note>One of the archangels in post-exilic rabbinic tradition and some Christian traditions.</note> and those who invoke Astaroth <note>In demonology, a powerful Great Duke of Hell.</note>. We shall see very shortly, as already indicated in the preface, that we are dealing with a bizarre literature that passes, through various fantastic phases, through all kinds of folly into actual crime. We have to account for these characteristics.

The desire to communicate with spirits is older than history; it connects with deeply rooted principles in human nature that have been discussed too often for it to be necessary to repeat them here. The attempts to satisfy that desire have usually taken a form that deeply offends reason. Between the most ancient processes, such as those of Chaldean <note>Refers to ancient Babylonia/Mesopotamia, famous in antiquity for its mages and astrologers.</note> Magic, and the rites of the Middle Ages, there are striking similarities. There is also something of a common doctrine—as distinct from mere intent, in which similarities would naturally occur—underlying them both. This is the doctrine of compulsion, or the...

<summary>The author argues that Ceremonial Magic was historically seen as a linguistic and formulaic art that directly challenged the authority of the Church. He asserts that the study of magical literature is a legitimate historical pursuit, revealing a progression from ancient rituals to medieval practices that often border on "folly" or "crime."</summary>
