<meta>This page continues the discussion on Ceremonial Magic as a word-based art. It moves from the historical impact of magic on society to the intellectual origins of magic rituals, specifically their connection to ancient traditions and Jewish mysticism (the Kabbalah).</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

The power that both forms claimed to exercise even over higher spirits through the use of specific words is a perfect example. When examining the Ceremonial Magic of the Middle Ages, we must therefore remember that although these texts look modern in how they are presented, they contain elements from very ancient times. 

It is unlikely that these ancient elements exist simply because humans in every age naturally happen to work toward similar goals in similar ways. A strange goal naturally tends to be achieved in a strange way, but in this case, the similarities are so close that they are better explained by the survival—whether through natural persistence or deliberate effort—of an ancient tradition following identifiable historical paths. One obvious path found in the literature is the use of Kabbalistic <note>original: "Kabalistic"; referring to Kabbalah, a school of thought in Jewish mysticism</note> symbolism, although this doesn't explain the tradition’s entire history.

There have been two ways to look at the vast and poorly understood literature of the Jewish Kabbalah, and these views lead to two ways of explaining the fake and ridiculous rituals that make up so much of Ceremonial Magic. It is viewed either as a pointless attempt at confusion—a collection of completely absurd books where confusing nonsense is declared with unnatural seriousness—or as a system of theosophy <note>original: "theosophy"; here referring to a system of religious philosophy or divine wisdom</note> written primarily through symbols. 

The first view is usually formed by those with only a surface-level knowledge of the subject. It is the most common opinion because it is the easiest one to find. It is only fair to add that this view is not very different from what many scholars have thought in the past. The alternative view is held by students of the literature who have prepared themselves by studying other parts of the Secret Tradition <note>The "Secret Tradition" refers to the idea of a hidden, spiritual wisdom passed down through the ages outside of mainstream religion.</note>. From the first perspective, it follows that Ceremonial Magic—which is distantly related to the Kabbalah—simply repeats its nonsense, perhaps making it even more exaggerated. From the second perspective, two mistakes often arise: giving too much importance to magic rituals because of their high-level connections, or—though this is rare—trying to treat these rituals as symbolic writing.

There is no reason to criticize the first conclusion; it is a logical one that most readers will find acceptable. Those who value Kabbalistic literature for its symbols (the inner meaning of which may be important), but see nothing significant in magic rituals, will be tempted to dismiss them as medieval frauds that must be separated from the true symbolic tradition. In either case, the ceremonial literature is disdainfully...

The author contrasts the view of Kabbalah as mere nonsense with the view of it as a deep symbolic system, explaining how both perspectives influence our understanding of ceremonial magic.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of magical rituals, specifically contrasting the skeptical view of Kabbalistic texts with the idea that they contain hidden, symbolic wisdom. <page-num>12</page-num>

rejected, and it follows in this way that alternatives which exclude one another both reach the truth as their final conclusion.

There is, however, yet another point of view, and it is of some importance, as it connects with that question of the Established Mysteries about which it has already been observed that very little has come to light. Most students of occultism are acquainted with hints and rumors of the existence in modern times of more than one Occult and more than one Mystical Brotherhood <note>original: "Fraternity"</note>, deriving, or believed to derive, from other associations of the past. There are, of course, many independent practitioners <note>original: "unaffiliated occultists"</note>, just as most mystics are independent, but the secret Brotherhoods exist, and the keys of magical symbolism are said to be in their possession. From a variety of isolated statements scattered throughout the works of professional Occultists in recent years, it is possible to summarize broadly the alleged position of these bodies regarding Ceremonial Magic. I will express it briefly as follows: 

There is no surviving Ritual, just as there is no doctrine, which contains, or can possibly contain, the real secret of magical practice or the essence of occult doctrine. The reason—whatever may be said by some self-appointed spokesmen—is not because there is any inexpressible <note>original: "indicible"</note> process, but because the knowledge in question is in the custody of those who have taken effective measures for its protection. Although, from time to time, some secrets of initiation belonging to this order have leaked through printed books into the world at large, the real mysteries have never escaped. The literature of Magic falls, therefore, according to this theory, under three headings: 

(*a*.) The work of supposed masters <note>original: "putative adepts"</note>, stating as much as could be stated outside the circle of initiation, and primarily designed to attract those who might be ready for entrance. 

(*b*.) The speculations of independent seekers, who, through thought, study, and intuition, sometimes attained genuine <note>original: "veridic"</note> results without assistance. 

(*c*.) Distorted versions <note>original: "travesties"</note> of occult doctrine, distorted versions of occult intention, and distorted versions of occult procedure, complicated by leaks from the superior source. 1

The opinions of professional occultists on any subject whatsoever are of no importance to me, and are mentioned only to

<page-num>13</page-num>

establish a point of view; but most Ceremonial Magic belongs to the third class, assuming that it still exists, like some other paths of Satanism. The first class, by its nature, is not represented in public writing, and the second is only represented slightly. In a word, Ceremonial Magic mainly reflects the outrageous <note>original: "egregious"</note> ambitions and incorporates the insane methods of medieval sorcery—above all, the Witches' Sabbath <note>original: "Sabbath"; a legendary gathering of those practicing witchcraft</note>. The additional elements are degraded applications of various Kabalistic methods, fortune-telling <note>original: "seering"</note> processes common among country people, and fantastic attempts to turn magical legends into a formal practice.

Whichever of the above views the reader may prefer to adopt, it will be seen that the net result regarding the Rituals is not fundamentally different: they are of literary and historical interest, but nothing more. For the occultist, they will possess, from their

associations—an importance which will be of no consequence to another student. It is desirable that they should not be undervalued as records of the past, because they have exercised an influence and are memorable as historical curiosities. However, it is even more desirable that the weak and gullible should be warned against acting like fools, and that those seeking spiritual certainty should be discouraged from the "science of the abyss" <note>The "science of the abyss" refers to the study of dark or demonic magic, often viewed as a spiritual trap.</note>.

7:1 Thus, the complex processes of astrology might, in theory, be skipped entirely in favor of summoning one of those numerous spirits whose job it was to provide instruction in astrology and an instant knowledge of it. It is otherwise obvious that the least "occult" of the secret sciences was rendered obsolete by an art that provided companion spirits who could see the past, present, and future. In the same way, Alchemy was unnecessary for a magician who could cause treasures to be transported from the depths of the sea or the bowels of the earth—and even from the royal treasury <note>original: "exchequer"</note>—in the more convenient form of common coins.

7:2 There is much the same reason for this distinction as for the one made between the Black and White Magic of the Rituals. Such meetings were either terrible or ridiculous, depending on the personality of those who attended them. As a whole, descriptions of them have probably been greatly exaggerated. Jules Garinet, in his *History of Magic in France, from the beginning of the Monarchy to our own day* <note>original: "*Histoire de la Magie en France, depuis le commencement de la Monarchie jusqu'à nos jours*"</note> (1818), suggests that monks, who took advantage of public gullibility for the sake of entertainment during their idle time, may have worn ridiculous disguises and committed the wild acts they blamed on devils. The same author claims, as certain and undeniable, that in all the criminal trials of sorcerers and witches, the scene of the Sabbath was always near a monastery. "Since the destruction of the monastic orders," he concludes triumphantly, "no more is heard of such meetings, even in places where the fear of the devil still exists." Add to this that seventy-five years later, Papus, the French occultist, would try to convince his readers that all the Books of Black Magic <note>term: "Grimoires"</note> were the work of priests, and the argument is as complete as French logic can make it. It is mentioned here to show that, outside the writings of demon-hunters <note>original: "demonologists"</note>, the Sabbath has been viewed more as a moonlight masquerade <note>original: "mummery"</note> where the presiding spirit was not the fallen star Wormwood <note>A biblical reference to a star that brings bitterness and destruction.</note>, but Venus <note>The goddess of love; here representing lust.</note>. Even in the theory about monks, the mysteries were focused on Priapus <note>A Greek god of fertility and sexual desire.</note> rather than Pluto <note>The Roman god of the underworld.</note>. The records of witch trials are highly suspicious; first, because there is no guarantee they haven't been edited, and second, because information forced out by torture is ultimately always exactly what the torturer intended to hear. They are also, at least largely, records of sexual delusion <note>original: "mania"</note>.

9:1 The Ceremonial Magic found in certain Greco-Egyptian papyri <note>These papyri, dating from the 2nd century BCE to the 5th century CE, are primary sources for ancient magical practices involving spells and rituals.</note> offers the closest similarities to the methods of the Kabbalistic school <note>The Kabbalistic school refers to Jewish mysticism, which often used divine names and alphabetic permutations for spiritual or magical goals.</note>, but they are the channel and not the original source. 12:1 In this connection, I may perhaps be permitted to quote a general statement on the subject which I once received from a correspondent who appeared to claim some kind of independent connection with the sources of secret knowledge.

> "Practical Magic is the science of the management of spiritual forces <note>original: "economy of spiritual dynamics"</note>, and is concerned with those theurgical <note>original: "Theurgic"; Theurgy refers to rituals intended to invoke the presence of gods or spirits.</note> processes whereby he who has trained himself for the purpose can—by virtue of powers inherent in man's spiritual constitution (but undeveloped in the majority of mankind)—enter into relations with the unseen intelligences to whom are assigned, in due order, the control of what are called natural forces. 'Ceremonial Magic'" (presumably not the type found in the common literature of ceremonies) "is Official Magic, in which the Magician, in connection with one or more assistants, acts as the representative of an occult Fraternity, who, for some very important end, wish to communicate with beings of a higher order than usual. For this purpose there is a recognized ceremonial, or rather there are two—the 'ceremony of approach' and the 'ceremony of the presence.' It is chiefly in the former that lights, incense <note>original: "fumigations"</note>, symbolic figures and numbers, and incantations occur, all of which have their use, either as credentials of authority or as weapons of attack and defense in the intermediate hostile region between the material and spiritual universe."

It should be understood that I cite this testimony merely as an illustration of a fantasy presented in the guise of exact science. I do not consider that the least credit should be attached to the statement by itself <note>original: "per se"</note> as representing an actual claim made by any secret fraternity; and the claim is of no consequence, even if it were true.

## § 2. The Distinction between White and Black Magic <note>1</note>

Having considered the possible standpoints from which these rituals may be regarded, we come now to the distinctions that are made between them. First and foremost is that which has been already mentioned and is artificially established between White and Black Magic. The history of this distinction is exceedingly obscure, but there can be no question that in its main aspect it is modern—that is to say, in so far as it depends upon a sharp contrast between Good and Evil Spirits. In Egypt, in India, and in Greece, there was no dealing with "devils" in the Christian sense of the word. Typhon, Juggernaut, and Hecate <note>Typhon (Egyptian/Greek), Juggernaut (Hindu), and Hecate (Greek) are deities or figures associated with destruction, chaos, or the underworld, but they were still considered part of the divine order.</note> were no less divine than the gods of

<summary>This page explores the origins of Ceremonial Magic, citing its similarities to Greco-Egyptian and Kabbalistic traditions while dismissing modern "theurgical" claims as fantasies. It also introduces the argument that the distinction between "White" and "Black" magic is a modern development rooted in Christian concepts of good and evil spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Ceremonial Magic, Greco-Egyptian papyri, Kabbalah, Theurgy, White and Black Magic, occult fraternity, spiritual dynamics, Typhon, Juggernaut, Hecate</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the history of ceremonial magic, moving from its ancient roots toward a critical analysis of how "White" and "Black" magic are defined and distinguished in occult literature.</meta>

the supernatural world, and the rituals of Canidia <note>Canidia was a famous witch mentioned in the works of the Roman poet Horace; she was often depicted performing dark or gruesome rites.</note> were probably, in their own way, as sacred as the peaceful mysteries of Ceres <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture; her "mysteries" were secret religious rites held in her honor, known for their focus on the cycle of life and death.</note>.

Each of the occult sciences was, however, liable to that species of abuse which is technically, but fancifully, known as Black Magic. Astrology—the study of celestial influences and how they operate on human nature and life—could be perverted into the creation of harmful talismans by using those same influences. Esoteric Medicine <note>Hidden or secret medical knowledge, often involving spiritual or non-traditional healing methods.</note> consisted of applying occult forces to heal human disease and included a traditional knowledge of the medicinal properties found in certain substances that were ignored by ordinary pharmacy. However, through malpractice, it <gloss>1</gloss> produced the secret science of poisoning and the destruction of health, mind, or life by invisible forces. The transformation of metals through Alchemy often resulted in their "sophistication" <note>original: "sophistication"; in this context, it refers to the adulteration or debasing of metals to make them appear like gold or silver.</note>. In the same way, Divination—the processes by which a state of "lucidity" or clear-seeing was supposed to be induced—became debased into various forms of witchcraft, and Ceremonial Magic descended into dealing with demons.

By definition, White Ceremonial Magic is an attempt to communicate with Good Spirits for a good, or at least a harmless, purpose. Black Magic is the attempt to communicate with Evil Spirits for an evil purpose—or for any purpose at all.

The contrasts established here seem, on the surface, perfectly

<page-num>15</page-num>

clear. However, when we come to compare the ceremonial literature of these two classes, we find that the distinction is not nearly as sharp as the definitions suggest. In the first place, so-called Theurgic <note>Theurgy refers to "god-working," or rituals intended to invoke the presence of a deity or beneficial spirit.</note> Ceremonial, under the guise of White Magic, usually includes rites for summoning evil spirits. If we assume they are summoned to be forced into performing tasks contrary to their nature, the issue immediately becomes complicated. White Magic would then have to be defined as the attempt to communicate with either Good or Evil Spirits for a good, or at least a harmless, purpose.

This, of course, still leaves a relatively clear distinction, though it is not one I would accept—if I were even willing to view the practical side of this entire subject as anything other than something to be unconditionally condemned. Yet the choice between a "good" goal and a "harmless" one creates even further confusion. As I proceed, it will be made clear that the goals and ambitions of magic are commonly very childish. Therefore, we must really distinguish between Black and White Magic not as a choice between essential good and evil, but as a choice between what is certainly evil and what might only be foolish.

Nor does this resolve the difficulty. As will also be shown as we proceed, White Ceremonial Magic seems to allow for a number of intentions that are objectionable, as well as many that are trivial. Thus, we must conclude that there is no very sharp distinction between the two branches of the Art. One cannot even say that Black Magic is always evil and White Magic is only occasionally so. What is called Black Magic is by no means always diabolical; it is concerned with ridiculous and stupid processes almost as often as the White variety is concerned with those of a cursed kind. Thus, the most that can be said is that the literature falls chiefly into two classes, one of

<page-num>16</page-num>

<summary>The author argues that the distinction between "White" and "Black" magic is often artificial, as both involve similar rituals and are frequently more characterized by foolishness or triviality than by true morality.</summary>
<keywords>Black Magic, White Magic, Astrology, Alchemy, Esoteric Medicine, Theurgy, Canidia, Ceres, Divination</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the history of ceremonial magic, moving from its ancient roots toward a critical analysis of how "White" and "Black" magic are defined and distinguished in occult literature.</meta>

which usually calls itself Black, except that they overlap one another.

In what might perhaps be called the "magical mind"—as distinct from the effects promised by the rituals—there has always been a clear contrast between the two branches corresponding to the Sage <note>original: "Magus"</note> and the Sorcerer. The fact that ceremonial literature tends to confuse this distinction may perhaps only show that the texts are corrupted. But this is not to say that they have been tampered with in the sense of being perverted by editors. White Magic has not usually been rewritten to become Black Magic; Goetic <note>referring to Goetia, a type of magic involving the summoning of demons</note> rituals have not been rewritten in pseudo-heavenly terms. They are, for the most part, naturally mixed, and it would be impossible to separate their elements without destroying their structure.

Modern occultism has adopted this clear distinction and developed it further. It appeals to a secret, traditional knowledge behind the written word of Magic—to a hidden science it believes exists behind all science, and to a religion behind all religion, as if the two were related or identical. This movement claims that the advanced occult life is entered by two classes of experts <note>original: "adepts"</note>, who have sometimes been imaginatively distinguished as the Brothers of the Right and the Brothers of the Left. These groups are said to seek transcendental good and transcendental evil, respectively, and in each case, they are something entirely different from what is conventionally understood as either White or Black Magic. As might be expected, the literature on the subject does not actually support this theory, but according to the theory's own logic, this lack of evidence is hardly considered an objection. Furthermore, while many rumors and a few questionable revelations might lead us to admit that there has been some revival of devil-worship in more than one European country,

and some modern attempts to communicate formally with the Powers of Darkness, it must be said that these attempts appear in their old, traditional forms. They are not dressed in the grand or terrifying qualities of the modern view. Parisian Devil-worship <note>original: "Diabolism"; this refers to the underground occult scene in late 19th-century Paris, which fascinated contemporary writers.</note>, for example—insofar as it can be said to exist—is the Black Magic of the <term>Grimoire</term> <note>a textbook of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects like talismans and amulets, how to perform magical spells, charms, and divination, and how to summon or invoke supernatural entities such as angels, spirits, and demons</note>. It is not the ultimate horror of the "Brothers of the Left-Hand Path," who wear their wickedness like a halo and are immortal in their spiritual evil. These mysterious characters are, however, the creation of fiction, as are their noble—or at least purified—colleagues <note>original: "*confrères*"</note>. Between Rosicrucian initiates of astral processes and the "lovers of the devil" <note>original: "*amatores diaboli*"</note>, there is undoubtedly one common bond: *there are no people more annoying than those people* <note>original: "*il n'y a pas des gens plus embêtants que ces gens-là*"</note>.

### § 3. The Manuscript <note>original: "Unprinted"</note> Literature of Ceremonial Magic

For the purpose of this inquiry, it is convenient to categorize the Rituals into three groups: Transcendental, Composite, and Black. This is subject to certain specific conditions regarding the first group (which will appear in Chapter 2) and to the understanding that all three are essentially one. Whenever possible, I will determine the age of individual Rituals during our examination. However, because this study is based almost entirely on printed books—with one very important exception—since only they have had a real influence on the public, it is helpful to conclude this introduction with some information about magical processes that exist only in manuscripts and can only be found in the public libraries of Europe. Almost without exception, these are inspired by the work mentioned in the preface, *The Key of Solomon*, and

<margin>p. 18</margin>

are therefore from a later period. The Arsenal Library <note>original: "Library of the Arsénal"</note> in Paris is famous for its collection of magical manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note>, but there is also a large collection in the British Museum that can be considered typical. There is nothing older or more important in the French collection than in our own; to settle the question of age briefly, none of our manuscripts date much earlier than the start of the fourteenth century.

The total number of these treatises, both old and new, is quite large. However, if we set aside the manuscripts <note>original: "codices"</note> used by the English editor of *The Key of Solomon*—since interest in those was satisfied long ago by that book's publication—there are only three small groups or "cycles" that are particularly relevant to this inquiry. The first is the "Honorius group," which includes three manuscripts; the second is the *Book of Raziel* <note>original: *Sepher Raziel*; a medieval grimoire often attributed to the angel Raziel</note>, which exists in two versions; the third includes the English manuscripts of the *Lesser Key of Solomon* <note>original: *Lemegeton*</note>. The most important manuscript of the first group is also one of the oldest magical treatises in the library: Sloane 313. This is a Latin manuscript written on vellum <note>a high-quality writing surface made from calfskin</note> that is in poor condition. The cramped handwriting and numerous abbreviations make it difficult to read. It is significant, however, because it connects to the *Book of Spells of Honorius* <note>original: *Grimoire of Honorius*</note>, one of the most famous and historically important rituals of Black Magic, attributed to a person of that name. It dates to the fourteenth century and has no formal title, but the text indicates it was known as the *Sworn Book of Honorius*. The introduction or prologue is vaguely worded in its opening pages, but it seems to explain the Church leaders' <note>original: "prelates"</note> condemnation of magic by claiming they were deceived by demons. The result is the

<meta>This page continues the survey of magical manuscripts, specifically describing the legendary origins of the "Sworn Book of Honorius" and its relationship to later Grimoires and the Solomonic tradition.</meta>

...gathering <note>original: "convocation"</note> of all the "masters of Magic," numbering 811 in total, all of whom seem to have come from Naples, Athens, and a place called Tholetus <note>likely Toledo, Spain, a city famously associated with magical schools in medieval legend</note>. Among these men, a spokesman was chosen named Honorius, the son of Euclid <note>original: "Euclidus"</note>, Master of the Thebans. He was appointed to work on behalf of the others. He sought counsel with an angel called *Hochmel* or *Hocroel* <note>original: "Hochmaël", likely derived from the Hebrew word for "Wisdom"</note>, and afterward wrote seven volumes of Magical Art, "giving the essence <note>original: "kernel"</note> to us and the empty husks <note>original: "shells"</note> to others." From these books, he is said to have later extracted ninety-three chapters, which contained a summary of the entire subject. He compiled them into a single volume which "we call the sacred or sworn book." 

In the meantime, the princes and high-ranking church officials <note>original: "prelates"</note>, having burned "certain fables or trifles," concluded that they had completely destroyed the magical arts and were therefore satisfied. However, the magicians took a secret oath among themselves to preserve Honorius's masterpiece in the most careful way. They agreed to make three copies at most. The owner of any copy was required to bury it before his death or otherwise ensure it was interred in his own grave, unless there was a trustworthy and worthy person to whom it could be passed down. It is interesting to note that this is the same *Law of Transmission* found in the study of Alchemy. 

The important point about this manuscript itself is that it identifies the source of the false <note>original: "mendacious"</note> tradition that attributes a Grimoire of Black Magic to a Pope named Honorius, as will be explained in detail later. The *Sworn Book* is not, of course, that Grimoire, but the existence and supposed authorship of the one helps us understand why the other was attributed to the same name. Honorius the sorcerer was confused with Honorius the Pope, first by the muddled legends of magic, and secondly by intentional deception. This happened in much the same way that Raymond Lully, the "illuminated doctor" of Majorca, was

---

identified with Lully the alchemist. This began with tradition and was soon followed by the creation of forged treatises. 

The *Sworn Book* is remarkable in other ways and has been strangely overlooked by writers on *Ceremonial Magic*. It may indicate that an association of magicians likely existed as an organized group during or before the fourteenth century. While the book is clearly Christian in its origin, it claims to be derived from the supposed works of Solomon. This suggests that the "Solomonic cycle" of magic was still being developed at that time. It also shows that the earliest versions of these spells are closer to the *Little Key* (also known as the *Lemegeton*) than to the *Great Key* <note>original: "Grand Clavicle"</note>. 

Regarding the magical operations found in the *Sworn Book*, they include both White and Black Magic without distinguishing between them. However, there is no trace of the typical "deals with the devil." The manuscript we are discussing is not necessarily the original; there is no way to know for sure. The British Museum also holds a later copy <note>original: "transcript"</note> from the sixteenth century, as well as a very valuable English translation written on parchment <note>original: "vellum"</note> in beautiful Gothic script <note>original: "characters"</note>. This translation is dated to the fifteenth century.

The second group of manuscripts consists of two items, both in the Sloane collection. Both contain, among other texts, the important and curious work attributed to Solomon titled the *Book of Raziel* <note>original: "Sepher Raziel"</note>. The manuscript numbered 3826 belongs to the sixteenth century. It is an English translation of a Latin original that I have not personally seen in its Latin form; the first line of the original Latin is usually provided at the start of each section. It is divided into seven books and claims to have been sent to Solomon by a prince of Babylon who was...

<summary>This page discusses the legendary "Sworn Book of Honorius," explaining its mythical origins, its role in the confusion between a legendary sorcerer and Pope Honorius, and its place in the development of the Solomonic magical tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Sworn Book of Honorius, Liber Juratus, Honorius of Thebes, Solomon, Grimoires, Raymond Lully, Sepher Raziel, Sloane Collection, British Museum, Solomonic Cycle</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of magical manuscripts, moving from the history of the "Sworn Book of Honorius" to the description of the "Sepher Raziel" and other Solomon-related texts found in the Sloane manuscripts.</meta>

...greater and more influential than any other man of his era, his name being Sameton, while the two wise men who brought the book were called Kamazan and Zazant. The Latin title of the treatise is said to be *The Great Angel of the Creator’s Secret* <note>original: *Angelus Magnus Secreti Creatoris*</note>. It was claimed to be the first book written after Adam, composed in the language of Chaldea <note>an ancient region in Mesopotamia</note> and later translated into Hebrew. It is a noteworthy fact that the first section of this work is titled *The Key* <note>original: *Clavis*</note>. If we consider the *Sepher Raziel* <note>The Book of the Angel Raziel</note> as being older than the *Little Keys* <note>original: *Claviculæ*</note>, it explains why a "Key" was attributed to Solomon. The "Key" in question is, however, concerned with the magical influences of the stars, "without which we can accomplish nothing." The second book is called *The Wing* <note>original: *Ala*</note>; it discusses the magical properties of stones, herbs, and animals. The third is the *Treatise on Incense* <note>original: *Tractatus Thymiamalum*</note>; the use of this specific term links it to the *Sworn Book of Honorius*. It deals with ritual fumigations <note>the use of aromatic smoke in ceremonies</note>. The fourth book outlines the times of the year, day, and night that are best suited for magical operations. The fifth contains the laws regarding ritual purifications <note>original: "lustrations"</note> and fasting. The sixth, called *The Heavens* <note>original: *Samaim*</note>, explains the nature of the celestial realms, the angels, and the specific tasks of each. The seventh and final book is concerned with the power of sacred names. A Latin version of the *Sepher Raziel* is found in the manuscript Sloane 3853, dated to the same period. It differs from the previous version in several major ways, being much shorter and filled with rare magical symbols.

The manuscripts of the third group are all written in English and are of a later date.

Sloane 2731 is a very tidy manuscript, begun on January 10, 1676. It contains the complete *Lemegeton*, or the *Lesser Key of Solomon*, in English. A further description of this famous work, which has surprisingly never been published, will be found in the third chapter of this section. Sloane 3648 is another manuscript of the *Lemegeton*, also in English, bound together with the *Notable Art* <note>original: *Ars Notoria*</note>—a book of invocations and prayers attributed to Solomon. Many copies of this latter work exist throughout England and Europe. <note>1</note> It is a work that is related to magic

<page-num>22</page-num>

without being a book of spells <note>original: "Magical"</note> itself. In fact, it stands in much the same relationship to the *Key* or *Clavicle* as the *Handbook of Pope Leo* <note>original: *Enchiridion of Pope Leo*</note> does to the *Introduction of the Arbatel* <note>original: *Isagoge of the Arbatel*</note>. Finally, the same manuscript contains the *Magical Archidoxies* of Paracelsus <note>a famous 16th-century Swiss physician and occultist</note>, though this version appears to be entirely different from the treatise of the same name found in the Geneva folio of his collected works. <note>1</note> In either case, despite its title, it is not a work of ritual magic. Sloane 3805 is a quarto-sized manuscript <note>a book size made by folding a sheet of paper twice to form four leaves</note>, primarily dealing with alchemy and medicine. It includes a translation of the forged letters of Sendivogius <note>a Polish alchemist</note>, and toward the end, the *Lemegeton*. The writer apparently started this with the intention of transcribing all the works attributed to Solomon under this angelic name. It stops abruptly, however, after describing the duties <note>original: "offices"</note> of the thirteenth spirit belonging to the Hierarchy of Hell.

It should be noted that these three groups of manuscripts contain material common to all of them. The independent treatises that follow the *Sepher Raziel* in manuscript Sloane 3826 extract material from...

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of magical manuscripts, specifically transitioning from the Sworn Book of Honorius to other lesser-known texts found in the Sloane collection of the British Museum.</meta>

the *Sworn Book*, while the one entitled the *Book of the Moon* <note>original: *Liber Lunæ*</note>, concerning the spirits <note>original: "intelligences"</note> of the mansions of the moon, the magic squares of the planets, their seals, rings, and so forth—which, by the way, seems in this form unknown to modern critics—has provided material for other and later collections.

The unpublished literature of Ceremonial Magic offers a considerable field for research, though even from a historical or bibliographical perspective, it is questionable whether it would be worth the effort, unless research of any kind is considered its own reward. Among the various manuscripts in the British Museum, it is only necessary to mention two here, as they contain materials connected with the current project.

Sloane 3884 includes a process in Necromancy—how to call the ghost of a dead body—the invocation of spirits into a crystal, the form for summoning spirits within a circle, and a method of exorcism in the Italian language <note>original: "Tuscan language"</note>—all boldly attributed to the author of *The Vanity of Magic* <note>original: *Nullity of Magic* or *De Nullitate Magiae*</note>, which is to say, Roger Bacon. In the second part of my current work, a special chapter is devoted to Infernal Necromancy, and the manuscript mentioned here will be useful for reference purposes. 

Sloane 3850 is a manuscript from the seventeenth century which contains transcripts from the fourth book of Cornelius Agrippa and from the *Heptameron* <note>The Seven Days</note> of Peter de Abano, originally in Latin. There is also a "Good and Proved Experiment" for summoning spirits <note>original: "evocation"</note> which uses the Our Father <note>original: *Pater*</note>, Hail Mary <note>original: *Ave*</note>, Apostles' Creed <note>original: *Credo*</note>, and the Litany of the Saints as magical formulas. Finally, there are procedures, mostly in Latin but some in English, for the discovery of lost things, the recovery of stolen items, for the spirits of the dead who cannot rest in their graves, and for people possessed by evil spirits. The treatise *The Nine Lamps of Solomon* <note>original: *De Novem Candariis Salomonis*</note>, containing strange figures and symbols <note>original: "sigils"</note>, deserves particular mention, as this again seems unknown to scholars. Despite its attribution to Solomon, it is clearly the work of a Catholic writer.

# CHAPTER II

## The Rituals of Transcendental Magic

### § 1. The Arbatel of Magic

The term "transcendental" must not be interpreted in any elevated or philosophical sense when it is used—informally and conventionally enough—in a context such as this. It has not been chosen because it is perfectly appropriate, but rather because there is no better word to define the claims implied by these texts, and because it has been used in this context before. It is perhaps roughly equivalent to the "High Magic" <note>original: *Haute Magie*</note> of Eliphas Lévi <note>a prominent 19th-century French occultist</note>. I have translated it as *Transcendental Magic*, not as a perfect equivalent, but because there is no other current or accepted expression that fits more closely. 

When we account for the ideas that supposedly underlie this subject, it must be said that Ceremonial Magic contains as much—and as little—"Transcendentalism" as the phenomena of modern spirit mediumship. Whatever might be claimed for the "intelligences" (spirits) that people try to communicate with, these spirits reveal themselves through their functions, which are either fantastic or frivolous. In this context, "frivolous" includes material interests, while "fantastic" includes goals that are impossible to achieve because of their absurdity and have nothing to do with spiritual growth.

Even if we assumed it were an exact science, there would be

<margin>p. 25</margin>

nothing in Practical Magic that connects, for example, with a true or even a vague Mystic Purpose. <gloss>1</gloss> Therefore, by "Rituals of Transcendental Magic," we must not understand a collection of methods by which the Divine within a human being seeks to unite with the Divine in the Universe. The works of St. John of the Cross, Ruysbroeck, Eckhart, Molinos, and Saint-Martin—even the *Imitation of Christ* by St. Thomas à Kempis—these contain the true "High Rituals" of genuine Transcendental Magic. Indeed, it would be a desecration to apply a term that is worse than ridiculous to those spiritual treasures by associating them with this subject. 

In this book, the term refers simply to those occult processes and so-called "Theurgic Ceremonial" <term>Theurgy refers to "god-working," or rituals intended to invoke divine or beneficial spirits.</term> that have no explicit connection to Black Magic. These rituals not only avoid dealing with evil spirits for wicked purposes, but they also appear to shun all such communication entirely, for any reason whatsoever. There is one exception—which

<summary>Waite defines "Transcendental Magic" not as high philosophy, but as a specific category of ceremonial magic (like the Arbatel) that avoids explicit Black Magic, contrasting it sharply with true spiritual mysticism.</summary>

<keywords>Transcendental Magic, Arbatel of Magic, Eliphas Lévi, Paracelsus, Archidoxies, Robert Turner, Theurgy, mysticism, ceremonial magic</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's comparison between Church rites (such as exorcism) and magical rituals. He argues that the Church's approach is more dignified than that of the occultists. He then begins a list of the few works that can be classified as "Transcendental Magic," including the Arbatel and the Enchiridion.</meta>

Properly understood, however, this is only a surface-level exception—one that should be made in favor of the procedure adopted by the Church for the expulsion of diabolical powers from living people. This is not because the symptoms of possession are necessarily anything other than medical <note>original: "pathological"</note> conditions, even in cases that appear severe and stubborn. Rather, it is because, based on the Church's own premises <note>original: "ecclesiastical assumption"</note>, the Rite of Exorcism is a far more dignified ritual than anything found in Transcendental Magic. 

In this matter, as in many other cases of much higher importance, no fair justice has been done to the position of the Catholic Church. It should also be observed that while Ceremonial Magic involves various processes that could clearly lead unsuspecting practitioners to experience the symptoms of possession, there is hardly a single process in any of the rituals—whether White or Black, Composite or Transcendental—that even claims to provide relief for those suffering in this way. <note>1</note> There is, therefore, no reason to doubt which side of these hallucinations produced the rituals. The sympathies of reasonable students will lie with the honorable institution that condemned these practices and sought to liberate the victims, leaving the reality of possession itself as an open question and, in this sense, a side issue.

->TALISMAN OF ARBATEL<-

<margin>The talisman includes the names PONTIEL, IQVIEL, GABRIEL, and ORIEL <note>These are names of celestial spirits or angels. Gabriel is a well-known archangel, while the others are specific to this magical tradition.</note></margin>

Even with the qualifications I have mentioned, the supposed Transcendental Rituals are very few. They are:
1. The *Enchiridion* <note>original: *Enchiridion*, a Greek word for a "handbook" or "manual"</note> of Pope Leo the Third. 
2. The *Arbatel of Magic*. 
3. The "Celestial Magic" of an anonymous German occultist, titled *Theosophia Pneumatica* <note>original: *Theosophia Pneumatica*, meaning "Spiritual Wisdom"</note>, which represents several other similar handbooks. 

Of these, the first is usually included among the Rituals of Ceremonial Magic due to the total ignorance of almost every person who has attempted to classify it. On the other hand, the...

<summary>The author argues that the Church's Rite of Exorcism is superior to magical rituals, which often cause rather than cure mental distress. He begins a short list of authentic "Transcendental Magic" texts, including the Arbatel and the Enchiridion.</summary>
<keywords>Exorcism, Catholic Church, possession, Transcendental Magic, Arbatel of Magic, Enchiridion of Pope Leo III, Theosophia Pneumatica, talismans</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of specific magical texts that the author classifies as "Transcendental Magic," focusing specifically on the history, authorship, and structure of the Arbatel.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

The third part derives all its importance from the second, which is clearly where the interest centers for both reasons. Regarding its origin, its authorship, and even its scope, there is, however, considerable mystery. To my knowledge, there are no manuscript copies—or at least none that date before the end of the sixteenth century. It appeared in a tiny volume in Basle and was dated 1575. <note>1</note> Back-dating and attributing authorship to famous figures are the two major bibliographical sins of grimoires <note>A grimoire is a textbook of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects, perform spells, and summon spirits.</note> and magical handbooks, and the antiquity of the *Arbatel* is somewhat suspect because of its literary connections. At the same time, it would require the knowledge of a typography expert to say for certain if the indicated date is reliable. The text is in Latin, but there is a slight possibility that it is the work of an Italian. <note>2</note> It makes a reference to "Theophrastic Magic," which indicates the influence of Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note>. Although it is difficult to speak with any certainty, it seems to hint at an early period of that influence—specifically the period of *Benedictus Figulus* <note>A 16th-century German alchemist and editor.</note>. This slightly predates the peak of Rosicrucian enthusiasm, which accounts for the lack of any Rosicrucian references; given the subject and style, such references would have been expected if the work had been written after the year 1610.

It should be observed that the *Arbatel* has no connection with the cycle of the *Keys of Solomon*, which will be considered later,

---

<page-num>28</page-num>

and it is filled with Christian ideas. The authorship is completely unknown. *Arbatel*, or *Arbotal* <note>original Hebrew: ארבתאל</note>, is probably not a pen name, but rather the name of an instructing or revealing Angel. The use of this Hebrew term is, however, peculiar considering that the references to the Old Testament are few and unimportant, while the sayings of Christ and the New Testament narrative in general are constantly cited. Furthermore, Solomon is not mentioned in the frequent lists of experts and wise men.

So much for the origin, authorship, and date of the book. It remains to be said that the work is incomplete. Of the nine "Tomes" <note>Volumes or sections.</note> into which it is supposed to be divided, we only possess one. It is not unlikely that the rest were never written, because the author has left us a plan of his entire proposal, and it is evident that his first book often overlaps with what should have followed. As it stands, the *Arbatel of Magic* is concerned with the most general rules of the magical arts—in other words, with the principles <note>original: "Institutions"</note>. It is entitled *Isagoge*, which means essential or fundamental instruction <note>original Greek: Isagoge</note>. The missing books are those of Microcosmical Magic, or Spiritual Wisdom; Olympic Magic, which is the summoning of the Spirits of Olympus; Hesiodic and Homeric Magic, concerning the operations of evil spirits <note>original: Caco-daimones</note>; Roman or Sibylline Magic, concerning guardian spirits <note>original: Tutelary Spirits</note>; Pythagorean Magic, dealing with the spirits <note>original: Genii</note> of the Arts; the Magic of Apollonius, giving power over the enemies of mankind; Hermetic or Egyptian Magic; and finally, that which depends solely on the Word of God and is called Prophetical Magic.

<summary>This page discusses the mysterious origins and Christian-influenced content of the Arbatel, noting its likely late 16th-century date and its incomplete structure. The author lists the intended nine volumes of the work, of which only the introductory "Isagoge" survives.</summary>
<keywords>Arbatel, Grimoires, Paracelsus, Benedictus Figulus, Isagoge, Olympic Magic, spirits, Christian magic, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of specific magical texts that the author classifies as "Transcendental Magic," focusing specifically on the history, authorship, and structure of the Arbatel.</meta>

<page-num>p. 29</page-num>

It is an open question whether all of these books could have been completed without a portion of that dangerous instruction which leads to blatant Black Magic. The *Isagoge* <note>An introductory treatise, specifically referring here to the *Arbatel*</note>, however, must be partially excused from such a charge; the Seven Septenaries <note>Groups of seven</note> of aphorisms of which it consists contain many moral and spiritual exhortations. Although these are not exactly original, they are on the surface quite acceptable and might indeed rank among the more high-minded of their kind, were it not for the magical system with which they are connected. The initial groups of these aphorisms serve to introduce the Ritual of the Olympic Spirits, who dwell in the heavens and in the stars, and among whom the government of the world is distributed. There are 196 <gloss>1</gloss> Olympic Provinces in the entire universe, divided so that *Aratron* has 49, *Bethor* 42, *Phaleg* 35, *Och* 28, *Hagith* 21, *Ophiel* 14, and *Phul* 7. These Provinces are termed "visible," but just as the *Seven Septenaries of Arbatel* cover the whole scope of reputed Transcendental Magic, so these seven successive multiples of the same mystical number likely indicate specific powers and duties. It is further said that the Olympic Spirits rule alternately, each for 490 years, which would cause mere confusion if separate groups of celestial spheres were permanently assigned to them.

The powers possessed by these "Intelligences" <note>Spiritual entities or beings</note> are described in a very curious way. They rule naturally over certain departments and operations of the material world, but outside these departments, they perform the same operations magically. Thus *Och*, the prince of Solar things, presides over the natural preparation or development of gold in the veins of the earth—that is to say, he is the Chief Mineralogist of Nature. He also presides over the faster preparation of the same metal by means of the chemical art—that is, he is the Prince of Alchemists. Finally, he can make gold in a moment by Magic. It is in this way

<page-num>p. 30</page-num>

that Ceremonial Magic connects with the Hermetic Art <note>The tradition of alchemy and esoteric philosophy</note> while assuming to transcend it. <gloss>1</gloss>

There is another curious instruction regarding the names and symbols <note>original: "characters"</note> of the Spirits. In opposition to much of the traditional doctrine of Magic, it is affirmed that there is no power in the shape of any symbol or in the pronunciation of any name, except in so far as a specific power or office has been ordained by God for both. The names, moreover, are not definite, final, or real names; this is why they differ among different writers according to how they were received. The only effective names are those delivered to a practitioner by the Spirits themselves, and even then, their effectiveness seldom lasts beyond forty years. Therefore, the *Arbatel* says it is better for the student to work only by the offices <note>The roles or duties</note> of the Spirits without using their names. Should the student be destined to master the Art of Magic, the other parts of that Art will present themselves of their own accord.

The sources of occult wisdom, it continues, are: firstly, in God; secondly, in spiritual essences—that is to say, the Angelic Hierarchy; thirdly, in physical creatures, which is likely a reference to the "signatures of things" <note>original: *signatura rerum*; a concept by Paracelsus suggesting that the physical appearance of objects reveals their hidden properties</note> of Paracelsus; fourthly, in Nature—that is to say, in a knowledge of the secret properties of natural things, such as herbs and minerals.

<summary>This section describes the Olympic Spirits of the Arbatel, detailing their governance over the universe in cycles of 490 years and their dual roles in natural processes and magic. It also notes the Arbatel's unusual stance that spirit names are temporary and that true magic comes from God-ordained roles rather than symbols.</summary>
<keywords>Olympic Spirits, Arbatel, Och, Aratron, alchemy, Hermetic Art, signatura rerum, Paracelsus, planetary magic, Angelic Hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the *Arbatel of Magic*, detailing the sources of magical knowledge and beginning the specific profile of Aratron, the first of the seven Olympic Spirits.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

precious stones; fifth, after a long interval, in the fallen spirits <note>original: "apostate spirits"</note> kept until the day of judgment; <gloss>2</gloss> sixth, in the administrators of punishment in hell—which seems to connect with the ancient Greek and Roman concept of avenging gods of the underworld; and seventh, in the people of the elements—namely, the Salamanders, Sylphs, Undines, and Gnomes (or Pigmies).

The secrets derived from these sources range from the highest achievements of supposed mystical science <gloss>1</gloss> to the mundane <note>original: "bourgeois"</note> ambitions of daily life. They span from the "Regeneration of Enoch" <note>A reference to the spiritual transformation or "translation" of the biblical patriarch Enoch</note> and the knowledge of God, Christ, and the Holy Spirit—which is the perfection of the Microcosm <note>The human being viewed as a miniature reflection of the entire universe</note>—to the gaining of honors and titles, the accumulation of wealth, the founding of a family, success in business, and effective housekeeping in both city and country. The extension of life, the changing of base metals into gold <note>original: "transmutation of metals"</note>, and the magical cure of all diseases, along with other "extraordinary claims of the highest science," also appear in the list.

Meditation, inner contemplation, and the love of God are the primary aids for acquiring the magical arts, along with great faith, strict silence <note>original: "taciturnity"</note>, and consistent fairness in the affairs of daily life. Finally, it is said that a true magician is born that way from his mother's womb; others who attempt to take on the role will be miserable. <gloss>2</gloss>

<image-desc>A magical sigil or character consisting of a horizontal base line from which three vertical lines extend upwards. The leftmost vertical is a simple line. The middle vertical is shorter and is crossed by a shorter horizontal line near its top. The rightmost vertical is a simple line. The entire figure is rendered in thick black strokes.</image-desc>

->THE SIGIL <note>original: "CHARACTER"</note> OF ARATRON<-

The powers and duties <note>original: "offices"</note> of the Seven Olympic Spirits are as follows: **ARATRON** rules over those things which are assigned astrologically to the planet Saturn. He can instantly turn any living organism—plant or animal—into stone; he can also change coal into treasure and treasure into coal. He provides familiar spirits and reconciles the spirits of the earth to mankind; he teaches alchemy, magic, and medicine,

<page-num>32</page-num>

reveals the secret of invisibility, makes the infertile fertile, and, lastly, grants a long life. He should be called upon <note>original: "invoked"</note> on a Saturday, during the first hour of the day, <gloss>1</gloss> using his magical symbol <note>original: "character"</note>, which was provided and confirmed by the spirit himself. <gloss>2</gloss>

->THE CHARACTER OF BETHOR<-

The affairs of Jupiter are overseen by BETHOR, who responds quickly when summoned. The person who honors his sigil <note>original: "character"; in this context, a "character" is a specific magical symbol or seal used to summon or command a spirit</note> may be raised to distinguished positions and may obtain great wealth. He brings the Spirits of the Air into harmony with humanity, so that they will provide truthful answers, transport precious stones, and create medicines with miraculous effects. BETHOR also grants guardian spirits <note>original: "familiars"</note> from the heavens and can extend a person's life to seven hundred years—though this is subject to the will of God, a condition that adds a note of caution.

Solar matters are governed by OCH, who extends life to six hundred years while maintaining the individual in perfect health. He grants great wisdom, provides excellent guardian spirits <note>original: "familiar spirits"</note>, creates perfect medicines, and transforms any substance into the purest of metals or into precious stones. He also grants gold and a purse, described in a charming way by the English translator of the *Arbatel* as "springing with gold." He causes the person who possesses his sigil <note>original: "character"; a magical symbol used to summon spirits</note> to be worshiped as a god by the kings of the entire world.

The governance of matters related to Venus <note>original: "Venereal concerns"</note> is entrusted to HAGITH, and the person who possesses his sigil is endowed with all beauty. He transforms copper into gold in a moment, and gold instantaneously back into copper; he also provides faithful serving spirits.

<image-desc>The Sigil of Phul: an occult symbol consisting of four vertical lines, each topped with a small circle. A horizontal bar connects these lines near the bottom. ->THE SIGIL <note>original: "character"; a magical symbol used to represent or summon a spirit</note> OF PHUL<-

Matters related to the Moon <note>original: "Lunary concerns"</note> are under the government of PHUL, who truly transforms <note>original: "transmutes"</note> all metals into silver, heals dropsy <note>a historical medical term for swelling caused by excess fluid</note>, and provides water spirits <note>original: "Spirits of the Water"</note> who serve humans in a physical <note>original: "corporal"</note> and visible form. He also extends life to three hundred years.

Legions of lower spirits are commanded by each of the Governors, who also have Kings, Princes, Presidents, Dukes, and Ministers ruling under them. Ceremonial Magic usually manages these hierarchies on a massive <note>original: "colossal"</note> scale. Invoking <note>original: "the invocation of"</note> the Governors is simple. It is performed during the day and hour of the planet that corresponds with the Olympic Intelligence using the following:

### *PRAYER*

> *O Eternal and All-Powerful <note>original: "Omnipotent"</note> God, Who has ordained the entire creation for Your praise and Your glory, as well as for the salvation of humanity, I beg <note>original: "beseech"</note> You to send Your Spirit N. <note>the "N." stands for Nomen, indicating where the magician should speak the name of the spirit</note>, of the Solar Race, <note>1</note> so that he may instruct me concerning those things about which I intend <note>original: "design"</note> to ask him [or—that he may bring me medicine for dropsy, etc.]. Nevertheless, not my will, but Yours be done, through Jesus Christ, Your only begotten Son, Who is our Lord. Amen.*

Unless the spirit—in the words of the translator Robert Turner—is "regularly devoted" <note>original: "familiarly addicted"</note> to the magician <note>original: "operator"</note>, he should not be kept longer than one hour. In either case, he should be "given permission to leave" <note>original: "licensed to depart"</note> as follows:

### *THE DISCHARGE*

> *Because <note>original: "Forasmuch as"</note> you came in peace and quietness, and have also answered my requests <note>original: "Petitions"</note>, I give thanks to God, in Whose Name you came. Now you may depart in peace to your own rank <note>original: "order"</note>; but return to me again when I shall call you by your name, or by your rank, or by your office, which is granted by the Creator. Amen.*

## Footnotes

25:1 By "Practical Magic," the author does not mean the alleged <note>original: "putative"</note> transcendent science said to be taught by mystical teachers <note>original: "occult initiators"</note>. That higher science is claimed to be totally different from "childish" attempts to find hidden treasure or obtain a ring of invisibility. Instead, this reference applies strictly to the magic found in Ceremonial Literature. I believe that this exotic form of magic belongs to the "depths" of worldly concerns rather than spiritual "heights" because it focuses on physical phenomena and procedures. However, it is worth noting this distinction here, which I have discussed elsewhere.

26:1 There are, of course, countless methods for breaking spells and enchantments; among them, one occasionally finds an exorcism for cases of demonic possession. There is one in the edition of the *Grimoire of Honorius* <note>a famous and controversial book of black magic attributed to a medieval Pope</note> published in Rome in 1760. It is claimed to be effective for both humans and animals afflicted by Satan. In the 1800 edition, there is another method which requires holy water for sprinkling <note>original: "aspersion"</note> on a human being. For an animal, it requires salt that has been ritually purified <note>original: "exorcised"</note> using blood drawn from the cursed <note>original: "bewitched"</note> creature. See also my comments on the Sloane Manuscript 3884 <note>a specific historical document held in the British Library</note>.

27:1 *Arbatel, Concerning the Magic of the Ancients* <note>original: "De Magia Veterum"</note>, Basel, 1575. The mottos on the title page are "The Highest Study of Wisdom" <note>original: "Summum Sapientiæ Studium"</note> and "In all things consult the Lord, and think, say, or do nothing that God has not advised you to do" <note>original: "In omnibus consule Dominum, et nihil cogites, dicas, facias, quod tibi Deus non consuluerit"</note>.

27:2 This possibility is supported by references in the 30th and 31st Aphorisms <note>short statements of principle or truth</note> to obscure points of Italian history. It is said that by a decree of the Magicians, it was ruled that no Italian should rule the kingdom of Naples. At that time, the monarch was an Italian, and he was eventually removed from the throne as expected. To restore the national ruling family, the decree must be canceled <note>original: "annulled"</note> by those who made it. A magician of even greater power might be able to enforce this, as well as the return <note>original: "restitution"</note> of a certain Book, Jewel, and Magic Horn, which had been stolen from the "Treasury of Magic." Control of the entire world would pass into the hands of whoever possessed these items.

29:1 The original edition of the *Arbatel* reads "186" and assigns 32 provinces to the spirit *Bethor*. This breaks the numerical pattern of seven <note>original: "progression of the septenary"</note> and is likely a printer's error. However, English and German translators have followed this error.

30:1 According to the magical theory <note>original: "hypothesis"</note>, this was also a gift from heaven, brought down by the Angel Uriel.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the "Arbatel of Magic," comparing its claims about spiritual power and the hierarchy of spirits with the theories of later occultists like Éliphas Lévi.</meta>

30:2 This reference, when considered alongside the topics intended for the seventh book, points decisively to the author's intention of including the governance of Evil Spirits within the framework of the *Arbatel*.

31:1 One should observe that mystical achievements are barely mentioned, and their attainment is granted by a spirit holding a specific office. This is conclusive regarding the use of the term, as I hardly need to point out. However, the author of the *Arbatel* considers it a high enough form of Mysticism to cause oneself "to be worshiped as a god" by virtue of the sigil <note>a magical sign or symbol</note> of *Och* <note>the Olympic Spirit associated with the Sun</note>—an opinion shared by the occultist Éliphas Lévi.

31:2 When his highest authorities disagree, the student of the occult is likely to discover the truth, finding occasionally that it lies neither at one extreme nor the other, nor even in the middle. It is informative to compare <gloss>p. 32</gloss> this explicit statement regarding a governing ceremonial process with another, no less explicit, which we owe to the man who reconstructed all magical processes and supposedly recovered the true practice of Magic. "Furthermore, certain physical constitutions are better suited than others for revelations from the occult world; there are sensitive and sympathetic natures for whom intuition in the astral light <note>Lévi's term for the spiritual medium that permeates the universe</note> is, so to speak, inborn. Certain afflictions and certain illnesses can modify the nervous system and, independent of the will, may convert it into a more or less perfect apparatus for divination; but these phenomena are exceptional. Generally, magical power should, *and can, be acquired through perseverance and labor*." — *Doctrine and Ritual of Transcendent Magic*, by Éliphas Lévi, English translation by A. E. Waite, p. 196. In this instance, the talent for making enlightened distinctions rests entirely with the later master. In fact, occult writers have always recognized that there is the "Natural Magician" and the "Magician by Art." *It either finds a person holy, or it makes them holy* <note>original: "Vel sanctum invenit, vel sanctum facit"</note>—this has been said of Magic. Similarly, magical knowledge means magical power; where it does not find power, it brings it. I speak, of course, theoretically <note>original: "ex hypothesi"</note>. But such knowledge does not come from Rituals or Grimoires, from the *Arbatel* or any other book; nor is the word Magic, nor the process itself, practical in terms of an external procedure. What is called Occultism is, in truth, the art of violating the innermost Sanctuary.

32:1 See Part 2, chapter 6.

32:2 It should be understood that the symbols <note>original: "characters"</note> of the so-called Olympic Spirits are, so to speak, their official seals. They are not to be confused with the images attributed to the planets. Those planet images were of various kinds, corresponding to a variety of influences, and were worn as talismans or charms.

34:1 This is merely a typical form, subject to variations depending on the spirit being summoned <note>original: "evoked"</note>.

## § 2. Spiritual Wisdom
->(Theosophia Pneumatica)<-

In the year 1686, the *Arbatel* was translated into German. In the same year—possibly in the same volume—there appeared, also in German, a work entitled *The Little Keys of Solomon* or *Spiritual Wisdom* <note>original: "Theosophia Pneumatica"</note>. <gloss>1</gloss> There are no copies of these editions in the British [Museum]...

The person who adapted the text had, however, been subject to other occult influences besides the *Arbatel*. He applies the term <term>Talmid</term> <note>Hebrew for "student" or "disciple"</note> <gloss>1</gloss> to the someone seeking to learn magic, a term which is not used in the original. The adaptation has been well executed and makes for additional clarity. The transcendental portions are slightly emphasized; it is said in one place that the elevation of prayer is the goal of the whole Mystery, and that such elevation will never be denied to the true seeker, who is recommended by no means to undervalue his own prayers. This is a better presentation of the theory than the corresponding passage in the *Arbatel* itself. There is also a supplement on Transcendental Medicine, which, so far as can be known, is original, and it is as curious as anything in the literature we are dealing with. <gloss>2</gloss>
