# Footnotes

35:1 *The Little Keys of Solomon, or Pneumatic Theosophy* <note>original: *Claviculæ Salomonis, vel Theosophia Pneumatica*</note>.

35:2 J. Scheible, *The Monastery: Secular and Spiritual. Mostly from Ancient German Folk-Literature* <note>original: *Das Kloster. Weltlich und Geistlich. Meist aus der Altern Deutschen Volks-Literatur*</note>, 12 volumes, Stuttgart, 1845-49.

36:1 There is a curious history attached to this word, but it can be explained only by a Hebrew scholar. It seems to be a late introduction, though it derives from the verb *to learn*. It has been said to represent a stage of initiation in certain secret societies of Islam.

36:2 As it is unlikely that *Pneumatic Theosophy* <note>original: *Theosophia Pneumatica*</note> will ever be printed in English, it may be useful to provide a summary of its appendix. This is the only section of the work that differs significantly from the *Arbatel*. It affirms that all things are threefold—from the Divine Trinity of Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, to man himself. Man is composed of a physical body, a sensitive soul, and a rational spirit. It defines these human principles as follows: The body is made of earth. The sensitive soul is made of the four elements but is influenced by the stars; it is the center of understanding and the source of talent for the arts and sciences. The rational spirit comes directly from God; its sphere of activity is heavenly and divine, and divine inspiration and influence pass through it to the physical body.

The body is a house where the soul and spirit live and perform their duties, having been "married" there by God. At the same time, they struggle against each other daily until the spirit overcomes the soul and achieves spiritual rebirth. There are two kinds of death: one resulting from the destruction of physical organs by disease or injury, and another resulting from the destruction of the sensitive soul by "poisoned" astral influences. In either case, physical decay and the departure of the divine spirit follow. The spirit may also be called back by the direct will of God, even without disease or injury. 

Man is therefore balanced upon three pillars; if one falls or breaks, the man dies. He should preserve and protect his body against infectious diseases, for a living organism can be preserved better than a corpse. He should also fight the poisonous influences of harmful stars, for God <note>original: *Elohim*</note> has gifted men with the power to create symbols <note>characters</note> that will destroy such influences. 

It is impossible, however, to cure those diseases with which God, in His mysterious compassion, may use to discipline mankind. In those cases, the best medicines are useless, the highest secrets <note>original: *arcana*</note> are in vain, <insert>for example, unicorn [horn],</insert> <note>original: *p. ex. unicorn.*</note> the "potent gold" <note>original: *potent aurum*</note> is in vain, the "spirit of gold" <note>original: *spiritus auri*</note> is in vain, the "quintessence" <note>original: *quinta essenda*</note> is in vain; even *Azoth* <note>a universal solvent or mercury in alchemy</note> is useless, and the philosopher's stone <note>original: *lapis philosophorum*</note> is made powerless. A skilled physician will immediately recognize such a case in a patient. All other diseases—whether coming from the planets, the natural decay of the earth, or neglect—may be cured by natural remedies like herbs, balms, spices, oils, metals, or alchemical preparations. When natural diseases become incurable, it is because of human ignorance or stubbornness. This appendix of *Pneumatic Theosophy* not only shows the influence of Paracelsus <note>a famous 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist</note> but also uses his specific terminology.

### § 3. The Handbook of Pope Leo <note>original: *Enchiridion*</note>

<summary>This page contains detailed footnotes explaining the alchemical and philosophical theories found in "Pneumatic Theosophy," specifically its Paracelsian view of the human composition of body, soul, and spirit. It also introduces the "Enchiridion of Pope Leo," another significant text in the history of ceremonial magic.</summary>

<keywords>Theosophia Pneumatica, Paracelsus, alchemy, threefold nature of man, astral influence, Azoth, philosopher's stone, Enchiridion of Pope Leo, J. Scheible</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the Arbatel of Magic to the analysis of the Enchiridion of Pope Leo III. The previous page concluded with a discussion of the relationship between the sensitive soul and the rational spirit.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

We now come to the *Enchiridion* <note>original: "Enchiridion" — a Greek word meaning a handbook or manual</note> of Pope Leo III. As previously mentioned, this is not actually a book of Ceremonial Magic. However, it is necessary to include it in this study and analyze it at some length to establish its true character. Misunderstandings and mistakes regarding a subject as obscure as magical rituals are generally excusable. In this case, however, errors are found where they should not be: specifically, among those who have claimed to provide an account of the work. Catholic bibliographers of the occult sciences—or at least the anonymous author of the occult encyclopedia in Jacques Paul Migne's <note>Migne was a 19th-century priest who compiled massive collections of theological and historical texts</note> great series—are very offended by the claim that it was written by a Pope. They label the *Enchiridion* as an infamous storehouse of Black Magic. Éliphas Lévi <note>A famous 19th-century French occultist and author</note>, who may have actually read it (since he occasionally seems to have glanced at his sources), exaggerates its importance to the occult by stating it has never been printed with its "true" illustrations. In the absence of any evidence, it is impossible to take this claim seriously. The *Enchiridion* is certainly not a book of Black Magic, nor does it require any illustrations other than the few simple ones that appear in it. Finally, Alfred Maury, in his book *Magic and Astrology in Antiquity and the Middle Ages* <note>original: "La Magie et l'Astrologie dans l'Antiquité et au Moyen Age"</note>, describes the *Enchiridion* as a work on sorcery that contains traces of Neo-Platonic <note>A philosophical system originating in the 3rd century that influenced later mysticism</note> and even older influences. He also clearly had not read it; as a person of such academic standing, he deserves criticism for following such a poor standard of research.

<page-num>41</page-num>

The legend of the *Enchiridion* is as follows: When Charlemagne <note>The King of the Franks and the first Holy Roman Emperor</note> was leaving Rome after his coronation by Leo III, the Pope presented him with a souvenir of the visit in the form of a collection of prayers. These prayers were said to have wonderful virtues. Whoever carried the small book on their person with the respect due to Holy Scripture, and also recited it daily to honor God, would never be defeated by their enemies and would pass safely through all dangers. They were promised that Divine protection would stay with them until the end of their lives. These events supposedly took place in the year 800. In the year 1523, the *Enchiridion* is said to have been printed in Rome for the first time. 

Looking at the broad outlines of this legend, there is nothing that seems impossible or that makes the Pope's authorship particularly unlikely. The alleged connection to occult science would actually be the strongest argument against it, because there has never been a literature so full of forgeries as the literature of Magic—except perhaps the related science of physical Alchemy. However, when we examine the work directly, the case against it changes, and it is condemned by its own words. While it is not a ritual of magic, it is also certainly not a simple collection of prayers designed to strengthen a person against the dangers of body and soul through the work of Divine Grace. Instead, it is a collection of charms <note>original: "charms cast in the form of prayers"</note> disguised as prayers. It is quite contrary to the devotional spirit of the Church; furthermore, it is much more concerned with gaining worldly advantages than spiritual ones. The work begins with a characteristic contradiction of its own claims by pointing out that of all the supreme princes of past ages, none was more fortunate than Charlemagne, and the source

<summary>Waite analyzes the "Enchiridion of Pope Leo III," debunking claims that it is a book of Black Magic while arguing it is actually a collection of worldly charms disguised as prayers, likely a forgery incorrectly attributed to the Pope.</summary>
<keywords>Enchiridion, Pope Leo III, Charlemagne, Black Magic, Éliphas Lévi, Alfred Maury, magical rituals, forgeries, charms, 1523 printing</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "Enchiridion of Pope Leo III," specifically addressing a forged letter from Charlemagne and the dubious publishing history of the work.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

He acknowledged his great prosperity in a letter of thanks addressed to Pope Leo. It is claimed that the original of this letter, written in the monarch's own hand, can still be seen in the Vatican Library. In the letter, he states that since he received a small book titled *Enchiridion*—filled with special prayers and mysterious figures sent by His Holiness as a precious gift—he has never ceased to be fortunate. He claims that of all things in the universe capable of harming man, not one has shown any ill will toward him. In gratitude, he proposes to devote himself and everything he owns to the service of his benefactor. The letter is in Latin; the monarch refers to himself as *Carolus Magnus* <note>original: "Carolus Magnus" — Latin for Charles the Great, known today as Charlemagne</note>, which appears highly unlikely for the period. He also addresses the Pope as the *Highest Bishop of Bishops* <note>original: "Summus Antistitum Antistes"</note>. While this title is not impossible, as the Papal claim to supreme authority over all bishops was fully developed by the beginning of the ninth century, the letter itself remains suspicious.

Needless to say, no such document is preserved in the Vatican Library. Furthermore, there are no surviving letters written by Charlemagne himself. Despite the encouragement he gave to scholars and the Academy mentioned by Alcuin <note>Alcuin of York was a leading scholar and teacher at the Carolingian court</note>, it is not at all certain that Charlemagne could either read or write. Finally, while it is true that his empire included Germany, as well as Holland, Belgium, Switzerland, and part of Italy, it is much more probable that after his coronation he would have referred to himself as the "Emperor of the Romans." In fact, there is no plausible claim to authenticity regarding this so-called "autograph" letter; to be precise, it betrays its own forgery, as I have already suggested.

Having established this fact, we may proceed to consider the alleged date of publication: Rome, 1523. This edition is mentioned by Pierre Christian in his *History of Magic* <note>original: "Histoire de la Magie"</note>. He defends the authenticity of the *Enchiridion* on the grounds, among others, that it went unchallenged in the "Eternal City" (Rome) during a papacy as significant as that of Clement VII. A second edition is said to have been printed in Rome in 1606. Between 1584 and 1633, it appeared four times in Lyons and once in Mainz. In 1660, it was published for the last time in Rome. 

Unfortunately for the purpose of this critique, only the editions from 1633 and 1660 have been available for study. The first claims to have been "very recently cleared of all mistakes" <note>original: "nuperrime mendis omnibus purgatum"</note>, but it has evidently been in the hands of a maker of grimoires <note>a "grimoire" is a textbook of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects and cast spells</note> and appears to have been edited and expanded to suit those interests. This much is certain, but it is impossible to say how much of the content—beyond the *Seven Mysterious Prayers* <note>original: "Orisons"</note> connected with the name of Pope Leo—was in the original, or whether the original itself was dated earlier than it actually was. Outside of these prayers, the modern tone of the work is unmistakable. It is difficult to understand how any educated person, much less a book-lover like M. Christian, could have been deceived by it. It is certain, however, that when Christian approached the secret sciences, their imitations, and their literary records, he relied more on his imagination than on actual knowledge or research.

The work itself, as already stated, is simply a collection of religious charms intended to be effective against all the dangers to which people of every rank may be subjected—on land, on water, from open and secret enemies, from the bites of wild and rabid beasts, from poisons, from fire, and from storms. While it promises protection against evil, it also provides...

<summary>Waite deconstructs the legendary origins of the Enchiridion, proving that the supposed letter from Charlemagne to Pope Leo III is a forgery and that the book is a later collection of folk-religious charms rather than an ancient magical text.</summary>

<keywords>Charlemagne, Pope Leo III, Enchiridion, Vatican Library, Pierre Christian, forgery, grimoire, religious charms, Seven Mysterious Orisons</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Enchiridion of Pope Leo III," detailing the specific instructions for using the book, the magical philosophy behind its talismans, and its connection to other occult traditions like the Arbatel and Natural Magic.</meta>

happiness in domestic matters and in the enterprises which contribute to prosperity and to the pleasures of a contented life. The proviso is that

<page-num>43</page-num>

"the instructions must be followed as accurately as human weakness will allow." Fortunately, they are simpler than those found in the Grimoires <note>A grimoire is a textbook of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects like amulets and spells.</note>. When a copy of the book has been obtained, it must be placed in a small bag of new leather so that it may be kept clean. A vow must be made to carry it as much as possible on one's person and to read at least one page daily with attentive devotion. If a specific danger is feared, a page suitable to its nature should be selected. Reading must be done while kneeling, with the face turned to the east: "so did Charlemagne invariably." Furthermore, works of piety must be performed in honor of the heavenly spirits <note>original: "celestial genii"</note> whose favorable influence one desires to attract; alms must also be given to the poor, "as this is of all things most pleasing to such spirits, for thereby we become their partners <note>original: "coadjutors"</note> and friends, the stewardship <note>original: "economy"</note> of the universe being committed to them by the Creator."

Here we have the magical doctrine concerning planetary intelligences which connects the *Enchiridion* with the *Arbatel* <note>The *Arbatel de Magia Veterum* is a 16th-century Latin grimoire focused on spiritual wisdom and the "Olympic spirits."</note>, and the hint of "secondary deities" which connects it with Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was a German abbot and polymath known for his writings on cryptography and magic.</note>. <note>1]</note>

The *In the beginning* <note>original: "In Principio" — the first chapter of the Gospel according to St. John</note>, or first chapter of the Gospel according to St. John, is declared to be the most powerful of all the devotions in the book, and it is to be recited the most frequently. The mysterious figures are said to have been extracted from the rarest manuscripts which antiquity has passed down to us, and their power is not only highly effective but so easily set in motion that it is enough to simply carry the work reverently on one's person. "Experience will remove any doubt which may be felt in this respect, while the concerns which may be caused by the idea that there is magic or"

<page-num>44</page-num>

superstition herein will be vanished by a slight exercise of reason.

As to this latter point, it is said that a little reflection upon the infinite number of secret sympathies and antipathies <note>The concept that certain things in nature are inherently attracted to or repelled by one another.</note> found in different beings here below will explain how it is that such figures may be in harmony with the Celestial Intelligences which govern this vast universe.

It will scarcely be necessary to observe that the doctrine of sympathies and antipathies is the very essence of Natural Magic <note>A branch of magic dealing with the hidden powers of nature (herbs, stones, stars) rather than the summoning of demons.</note>, and connects it with the more hidden branches of the study. The mysterious figures referred to were originally nine in number, and in most cases appear several times. The most prominent is the *Standard of Constantine* <note>original: "Labarum" — a military standard featuring the Chi-Rho symbol (the first two letters of "Christ" in Greek) used by Emperor Constantine the Great</note> and the *Tau* symbol <note>A cross shaped like the letter T, often associated with salvation or protection in occultism</note>, which Lévi <note>Éliphas Lévi (1810–1875) was a highly influential French occultist and author.</note> connects with the Tarot.

The apparently meaningless listing of various Divine Names is a special characteristic of Ceremonial Magic, and certainly makes the *Enchiridion* link with a cycle of literature from which it is otherwise slightly distinct. There is, indeed, little

<summary>This page outlines the ritual requirements for using the "Enchiridion," such as carrying it in a leather bag and performing acts of charity, while explaining the work's philosophical roots in the doctrine of "sympathies" and "planetary intelligences."</summary>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Enchiridion of Pope Leo III, detailing the specific prayers, religious texts, and magical rituals it contains, as well as its claims of divine authority.</meta>

the specific difference between the prayers that include these names and the invocations that overflow in the rituals. It should be added that the use of these Divine Names is supported by a dishonest <note>original: "mendacious"</note> reference to the *Angelical Theology* of Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian whose writings on the hierarchy of angels were highly influential in medieval mysticism.</note>.

The introductory material ends at this point. The prose of the Gospel of John follows, accompanied by short rhythmic verses <note>original: "versicles"</note> and a prayer. Next come the Seven Penitential Psalms and the Litany of the Saints, after which are the *Mysterious Prayers of Pope Leo*, followed by many others equally mysterious, which are said to be effective against human weakness <note>original: "fragility"</note>, and so on. There are prayers for voyages and prayers addressed to the Cross; then, under the Tau symbol <note>The letter T, often used in magic and religion as a symbol of protection or salvation.</note>, beginning with the strange exclamation, "By the sign of the Lord Tau, deliver me" <note>original: "Per signum Domini Tau, libera me"</note>, there follows a long

conjuration, as explicit as anything in magic, designed to prevent the person praying from being injured by any steel weapons whatsoever. Forming part of this ceremony is the false letter <note>original: "pseudo-epistle"</note> supposedly written by Jesus Christ to King Abgar <note>A legendary correspondence between Jesus and Abgar V of Edessa, which was historically used as a protective charm or amulet.</note>, explaining why our Savior could not come in person to that monarch and promising to send His disciple Thaddeus once He had fulfilled the work given to Him by His Father. The text goes on to say that Christ wrote it with His own hand, and that wherever the recipient might be—in a house or field, by sea or stream, "whether in the battle of Pagans or Christians" <note>original: "sive in prælio Paganorum seu Christianorum"</note>—an absurdity <note>original: "stultification"</note> given the context—his enemy shall never defeat him. 1 The king received the letter with many tears and prayers, and after all this is duly described, the conjuration of "staffs, swords, spears, long-swords, knives, arrows, keys, ropes, and all other kinds of weapons" <note>original: "baculi, gladii, lanceæ, enses, cultelli, sagittæ, claves, funes, et omnia alia genera armorum"</note> continues.

Just as it is difficult to say where the original *Enchiridion* <note>A Greek word meaning "handbook" or "manual."</note> actually begins, it is also uncertain where it ends. A variety of miscellaneous prayers are, however, attributed to well-known saints from well after the Carolingian <note>original: "Carlovingian"; the era of Charlemagne (8th-9th century), to which the book's origin is falsely attributed.</note> period, and to Popes Innocent IV and John XX, without ruling out yet another prayer <note>original: "orison"</note> by the great Pope Leo himself. Then come the "strange secrets"—to win favor and discover one's own guiding spirit <note>original: "proper genius"</note>, to become invulnerable, to prevent a gun from firing, and to see a future husband or wife. All of these are achieved by means of formal prayers—a kind of easy path to the main goals of magic, seemingly without exceeding the religious discipline of the Church.

To complete the analysis of this strange collection, its most important practical part is added here:

> *He had said to them: I am He, and they fell backward upon the earth. Then He asked them again: Whom do you seek? And they said: Jesus of Nazareth. Jesus answered: I have told you that I am He; if therefore you seek Me, let these others go their way (speaking of His disciples). The lance, the nails, the cross, the thorns, and the death which I have endured, prove that I have wiped away and atoned for the crimes of the unfortunate. Preserve me, O Lord Jesus Christ, from all afflictions of poverty and from the snares of my enemies. May the five wounds <note>The "five wounds" refers to the stigmata, the piercing of Christ's hands, feet, and side during the Crucifixion.</note> of our Lord be to me an everlasting remedy. Jesus is the Way, Jesus is the Life, Jesus is the Truth, Jesus has suffered, Jesus has been crucified; Jesus, Son of the living God, have pity on me. Now, as Jesus was passing, he went through their midst, and no one could place his murderous hand upon Jesus, because His hour had not yet come.*

While these prayers are in some respects quite curious, and may not be unreasonably termed "pure and simple" in their intention, they compare unfavorably with the sublimity and good sense which characterize those of the Roman Liturgy. They are inaccurate in their references and confused in their method, and no person who seriously considers them will support the early date that is claimed for them. Their period is later than the Renaissance, and the ignorance of liturgical formulas which they show—in common with all

<margin>p. 54</margin>

similar forgeries—makes it doubtful whether they are the work of a member of the clergy <note>original: "ecclesiastic"</note> at all. In any case, they are clumsy imitations, the product of an unskillful hand.

## § 5. Summary of Transcendental Magic

This concludes our examination of the rituals of so-called "Transcendental Magic," comprising all those to which any popularity has been given. There are no doubt other treatises which exist only in manuscript and possess no literary history, but they scarcely come within the scope of the present inquiry. I do not believe that anything has remained in obscurity which is more entitled to come into notice than those which have been mentioned briefly in the first chapter. But if I am wrong, it signifies nothing, for there has been more than enough shared already.

The general conclusion which must follow from this examination is naturally a predictable one. As stated at the outset, the rituals with which we have been dealing are not worthy of the name "Transcendental," nor does that title belong to them. We have, on the one hand, a collection of prayers, falsely attributed and modern in their origin, to which no occult significance can be reasonably attached. Devotionally, they are innocent enough; but applied in the manner of a charm, they are merely rituals of empty observance. On the other hand, we have a bare rite for the invocation of Olympic Spirits <note>In the *Arbatel of Magic*, these are seven planetary spirits who govern the world under God's providence.</note>, which, despite the boastful claims of the unknown author, is rather childish than exalted. It may be assumed that no person is naive enough today to take these "angelic offices" literally—and to believe, for example, that by the summoning of Aratron <note>In the *Arbatel*, Aratron is the Olympic Spirit of Saturn, claimed to have the power to turn any object into stone or gold.</note>, the contents of his coal-cellar will be transformed into real treasures. Transcendental Magic is, therefore, exaggerated <note>original: "hyperbolical"</note> in its promises, while these rituals from the...

<summary>Waite concludes his critique of the Enchiridion prayers, dismissing them as post-Renaissance forgeries, and begins a summary dismissing "Transcendental Magic" as either empty devotion or childish superstition.</summary>

Regardless, from a mystical standpoint, these [rituals] would be frivolous if they were meant to be interpreted literally. The student of the occult will find no enlightenment <note>original: "light"</note> from such procedures. However, the subject is at the same time a very strange and imaginative <note>original: "fantastic"</note> branch of research into historical books <note>original: "bibliographical research"</note>. In this study, I have been able to clarify some uncertain points, and in that sense, the work has been worth pursuing. 

The conclusion, then, regarding the Rituals of High Magic <note>original: "Transcendental Magic"</note> is that no such High Rituals actually exist, and that there is no truth to be found within them.

# CHAPTER III

## Composite Rituals

### § 1. The Key of Solomon the King.

By far the most important category of magical rituals is the one that incorporates elements of both "Black" and "White" procedures. For ease of discussion, these are referred to here as "composite" rituals. At the head of this class—and to a great extent the inspiration and source for all others—stands the *Key of Solomon*. It is accompanied by its supplement, the *Lemegeton*, or *Lesser Key*, which is in many ways more significant than the *Key* itself. This work is sometimes attributed to "Solomon Rabbi"; however, the Rabbi and the King are the same figure—at least regarding their role in these writings, which are the fraudulent creations of impostors using a famous name <note>original: "pseudonymous production of impostures." Waite is pointing out that these books were not actually written by the biblical King Solomon, but were written much later by anonymous authors using his name to gain authority.</note>. 

The other rituals discussed in this category include the so-called *Fourth Book* of Cornelius Agrippa and the *Magical Elements* attributed to Peter of Abano. The student of the occult—who is frequently misled by the foolish or the deceitful—has traditionally been taught to view these works as dealing strictly with "White Magic." One goal of this book is to reveal for the first time the mixed nature of these ceremonies, which is apparent even upon a surface-level reading. 

Countless other magical rituals exist only in manuscript <note>original: MS.</note> form and will likely never be published. With few exceptions, these also belong to the composite class. However, aside from the *Lemegeton* (which deserves special attention), these manuscripts have had very little influence. Because they are of no real importance to the history of the occult sciences, they do not require more attention than the brief overview already provided at the end of the first chapter.

<summary>Waite introduces "Composite Rituals," which mix "Black" and "White" magic, focusing on the Key of Solomon and the Lemegeton as the most influential examples. He argues that despite their reputation as "White Magic," these texts actually contain mixed or dark elements and are the works of later impostors rather than King Solomon.</summary>

<keywords>Key of Solomon, Lemegeton, Lesser Key, Solomon Rabbi, Fourth Book of Cornelius Agrippa, Magical Elements, Peter of Abano, composite rituals, White Magic, Black Magic, pseudonymous works</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Key of Solomon, transitioning from its general reputation to a critical examination of its historical manuscripts and origins.</meta>

Mr. Mathers’ presentation of the *Key of Solomon*, <gloss>1</gloss> which is still in print, was compiled by someone without a critical scholarly eye. However, its existence makes it unnecessary to provide a detailed summary of the contents of that strange work here. Where it has been included by later authors of Grimoires <note>A grimoire is a textbook of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects like protective amulets and spells.</note>, it will appear with its Goetic <note>original: "Goëtic." This refers to Goetia, a type of magic involving the invocation of spirits or demons, often considered "low" or "black" magic.</note> variations in the Second Part of this book. Our task here is only to consider how old the work really is and to establish its true character.

The *Key of Solomon* proper is familiar to scholars in Latin, French, Italian, and one or two German manuscripts. The oldest codex <note>An ancient manuscript in book form.</note> used by the English editor (Mathers) is written in contracted Latin <note>Latin written with many shorthand abbreviations, common in medieval and Renaissance manuscripts.</note> and dates to the sixteenth century. It is kept in the British Museum. It is possible that older manuscripts exist in libraries elsewhere in Europe, but those in the National Library and the Arsenal Library in Paris are of a later date. <gloss>2</gloss> The majority of known manuscripts are in French. However, it is often claimed that the work was originally written in Hebrew. There is nothing impossible about this claim, but it suffers from being supported by the very class of people whose opinions are least trustworthy. Even if the claim is true, it is not unlikely that the original might

<page-num>60</page-num>

still exist. The vast Hebrew literature of the Middle Ages has been only partially explored, especially the portions relating to practical magic. However, the dishonest methods typically used in the production of most magical books greatly reduce the likelihood of this being true. A mere statement within a manuscript cannot be taken as evidence. No Hebrew scholar today is aware of such an original. Even three hundred years ago, according to P. Christian <note>A pseudonym for Jean-Baptiste Pitois (1811–1877), a French author and occultist.</note>, the matter was just as uncertain. At the end of the sixteenth century, the learned Jesuit Gretser stated that the Hebrew original was unknown, though a Greek translation supposedly existed in the library of the Duke of Bavaria. I have not managed to track down the current location of this important manuscript, though I cannot claim to have gone to great lengths to find it. 

In this type of literature, whether the "best" version is the one most easily available or not, the accessible versions are usually sufficient for practical purposes. I do not intend to be the definitive historian of magic—whether Black or White—or the person who categorizes all its manuscripts. In my view, all occultism is part of a "path of descent" <note>Waite believed that seeking magical power was a spiritual decline compared to seeking mystical union with the divine.</note>, and that which is explicitly "Black Magic" may, in one sense, be the least harmful, because we know exactly what we are dealing with. I will finish this point by noting that in the eighteenth century, the Abbé d’Artigny mentioned several examples of the *Key of Solomon* in Latin, as well as a printed edition from 1655. This edition seems unknown not only to Mr. Mathers but to all modern bibliographers. <gloss>1</gloss>

<page-num>61</page-num>

Leaving the question of the original language open, it is clear that there is no reason to believe the *Key of Solomon* in its current form is older than the fourteenth or fifteenth century. This was a time when Hebrew literature was expanding rapidly. <gloss>1</gloss> Even if it were first written in Latin, it is saturated with late Jewish ideas. Furthermore, the corrupted state of the Hebrew used in the conjurations and talismans—which is equally bad in every existing copy—could scarcely 

<summary>Waite examines the historical origins and manuscript evidence of the Key of Solomon, concluding that it likely dates to the 14th or 15th century despite claims of ancient Hebrew origins.</summary>
<keywords>Key of Solomon, S.L. MacGregor Mathers, manuscripts, Hebrew magic, Grimoires, Goetia, occult history, Jesuit Gretser</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Key of Solomon, focusing on its likely date of origin, the false attribution to King Solomon, and the flaws found in the modern English edition edited by S. L. MacGregor Mathers.</meta>

could have been reached in less than two centuries of careless and ignorant copying. We can therefore estimate the date of its creation—or its translation—to be around the period previously mentioned.

Attributing the work to King Solomon is a predictable choice. It would naturally occur to any compiler with Kabbalistic <note>Kabbalah: a form of Jewish mysticism.</note> interests and a knowledge of Jewish traditions dating back to the time of the Talmud <note>The primary source of Jewish religious law and theology.</note>. Furthermore, this is entirely consistent with this type of literature, which is famous for falsely attributing works to historical figures.

<page-num>p. 62</page-num>

It is honestly impossible to understand why any well-educated person today would take this claim of authorship seriously. Regarding the English editor <note>Referring to S. L. MacGregor Mathers, who published an edition of the Key of Solomon in 1888.</note>, those concerned might explain his belief in one of two ways: either he was inclined to believe statements based on "occult tradition" because he already believed in the reality of occult science; or, he possessed some secret knowledge passed down through "traditions of initiation." 

The second explanation is unlikely. It insults the intelligence of the biblical King of Israel to suggest he actually wrote the *Clavicle* <note>original: "Clavicle." From the Latin "Clavicula," meaning "Little Key."</note>. However, I suspect that certain "storehouses" of occult tradition might be willing to validate this false attribution or even create fake records to support it. In short, it is perfectly in character for those involved in occult initiation to pass down a false tradition or simply invent one.

So much for the age of the work and the grand deception regarding its author. Now, we must discuss its actual character. *The Key of Solomon* cannot be accurately judged by its English version. The translator, who strangely viewed the book as a highly respectable record of "lawful" magic, removed as many of the Goetic <note>original: "Goëtic." This refers to Goetia, a type of magic involving the invocation of spirits or demons, often considered "low" or "black" magic.</note> sections as possible. He claimed they were later additions, but this choice was entirely arbitrary. 

However, he still kept what is usually condemned as a hallmark of Black Magic: the ritual is filled with animal sacrifices. While Mr. Mathers condemns this practice, he did not find a way to remove it from his text. His version also includes various instructions for acts of hatred and destruction—tasks that clearly show an evil intent or a direct connection to Black Magic. The chapter explaining

<page-num>p. 63</page-num>

how to achieve these dark goals was omitted from his edition, but it appears in five of the seven manuscripts <note>original: "codices."</note> he used as his sources. Furthermore, even when the goal is not evil, it is often trivial, exaggerated, or utterly foolish. 

The experiments are:
*   (**a.**) **Trivial**, such as those for finding stolen goods, which puts the book on the same level as cheap fortune-telling pamphlets sold by street peddlers.
*   (**b.**) **Exaggerated and fantastical**, such as the experiment for becoming invisible, or the instructions for making "Magic Garters" and a "Magic Staff."
*   (**c.**) **Foolish**, such as the chapters on how to prevent a hunter from killing any game.

Perhaps, however, these categories overlap. M. Papus <note>Gérard Encausse (1865–1916), a prominent French occultist who wrote under the pseudonym Papus.</note>, a leading voice for French occultists, makes a distinction between the *Keys of Solomon* and the—

<summary>This page critiques the historical legitimacy of the Key of Solomon, arguing its attribution to the biblical king is a fabrication of later occultists. Waite also criticizes S. L. MacGregor Mathers for sanitizing the text's "darker" magical elements in his English translation.</summary>
<keywords>Key of Solomon, S. L. MacGregor Mathers, Papus, Goetic magic, Black Magic, Kabbalah, Talmud, occult tradition, animal sacrifice, pseudepigrapha</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's critique of the Key of Solomon, arguing that its elaborate rituals are disproportionate to its petty and often unethical goals. It begins mid-sentence, continuing a thought from the previous page regarding the nature of such occult works.</meta>

...the frauds of street peddling <note>original: "colportage," the distribution of books by traveling salesmen</note>; but in what way, one might ask, are these rituals superior to the cheap pamphlets <note>original: "chapbooks"</note> sold by those same peddlers?

The highest goal of the *Keys of Solomon* <note>original: "Clavicles," from the Latin "Clavicula," meaning "Little Key"</note> is identical to that of the *Grimoires* <note>textbooks of magic</note>—to become the master of a treasure guarded by spirits. It should also be noted that rituals designed to interfere with the free will of another person—such as seeking someone's favor or love—are essentially evil acts.

I have now listed all the rituals found in this "fountainhead and storehouse of Kabbalistic Magic." It is for such worthless <note>original: "trumpery"</note> purposes that the Magician <note>original: "Magus"</note> is told to undertake his exhausting preparations. For these petty goals, he is directed to use the powers believed to reside in Divine Names, recite long pages of arrogant prayers, and perform "stronger and more powerful" conjurations. However much a critic's judgment might be softened by the mercy of a researcher toward a relic of occult science that has been unfairly respected—that is, respected as anything other than a literary curiosity—one must conclude that the

<margin>p. 64</margin>
*Key of Solomon* is a bizarre mix of the arrogant and the absurd. It is, in fact, the old story of the mountain in labor that gives birth to a mouse <note>A reference to an Aesop’s fable; it describes a situation where a great deal of effort results in something insignificant.</note>; but in this case, the labor is so great that the mouse is born dead.

...of Solomon under another title, but it <gloss>p. 61</gloss> is probably the Latin version of the *Sepher Rasiel* <note>original: "Book of Raziel," a 13th-century Kabbalistic grimoire</note>. Whether any of these works were actually printed is not explicitly clear, and the Abbot <note>original: "Abbé"</note>, much like Christian himself, knew very little about his subject. It is he, however, who provides the information concerning Gretser <note>Jacob Gretser (1562–1625), a Jesuit scholar known for his massive volumes of theological works</note>, though the account reads quite differently: "Perhaps this *Book of Pentacles* <note>original: "Liber Pentaculorum"</note> is the same as the treatise *On Necromancy, addressed to his Son Rehoboam* <note>original: "De Necromantia ad Filium Roboam"</note>, which Father Gretser, a learned German Jesuit, had seen written in Greek in the library of the Duke of Bavaria." The collected works of Gretser fill seventeen large folio volumes, and there are limits to how much one can research. However, it can be confirmed that the *Catalogue of Manuscript Books of the Royal Bavarian Library* <note>original: "Catalogus Codicum Manuscriptorum Bibliothecæ Regiæ Bavariæ"</note>, written by the library's superintendent Ignatius Hardt (5 volumes, Munich, 1806), does not mention such a work. The reference to the printed edition of the *Key* <note>original: "Clavicle"</note> occurs on pages 36 and 37 of D'Artigny's article; he describes it as a 125-page quarto volume, printed without the name of a location or publisher. The frontispiece (or perhaps the title page) reads "Key of Solomon," featuring a cross within a circle, and below this symbol is the date 1655. The entire work is divided into twelve paragraphs. D'Artigny transcribes part of number nine, an "Exorcism of the Spirits of the Air," which certainly does not appear in any known edition of the *Great Key* <note>original: "Grand Clavicle"</note> and seems to be adapted from the *Lemegeton*.

61:1 A bibliography by Papus <note>Gerard Encausse (1865–1916), a famous French occultist</note> attached to his *Methodical Summary of the Kabbalah* lists forty-seven separate Kabbalistic treatises that appeared in Hebrew between the mid-thirteenth and late-sixteenth centuries. These are only the most famous ones; the broader Kabbalistic literature was much larger. I mention this for the benefit of the student of the occult, as it may be a useful resource. The *Great Rabbinical Library* <note>original: "Magna Bibliotheca Rabbinica"</note> by Bartolocci—which, with its supplement by another author, spans five large folio volumes—is the definitive and monumental catalogue of Jewish literature.

### § 2. The Lesser Key of Solomon

The *Lemegeton*, or *Lesser Key of Solomon, Rabbi and King*, is a work with much higher claims <note>original: "exalted pretensions"</note>. It enlists entire hierarchies of spirits and claims to summon them by the billions. There is no need for serious debate regarding its age; while it claims to be translated from Hebrew, the earliest complete versions are in seventeenth-century French, and no one has ever seen the original Hebrew manuscript. It must have existed in a much earlier form, however. It is frequently referenced by demonologists like Wierus <note>Johann Weyer (1515–1588), a physician and occultist who studied under Agrippa</note> under the title of the sorcerer's *Book of Spirits* <note>original: "Liber Spirituum"</note>. It was from this source that the skeptical scholar Weyer derived his *False Monarchy of Demons* <note>original: "Pseudomonarchia Dæmonum"</note>, though with significant differences from the surviving copies.

The *Lemegeton* is divided into four parts, which allow the practitioner to control the duties of all spirits according to their will, provided the usual ritual requirements are met. <gloss>1</gloss> Except for the first part, which provided material for Wierus, this strange and historically significant work has never been officially printed. However, it has been secretly mined for content by most creators of magical rituals and grimoires. As I have mentioned, it deals with the summoning of all classes of spirits—evil, indifferent, and good. Its opening rituals involve Lucifer, Bel, Astaroth, and the entire army of Hell <note>original: "cohort of Infernus"</note>. It is titled *Goëtia* <note>a term derived from the Greek word for "sorcery," referring to the practice of invoking spirits or demons</note>, a name that explains its purpose, and it contains the forms of conjuration...

<summary>Waite critiques various bibliographic accounts of the Key of Solomon and introduces the Lemegeton (Lesser Key of Solomon), identifying it as a major source for later demonologists and grimoires.</summary>
<keywords>Lemegeton, Lesser Key of Solomon, Goetia, Johann Weyer (Wierus), Sepher Rasiel, Kabbalah, magical manuscripts, 17th-century grimoires</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the *Lemegeton*, or the *Little Key of Solomon*, detailing its four distinct books and their contents. It transitions from discussing the demonic hierarchy to the differences between the *Little Key* and other Solomonic texts regarding talismans and spiritual contracts.</meta>

...listing seventy-two primary demons <note>original: "devils"</note> and their subordinates, with an account of their powers and roles. The second part, or *Theurgia Goëtia* <note>original: "Theurgia Goëtia," meaning "Divine Witchcraft"</note>, deals with the spirits of the cardinal points and those beneath them. These spirits are of a mixed nature—some good and some evil. The third book is called the *Pauline Art*—a name for which I cannot account—and it concerns the Angels of the Hours of the Day and Night and of the Zodiacal Signs. The fourth part, or *Almadel*, lists four other choirs of spirits in a rather unclear manner. 

There is one significant point about the entire work: the powers associated with the offices of the Infernal Spirits are described in great detail, but the Ritual is almost silent regarding the special benefits one might expect from communicating with higher forms of intelligence. Therefore, it is obvious which spirits a magician would turn to if he had a specific goal in mind. It is, indeed, quite likely that the first or "Goetic" <note>original: "Goëtic," relating to the practice of low magic or witchcraft</note> portion constitutes the true *Lemegeton*, and that the other sections (which were apparently unknown to Weyer <note>original: "Wierus," referring to Johann Weyer (1515–1588), a physician and occultist who wrote against the persecution of witches</note>) are later additions. In either case, this categorization is not only explicitly connected with Black Magic—or rather, exclusively devoted to it—but it undoubtedly shares with the so-called *Greater Key* the dark reputation <note>original: "forbidding honour"</note> of being the primary inspiration for all later handbooks of demonic ritual. Since it lacks any doctrinal sections, there is nothing here worth quoting; however, as no book of magic <term>grimoire</term> can claim to be complete without it, all of its hierarchical charts <note>original: "tabulations"</note> and its summoning processes will be provided in the Second Part of this work.

In conclusion, I should state that the *Little Key* is sometimes attributed to King Solomon and sometimes to a Rabbi of that same name. It should be distinguished, however, from the *Key of Rabbi Solomon* proper, which is more specifically concerned with the creation, consecration, and use of planetary talismans. That work claims to deal only with "good spirits" <note>original: "Genies"</note>, yet it still includes blood sacrifice, and it displays the usual anxiety regarding the form and appearance in which the spirit might manifest. It is, however, specifically stated that no seal, pentacle, or symbol <note>original: "character"</note> shall be used to harm another person, especially a student of this art. Ultimately, this practice does not involve any contract <note>original: "compact"</note>, whether "implied or stated." I suspect that this specific text was produced later than others in this collection of writings.

### § 3. The Pauline Art

<summary>This page outlines the structure of the Lemegeton, including its focus on demonic hierarchies and its distinction from the Key of Rabbi Solomon, which focuses on planetary talismans.</summary>
<keywords>Lemegeton, Little Key of Solomon, Theurgia Goëtia, Pauline Art, Almadel, Black Magic, Grimoire, Johann Weyer, planetary talismans, goeteia</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the *Lemegeton* (the *Little Key of Solomon*), shifting focus from the demonic hierarchies to the third book, known as *The Pauline Art*, which details the celestial hierarchies of the day and night.</meta>

If the suggestion from the last section is accepted as a working hypothesis regarding the *Lemegeton*, and if its third and fourth books are therefore set apart as later additions <note>original: "accretions"</note>, there is perhaps some plausible reason for placing *The Pauline Art* among composite rituals <note>A "composite ritual" is a text compiled from various different sources and traditions over time</note>. It is very difficult to speak with certainty, as the work offers a ritual for the invocation of the Angels of Day and Night, the Angels of the Twelve Signs, and those of the Zodiacal degrees, who are called the "Angels of Men." However, there is no distinct account of either their duties or qualities, except in the case of the last group. For them, it appears that their intervention can be sought only for purposes that are in harmony with the laws of God and the general good of mankind. It is a dry document largely consisting of lists of names; however, because it can be summarized briefly and because it holds a certain status in occult literature, I shall present it in the following form.

### HOURS OF THE DAY

The Ruler of the First Hour is **SAMAEL**, who has 444 serving spirits under eight leaders, namely: *Arniniel, Charpon, Daresiel, Monasiel, Brumiel, Nestoriel, Charmeas, Meresijn*.

The Ruler of the Second Hour is **ANAEL**, who has under his control ten chief and one hundred lesser lords. These in their turn command serving spirits. The chiefs include *Monarchas, Archiel* and *Chardiel*; the subordinates <note>original: "subalterns"</note> include *Orphiel, Carsiol, Elmoym, Quosiel, Ermosiel, Graniel*.

The Ruler of the Third Hour is **VEGUANIEL**, under whom are twenty chief officers, two hundred lesser officers, and a multitude <note>original: "cloud"</note> of serving spirits. The chief officers include *Murriel, Leosiel, Drelmeth, Sardiniel*; among the lesser officers are *Parmiel, Comadiel, Glmarij, Hantiel, Serviel, Furiel*.

The Ruler of the Fourth Hour is **VACHMIEL**, who has ten chief and one hundred inferior officers, who govern many serving spirits. The chiefs include *Ammiel, Larmiel, Marfiel, Ormijel, Sardiel*, and the inferiors include *Emarfiel, Permiel, Quoriel, Sturbiel, Daniel*.

The Ruler of the Fifth Hour is **SAZQUIEL**, who has ten chief and one hundred lesser officers, each with their own attendants. The chiefs include *Damiel, Armarele, Maroch, Serapiel, Fustiel*, and the inferiors include *Jamersil, Futiniel, Ramisiel, Amisiel, Pubrisiel*.

The Ruler of the Sixth Hour is **SAMIL**, who has ten chief and one hundred lesser officers, with a vast assembly of serving spirits. Among the chiefs are *Araebel, Charuch, Medussusiel, Nathaniel, Perniel*, and among the subordinates are *Jamiel, Joustriel, Sameon, Farsiel, Zamion*.

The Ruler of the Seventh Hour is **BARGINIEL**, also having ten officers and one hundred subordinates. Among the chiefs are *Abrasiel, Farmos, Nosteri, Mamiel, Sagiel*, and the inferior examples include *Harmiel, Nastoro, Varmaj, Tusmas, Crociel*.

<summary>This page introduces "The Pauline Art," a section of the Lemegeton, and begins a catalog of the angels governing the specific hours of the day and their subordinate spirits.</summary>

The Ruler of the Eighth Hour is **OSGAEBIAL**, who has many chief and subordinate officers and a great cloud of serving spirits. Among the chiefs are *Sarfiel, Amatino, Choriel, Mosiel, Lanthros,* and among the subordinates <note>original: "subalterns"</note> are *Demaros, Osmajloel, Januviel, Vernael, Thribiel.*

The Ruler of the Ninth Hour is **VADRIEL**, who has ten chief and one hundred lesser officers and serving spirits as numerous as the sands of the sea. The greater include *Astroniel, Charms, Pamerif, Damiel, Madriel;* among the lesser are *Kremos, Meros, Brasiel, Nafarin, Zoigmiel.*

The Ruler of the Tenth Hour is **ORIEL**, who has ten cohorts of officers presiding over many serving spirits. These orders include *Armesi, Darbiel, Panales, Mesriel, Chorob, Lemure, Ormas, Charnij, Zazior, Namon*—with each representing one of the angelic choirs.

The Ruler of the Eleventh Hour is **BARIEL**, whose army is similarly divided. As typical examples of the ten orders, there are *Almariz, Pralimiel, Chardros, Furmiel, Lamie, Menafiel, Demasar, Ormary, Hevas, Zemoel.*

The Ruler of the Twelfth Hour is **BERATIEL**, whose officers are divided into twelve orders, some greater and some lesser. The examples of each are: in the first class, *Cameron, Altrofiel, Penatiel, Demarae, Famaris;* and in the second, *Pharniel, Nerostiel, Emarson, Voizia, Sameron.*

## HOURS OF THE NIGHT

The activities that wander in the darkness <note>original: "Negotia perambulantia in tenebris"</note> are more numerous than those of the daylight. They are divided under their respective Supreme Rulers into six chief and six secondary orders, and the serving spirits are proportionately great in number.

The First Hour. Supreme Ruler—**GAMIEL**. The chief officers include *Moriel, Garthiel, Sepharon, Mousien, Arathiel,*

<page-num>69</page-num>

*Cosel.* Lesser officers: *Zanias, Noxin, Tempast, Farion, Glaras, Belsal.*

The Second Hour. Supreme Ruler—**FARRIS**. The chief officers include *Almodar, Tamarid, Nedros, Ormisiel, Chabril, Praxil.* Lesser officers: *Permaz, Umeroz, Emaril, Fromezin, Ramasiel, Granozin.*

The Third Hour. Supreme Ruler—**SARQUAMICH**. The chief officers include *Meriarijm, Crusiel, Penarys, Amriel, Dencinoz, Nestozoz.* Lesser officers: *Evanel, Sarmezin, Haglon, Uvabriel, Thymitzod, Fromzon.*

The Eleventh Hour: **DARDARIEL**. The great officers include *Cardiel, Permon, Armiel, Hastoriel, Casmiros, Dumariel,* and *Tumoriel*. The lesser officers are: *Hermas, Druchas, Charman, Elamiz, Ialcoajul, Lamersij,* and *Hamarytzod*.

The Twelfth Hour: **SARINDIEL**. The chief officers include *Adoniel, Darmosiel,* and *Ambriel*; the lesser officers include *Nefrias, Irmanotzod,* and *Melanos*. There are others mentioned, but the list is confused in my source manuscript, and these will serve for all purposes of naming.

The Angels of the Twelve Signs—classing the latter according to the elementary triplicities <note>original: "elementary triplicities"; refers to the astrological system of grouping three zodiac signs together by their shared element: fire, air, water, or earth.</note>—are:

*   **Fiery Triplicity:** Aries—Angel = *Aiel*; Leo—Angel = *Ol*; Sagittarius—Angel = *Sizajasel*.
*   **Airy Triplicity:** Gemini—Angel = *Giel*; Libra—Angel = *Jael*; Aquarius—Angel = *Ausiul*.
*   **Watery Triplicity:** Cancer—Angel = *Cael*; Scorpio—Angel = *Sosol*; Pisces—Angel = *Pasiel*.
*   **Earthy Triplicity:** Taurus—Angel = *Tual*; Virgo—Angel = *Voil*; Capricorn—Angel = *Casujoiah*.

However, the supreme zodiacal leadership is assigned to the archangels as follows: for the Fiery Triplicity, Michael; for the Airy Triplicity, Raphael; for the Watery Triplicity...

Gabriel; for the Earthy Triplicity <note>original: "Earthy Triplicity"; in astrology, a "triplicity" is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (Earth, Air, Fire, or Water).</note>, Uriel. This follows all traditional precedents. It is impossible to list here all the so-called angels or spirits <note>original: "genii"</note> of humanity who govern the 360 degrees of the zodiac. They are represented by the ruling archangels—whose direct invocation does not seem to be possible—and by the spirits who serve them. For example: *Asajel, Sochiel,* and *Cassiel* of the Earthy Triplicity; *Samael, Madiel,* and *Mael* of the Watery Triplicity; and so forth. These lesser spirits will permit citation <note>original: "suffer citation"; meaning they allow themselves to be summoned or called forth.</note>, but the success of the Pauline experiments in general will depend upon:

(a.) The construction of the seals assigned to the twenty-four ruling hours of the day and night; these seals must show the correct astrological signs related to the day and hour of each intelligence;
(b.) The use of the proper planetary perfumes <note>Incense blends specifically associated with the seven classical planets.</note> during the invocation;
(c.) The astrological seals related to the chief intelligences of the Twelve Signs, under the leadership of the four great archangels;
(d.) A consecrated crystal into which the zodiacal spirits must be called by the practitioner; and
(e.) The ceremonial use of the invoking formulas, examples of which follow below.

### HOURS OF THE DAY AND NIGHT

->*Invocation of a Supreme Ruler*<-

> *O mighty and powerful Prince Samael, who are the ruler and governor of the first hour of the day by the decree of the Most High God, King of Glory; I, the servant of the Most High, desire and entreat you by three great and powerful names of God—ADONAI <note>Hebrew: "My Lord"</note>, AGLAON <note>likely a variation of AGLA, a Hebrew acronym meaning "You, O Lord, are mighty forever"</note>, TETRAGRAMMATON <note>Greek: "the four-lettered name," referring to the sacred Hebrew name of God, YHWH</note>—and by the power and virtue within them, to assist me in my affairs. By your power and authority, I ask you to send to me—causing them to appear before me—all or any of the angels whom I shall call by name, who live under your government. I further entreat and require that they shall help in all matters which match their office, just as I desire, and that they shall act for me as a servant of the Most High. Amen.*

### THE INVOCATION OF A SPIRIT <note>original: "Genius"</note> IN THE CRYSTAL

The ceremony is performed while facing toward the direction of heaven where the zodiac sign is currently situated.

<page-num>72</page-num>

->*PRAYER*<-

> *O you great and blessed angel [Name], my guardian angel, graciously descend from your holy dwellings, bringing your holy influence and presence into this crystal stone, so that I may behold your glory and enjoy your company and aid, both now and forever. O you who are higher than the fourth heaven, you who know the secrets of ELANEL <note>A divine name or angelic power</note>, you who ride...*

> *...on the wings of the wind and are endowed with motion beyond the earthly sphere <note>original: "superlunary motion"; motion occurring above the moon, traditionally considered the realm of perfection and celestial spirits</note>; descend and be present, I pray you. Amen.*

By properly observing the rules laid down in the *Pauline Art* regarding the Angels of Humanity, and assuming that the practitioner knows the exact minute of their own birth, it is said that the operator may master all arts and sciences, and even attain the sum of all wisdom. In short, it is the "royal road" to knowledge, in both theory and practice.

This is the nature of Astronomical Magic according to the *Lemegeton* <note>original: "Lemegeton"; also known as the *Lesser Key of Solomon*, a famous 17th-century grimoire</note> and its related texts. It does not differ essentially from the system of the *Enochian Tablets* followed by John Dee and his "skryer" <note>a medium who looks into a crystal ball or mirror to see spirits</note> Edward Kelley, as found in the book *A Faithful Relation* <note>referring to Meric Casaubon’s 1659 publication of Dee’s private journals, *A True and Faithful Relation of What Passed Between Dr. John Dee and Some Spirits*</note>. The same observation applies to the content of the next section, which follows here without further introduction.

### § 4. The Almadel

I shall explain first that this system of invocation takes its title from the traditional talisman on which the process depends.

<margin>p. 73</margin>

It is made of white wax and is inscribed as shown below.

<image-desc>A square talismanic diagram known as the Almadel. The outermost border is a square with five-pointed stars (pentagrams) in each of the four corners. At the top center of the outer border is the letter "N". Inside the top border, the words "Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter name of God</note>, Shadai <note>Almighty</note>, Jah <note>God</note>" are written. The left border contains the names "Adonai <note>Lord</note>, Helomi, Pine", written vertically. The right border contains "Jod, Hod, Agla <note>A Hebrew acronym meaning 'You are mighty forever, O Lord'</note>", written vertically. The bottom border contains "Helion, Heloi, Heli <note>Divine epithets</note>", written upside down. Within this outer frame is a second square border, each side of which is inscribed with the word "ANABONA" <note>A magical word of power</note>. In the center of the talisman is a hexagram (six-pointed star) composed of two overlapping triangles. At the very center is a smaller triangle pointing upwards. Small circles are positioned at the points where the hexagram meets the inner square border.</image-desc>

This figure is used for the four chief angels whose government extends over the four quarters <note>original: "altitudes"</note> of the world—namely East, West, North, and South. It also serves for their ministers and subordinates. However, because a different color is assigned to the choir of each quarter, and because this color must be used to dye the white wax, it follows that those who wish to work

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the "Pauline Art" and introduces the "Almadel," the fourth book of the Lemegeton, which details the construction of a wax talisman used to contact angels of the four cardinal directions.</summary>
<keywords>Almadel, Lemegeton, John Dee, Edward Kelley, Pauline Art, talismans, wax magic, ritual invocation, planetary magic</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Fourth Part of the Lemegeton, the "Almadel of Solomon," focusing on the construction of the wax talismans and the appearance of the spirits associated with the four "Altitudes" or celestial levels.</meta>

...with the entire hierarchy must compose four *Almadels*. Furthermore, their work does not end there, for each talisman must have four candles made from the same wax and set aside specifically for it. Finally, there must be provided a seal of gold or silver, on which must be engraved the names HELION, HELLUJON, ADONAI <note>original: "HELION, HELLUJON, ADONAI"; These are divine names used in ceremonial magic. Adonai is Hebrew for "Lord," while Helion and Hellujon are likely corrupted forms of Greek or Hebrew titles for God</note>. It serves for all the choirs. The candles are provided

<page-num>74</page-num>

with wax feet, and the Almadel <note>A ritual wax talisman used for communicating with angels</note> rests on them, being a square figure measuring six inches on all sides. The color for the Eastern choir is lily white; the others are red, green, and apparently a dark green which approaches black, but only the first choir is assigned to its distinct quarter of the heavens. The names of the Intelligences <note>spiritual beings or angels who govern specific spheres of existence</note> are as follows:

FIRST ALTITUDE: *Alimiel, Gabriel, Borachiel, Lebes, Hellison*. They make all things in creation fruitful.

SECOND ALTITUDE: *Alphariza, Genon, Geron, Armon, Gereinon*. They have power over goods and riches, and can make any person rich or poor. They also cause decrease and barrenness.

THIRD ALTITUDE: *Eliphamasai, Gelomiros, Gedobonai, Saranana, Elomnia*. Their duties are not stated.

FOURTH ALTITUDE: *Barchiel, Gediel, Gebiel, Deliel, Captiel*. In this case also, their duties are not stated.

When an Angel of the First Altitude responds to an invocation, he carries a banner emblazoned with a white cross, his body is clothed in a luminous cloud, and he is crowned with roses. He is fair of face, and upon his departure, he leaves a sweet perfume. An Angel of the Second Altitude has the form of a young child in rose-colored satin, with a crown of red gillyflowers <note>original: "gillyflowers"; an archaic name for carnations or clove pinks</note> on his head. His face looks toward heaven and shines with the light of the sun. The perfume which he leaves lingers for a long time. An Angel of the Third Altitude appears in the form of a child or a small woman in green and silver colors, wearing a crown of bay leaves <note>original: "bay leaves"; laurel leaves, traditionally associated with honor and victory</note> adorned with white flowers. This apparition also leaves a sweet perfume. An Angel of the Fourth Altitude appears in the form of a boy or a small man, in black or olive garments, crowned with ivy berries. His perfume differs from the rest, but is no

<page-num>75</page-num>

less sweet. All the manifestations are beautiful, and, as it is said that they must not be called for anything against God and His laws, it is only the suspicious company in which the *Almadel* appears that renders its operations doubtful—I mean, in respect to their intention. <note>Waite is suggesting that while these angels seem holy, the fact that this ritual is found in grimoires alongside darker magic makes their true nature or the purpose of the ritual questionable.</note>

<summary>This page details the physical construction of the Almadel wax talisman and lists the names, powers, and visual appearances of the angels belonging to the four Altitudes.</summary>
<keywords>Almadel, Altitudes, Intelligences, Alimiel, Gabriel, talisman, wax candles, ceremonial appearance, Solomonic magic</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific ritual instructions for using the Almadel, the wax talisman described on the previous page, detailing the physical setup of the oratory and providing the text of the primary invocation.</meta>

The invocation takes place on a Sunday, during the day and hour of the sun. The candles are placed in their holders; the *Almadel* rests—as we have seen—on the projecting feet of the candles, so that there is a clear space beneath it. There is a hole in each corner, and when the spirit <note>original: "intelligence"; in this context, it refers to a sentient spiritual being or angel</note> is about to appear, an earthen vessel containing hot ashes and three grains of powdered mastic <note>A resin obtained from the mastic tree, often used in incense</note> is placed under the talisman. The smoke produced passes through the holes, and it is said that when the Angel smells it, he begins to speak.

I should explain, however, that the furniture of the oratory <note>A private room or chapel set aside for prayer and ritual</note> and the vestments worn by the operator must be the same color as the *Almadel*. The candles are lit at the beginning, the golden seal is placed in the middle of the talisman, and the practitioner <note>original: "Invocant"</note> kneels down, holding the "Prayer at Calling," which must be written on virgin parchment <note>Parchment made from animal skin that has never been used for any other purpose</note>. The apparition manifests over the *Almadel*, appearing at first as a mist. This clears gradually, and when the incense rises, the face of the Angel is turned toward the operator. The Angel then asks in a low voice why the Princes of that particular Choir or Altitude <note>The specific rank or level of the angelic hierarchy</note> have been called. The answer is: "I desire that all my requests may be granted and that which I pray for accomplished, for it appears and is declared by your office that this is possible for you, if it please God." The specific requests are then listed without fear and without disrespect.

On the surface of the text, it seems as if no preliminary invocation is needed for this work, but that is due to the confusion of the Ritual, which places the prayer at the end in a separate section.

### THE PRAYER AT CALLING

> You great, mighty, and blessed angel of God, [Name], who rule as the chief and first governing Angel in the first choir or altitude in the East, under the great Prince of the East whom you obey, and who is also set over you as King by the Divine Power of God—ADONAI, HELOMI, PINE <note>original: "ADONAI, HELOMI, PINE"; Adonai is Hebrew for "Lord." Helomi and Pine are "barbarous names" or corrupted terms used in magic for their perceived resonance rather than literal meaning</note>, Who is the disposer of all things in heaven, earth, and hell: I, the servant of that God—ADONAI, HELOMI, PINE—Whom you obey, do invoke, conjure, and entreat you, [Name], to appear immediately. By the virtue and power of the same God, I command you from your order or place of abode to come to me and show yourself plainly here before me in your own proper shape and glory, speaking in a voice that my understanding can perceive. O you mighty and blessed Angel [Name], I, the servant of the same, your God, entreat and humbly beseech you to come and show me all things that I shall desire of you, subject to the power of your office and the good pleasure of the Lord our God. By the three names of the true God—ADONAI, HELOMI, PINE—and by the name ANA BONA, I beseech and compel you to appear visibly in your proper shape right now, speaking audibly in my ears, so that I may have your blessed, angelic, and glorious assistance, familiar friendship, constant society, communication, and instruction, now and at all times, regarding this and all truths which Almighty God, King of Kings, Giver of all good gifts, shall be graciously pleased to bestow on me. Therefore, O you blessed Angel [Name], be friendly to me, and do for me as God has...

<keywords>Almadel, invocation ritual, mastic incense, oratory, virgin parchment, Adonai, Angelic Choir, ceremonial magic</keywords>

*...empowered you, whereby I now command you to appear in power and presence, so that I may sing with His Holy Angels: O MAPPA LAMAN, HALLELUJAH. AMEN.*

*When he appears,* says the Ritual, *give him a kind reception; ask for what is lawful, possible, and appropriate for his office <note>A spirit's "office" refers to their specific duties or area of professional influence</note>; and you shall obtain it. If, however, he should fail to appear, the four candles must be marked with the gold or silver seal, which apparently will ensure obedience.*

There are, however, certain planetary considerations connected with this process on which its success depends. The first two Angels of the First Altitude <note>The specific rank or level of the angelic hierarchy</note> can only be called on the first and second Sundays in March; the third and fourth on the Sundays in April; the fifth after the sun has entered Gemini in May. And so on for the rest in succession.

### § 5. The Fourth Book of Cornelius Agrippa

It is now almost common knowledge that a *Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy* is attributed to Cornelius Agrippa, and that it is rejected as a forgery <note>original: "spurious"</note>. The authenticity of the famous first three books has never been questioned and is indeed beyond doubt; the fourth is perhaps less interesting for its actual contents than for the question of who wrote it. At the same time, it is a much more skillful performance than the usual run of magical frauds; it connects with and grows out of the genuine work in a very curious way. Considering the specific magical tone of the genuine books, there might seem to be no inherent reason why Agrippa could not have written it. The difficulties regarding it can be reduced to three main points. The first is timing; it appeared after the death of the restless, speculating philosopher of Nettersheim <note>Nettersheim was the birthplace of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous theologian and occultist</note>. Now, a posthumous publication is not necessarily suspicious unless it is a treatise on Magic; however, a magical treatise from that period that did not appear during the author's lifetime is highly suspect because of the dishonest company to which such books belong. <meta>catchword: The</meta>

The second difficulty is internal, and I do not think it can be ignored. To a large extent, it is a rehash <note>original: "rechauffé"; literally "reheated," meaning a reorganization of previously published material</note> of various parts of the three undisputed books; even in Agrippa’s day, it is unlikely that any author would have copied his own work so extensively. The third difficulty is that it was rejected as a forgery by Wierus <note>Johann Weyer (1515–1588), also known as Wierus, was a physician and occultist who studied under Agrippa and became a famous critic of witch-hunting</note>, the pupil of Agrippa, who must have had a good opportunity to know the truth. Its rejection by later writers simply follows the lead of Wierus and is therefore of no great significance on its own. The strength of the case against it lies mainly in this third difficulty, though other objections are possible. Although it is not the work of Agrippa, it was clearly produced very close to his time.

The book itself, which is written quite informally, is divided into several sections. First, there is an elaborate treatise on the method of extracting the names of the good and evil spirits associated with the seven planets. This is a further development of a subject treated at some length in the third book of *Occult Philosophy*. The method is of no importance to our current inquiry, but those who have tried to untangle it admit they have been baffled.

<summary>This page concludes the Almadel ritual's timing and moves into a scholarly critique of the "Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy," arguing it is a skillful forgery not written by Cornelius Agrippa.</summary>
<keywords>Almadel, Cornelius Agrippa, Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy, Nettersheim, Wierus, planetary spirits, magical authenticity, occult history</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy" attributed to Cornelius Agrippa, specifically focusing on its sections concerning planetary spirits, sigils, and the ritualistic summoning of both good and evil entities.</meta>

I ask permission to assure my readers that I have not tried to match their enthusiasm by seeking to follow in their footsteps; as far as I am concerned, the method therefore remains hidden within its own mystery. It is possible that Agrippa and his successor were simply tricking their readers and did not actually reveal the secret. The essay on Names is followed by one on Characters, and because it depends on the first one, this is also not easily understood. I have left it to those who are interested in such things. Afterward, there is a formal table listing all the known shapes common to the spirits of the planets, followed by a discussion on Pentacles and Sigils <note>Pentacles and Sigils: Talismans or symbolic diagrams used in magic to represent and command specific spirits or energies.</note>, and another on the consecration of tools used in magical ceremonies—as well as fire, water, and so on. The work ends with methods for

<page-num>79</page-num>

the summoning of good and evil spirits and a short procedure for <term>Necromancy</term> <note>Necromancy: Historically, the practice of communicating with the dead; in this context, it refers to a specific magical ritual for that purpose.</note>. Since I will have frequent occasion to quote it in the Second Part of this book, the current analysis will focus on establishing its connection with diabolism <note>The worship of or dealings with the devil.</note> and the types of manifestations that are supposedly produced by its processes.

Like the *Lemegeton* <note>original: "Lemegeton" — Also known as the Lesser Key of Solomon, a famous 17th-century grimoire.</note>, it provides specific directions for communicating with evil spirits. There is no question whatsoever regarding the "lawful" nature of the experiment—by which I mean that no doubt on the subject seems to have entered the mind of the author, who offers his material to the practitioner <note>original: "artist"</note> without attempting to justify its morality. Moral refinements of this kind were evidently not a concern in fifteenth-century magic. In the following quote, I will depart from my usual habit of translating directly from the original and instead use a version by Robert Turner. I have pruned it slightly where necessary, but it is quite accurate and possesses a pleasant, antique feel.

### CONCERNING THE SUMMONING OF EVIL SPIRITS

If we wish to call any evil spirit to the circle, we must first consider and know his nature: which planet he corresponds to and what duties <note>original: "offices"</note> are assigned to him by that planet. Once this is known, a proper and fitting place for his summoning should be found, based on the nature of the planet and the quality of that spirit's duties, as closely as possible. For example, if his power is over the sea, rivers, or floods, then a place on the shore should be chosen, and so on for the others. Similarly, a convenient time should be chosen, based both on the quality of the air—which should be calm, clear, quiet, and suitable for spirits to take on physical forms—and on the quality and nature of the planet, and so

<page-num>80</page-num>

also of the spirit. This means choosing his specific day and noting the time when he rules—whether it is lucky or unlucky, day or night—as the stars and spirits require. Once these things are considered, a circle should be drawn at the chosen location, both for the protection of the summoner <note>original: "invocant"</note> and for the strengthening <note>original: "confirmation"</note> of the spirit's presence. Inside the circle, the general Divine Names should be written, along with those things that provide us with protection. You must also write the Divine Names that rule that specific planet, along with the duties of the spirit itself. Finally, include the names of the good spirits who rule over and are able to bind and restrain that...
