<meta>This page continues the instructions for the ritual summoning of spirits, likely excerpted from the "Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy" (pseudo-Agrippa). It outlines the physical preparations, the hierarchy of prayers, and the methods for compelling stubborn spirits to appear or depart.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>

Spirit which we intend to call. If we wish to further strengthen our circle, we may add symbols and pentacles <note>Pentacles: Symbolic diagrams, often inscribed on parchment or metal, used to protect the magician and command spirits.</note> that correspond to the work. We may also, whether inside or outside the circle, frame a geometric figure <gloss><u>1</u></gloss> <note>original: "angular figure" — likely a triangle or other polygon used to contain the spirit.</note> inscribed with numbers that are appropriate to our task. Furthermore, the operator must be provided with lights, perfumes, ointments, and medicines prepared according to the nature of the planet and the Spirit. These partially align with the Spirit because of their natural and celestial virtues, and are also presented to the Spirit as a form of religious and ritual worship. The operator must also be equipped with holy and consecrated items, which are necessary for the defense of the summoner and his companions, and also serve as bonds to bind and constrain the Spirits. These include holy papers, lamens <note>original: "lamens" — metal plates or amulets worn by the magician for protection or to show authority over a spirit.</note>, pictures, pentacles, swords, scepters, garments of suitable material and color, and similar things.

When all these are provided, and the master and his companions are within the circle along with the tools he is using, let him begin to pray with a loud voice and an appropriate gesture and expression. Let him offer a formal prayer to God, and afterward appeal to the good Spirits. If he wishes to read any prayers, psalms, or gospels for his defense, they should be done first. Thereafter, let him begin to summon the Spirit he desires with a gentle and loving enchantment directed toward all corners of the world, proclaiming his own authority and power. Let him then rest a little, looking around to see if any Spirit appears. If the Spirit delays, let him repeat his invocation as before, until he has done it three times.

If the Spirit is still stubborn <note>original: "pertinacious"</note> and will not appear, let him begin to conjure it with Divine Power, but in such a way that all the conjurations and proclamations agree with the nature and duties of the Spirit itself. Repeat this three times, from stronger to stronger, using severe rebukes, insults, curses, punishments, suspensions from its office and power, and the like.

After all these steps are finished, pause again for a moment. If any Spirit appears, the summoner should turn toward it, receive it politely, and—earnestly appealing to it—ask for its name. Then, proceeding further, let him ask whatever he wishes. But if the Spirit shows itself to be stubborn or lying in any way, let it be bound by suitable conjurations. If you still suspect a lie, use the consecrated sword to draw a triangle or pentacle outside the circle and compel the Spirit to enter it. If you want any promise confirmed by an oath, stretch the sword out of the circle and make the Spirit swear by laying its hand upon the sword. Then, having obtained from the Spirit what you desire, or being otherwise satisfied, give it permission to depart with polite words, commanding it to do no harm. If it will not leave, compel it with powerful conjurations and, if necessary, expel it by exorcism and by burning foul-smelling incense <note>original: "contrary fumigations" — the use of unpleasant scents to drive away unwanted spirits.</note>. When it has departed, do not leave the circle, but stay and pray for your protection and safety, giving thanks to God and the good 

<page-num>82</page-num>

angels. Once all these things have been performed in order, you may depart.

<vocab>magic circle, pentacles, lamens, conjuration, exorcism, summoner, fumigations, spirits</vocab>

<summary>This page details the ritual steps for summoning a spirit, including the tools needed, the prayers to be said, and the methods for commanding or dismissing a spirit using divine authority and physical symbols.</summary>
<keywords>magic circle, ritual tools, conjuration, exorcism, pentacles, magical protection, ceremonial magic</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the ritual summoning of spirits, specifically dealing with what to do if a spirit fails to appear, the necessity of a "license to depart," and the creation of a "Book of Spirits" to ensure their future obedience.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>

But if your hopes are frustrated and no spirit appears, do not despair. Instead, leave the circle and return at another time, repeating the process as before. If you judge that you have made a mistake in any part of the ritual, you should correct it by adding or removing elements. Constant repetition often increases your authority and power, striking terror into the spirits and humbling them into obedience.

Because of this, some practitioners choose to make a "gate" in the circle through which they can enter and exit. They open and shut this gate as they please, fortifying it with Holy Names and pentacles <note>Pentacles: Symbolic diagrams, often inscribed on parchment or metal, used to protect the magician and command spirits.</note>. It is also important to note that if no spirits appear and the Master, being exhausted, decides to stop and give up, he must not leave without "licensing" <note>original: "licensing" — a formal ritual command giving the spirits permission to leave, ensuring they do not remain to haunt or harm the magician.</note> the spirits to depart. Those who neglect this are in great danger unless they are protected by some powerful spiritual defense. Often, spirits are present even if they are not visible (intending to frighten the one who calls them), manifesting either within the tools the magician uses or in the ritual itself. In these cases, the "license to depart" is not given unconditionally, but is instead a form of "conditional suspension" until the spirits prove themselves obedient.

When we intend to achieve a specific result through evil spirits and a physical appearance is not necessary, we do this by creating the required ritual tool or object. This might be an image, a ring, a scroll, a candle, a symbol <note>original: "character" — a specific occult sign or sigil unique to a particular spirit.</note>, a sacrifice, or something similar. The name of the spirit must be written on the object along with its symbol, according to the needs of the experiment. This is done by writing in blood or using a perfume <note>Perfume: In magic, "perfumes" refer to specific incenses burned to attract or appease particular spirits.</note> that the spirit finds pleasing. One should also offer frequent prayers to God and the good angels before summoning the evil spirit and commanding it by Divine Power.

---

Beyond the formal devilry <note>original: "diabolism"</note> of this process, there are instructions for creating a "Book of Evil Spirits," to be prepared through a ceremony according to their names and hierarchy. Through a "holy oath," the immediate obedience of the spirit whose name is written in the book is supposedly guaranteed. The book itself must be made of the purest and cleanest paper that has never been used before—a requirement that may have been important in the days of the palimpsest <note>Palimpsest: A manuscript page from which text has been scraped off so it could be reused; magical tradition usually requires "virgin" materials that haven't been "contaminated" by previous use.</note>, but is, of course, hardly necessary in our own time. An image of the spirit must be drawn on the left side and its symbol on the right, preceded by the oath that includes the spirit's name, rank, location, office, and power. 

The ritual must be performed on the day and in the hour of the planet associated with that spirit <note>Planetary hours: A system of traditional astrology that assigns each hour of the day to the influence of one of the seven ancient planets.</note>. Once the book is finished, it must be well-bound and decorated, and fitted with markers and seals. Opening it at random after its consecration <note>Consecration: The ritual act of dedicating an object to a religious or magical purpose, "charging" it with power.</note> might put the operator in danger. It should be kept with reverence and protected from being dishonored, otherwise it will lose its power. Consecrating the book is quite difficult, as every spirit named within it must be summoned to the circle. The binding oaths must be read in the spirit's presence, and each spirit in turn must be forced to place its hand where its image and symbol are drawn to "confirm and consecrate the same with a special and common oath." In short, the document must be formally and legally delivered as the "act and deed" of each spirit. During this ceremony, the book must be placed within a triangle drawn outside the protective circle.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Book of Spirits" and the specific appearances of spirits associated with different planets, drawing comparisons between the *Lemegeton* and the writings of pseudo-Agrippa.</meta>

There is no doubt that these instructions were written by someone well-acquainted with the *Lemegeton* <note>original: *Lemegeton* — also known as the *Lesser Key of Solomon*, a famous 17th-century grimoire concerning the summoning of demons.</note>, or that the *Book of Spirits* <note>original: *Liber Spirituum*</note> in question is the same one mentioned by Wierus <note>Johann Weyer (1515–1588), a Dutch physician and occultist who wrote *Pseudomonarchia Daemonum*.</note>. The appearances taken by the summoned spirits differ

<page-num>p. 84</page-num>

slightly from those described in the *Lesser Key*. Furthermore, the *Lesser Key*—at least in its *Goetic* <note>original: *Goëtic* — relating to Goetia, a branch of magic focused on summoning demons or lower spirits.</note> section—does not assign spirits to specific planets. According to the writings of pseudo-Agrippa <note>pseudo-Agrippa: Works falsely attributed to the famous occultist Henry Cornelius Agrippa; specifically referring here to the *Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy*.</note>, the Spirits of Saturn usually appear with tall, thin bodies and angry faces. They have four faces: one in the usual position, another at the back of the head, and two with beaks on each side. They also have a shining black face on each knee. They move like the wind, accompanied by a sensation like an earthquake. Their sign is white earth, "whiter than any snow." Their specific forms include a bearded king riding on a dragon; <u>1</u> an old bearded man; an old woman leaning on a staff; a boy; a dragon; an owl; a black garment; a hook or sickle; and a juniper tree. How the last three manifestations could possibly possess six faces is a mystery that must be left for occult experts to solve.

The Spirits of Jupiter appear with a physical form that is ruddy and hot-tempered <note>original: "sanguine and choleric" — terms from the ancient theory of the Four Humors; "sanguine" suggests a blood-rich, energetic nature, while "choleric" suggests irritability.</note>. They are of medium height. Their movement is "horrible and frightening," yet they have kind faces and speak gently. They are the color of iron (which really should have linked them to Mars); they move like flashing lightning and are accompanied by thunder. Their sign is the appearance of men around the magic circle who seem to be being eaten by lions. Their specific forms include a king with a drawn sword riding a lion; <u>2</u> a person wearing a miter <note>Miter: A tall, pointed ceremonial hat worn by high-ranking clergy.</note> and long robes; a young woman crowned with laurel and decorated with flowers; a bull; a stag; a peacock; a sky-blue garment; a sword; and a boxwood tree.

The Spirits of Mars have tall, hot-tempered bodies and a repulsive appearance. They are brown, dark-skinned, or red in color. They have horns like a deer, claws like a griffin <note>Griffin: A mythical creature with the body of a lion and the head and wings of an eagle.</note>, and they roar like wild—

<page-num>p. 85</page-num>

### PLATE V: CHARACTERS OF EVIL SPIRITS

<note>This plate displays 27 distinct occult symbols or "characters" used in ritual magic to represent or summon specific infernal entities. They range from simple geometric lines to complex, representational drawings like faces, animals, and tools.</note>

...bulls. They move like burning fire, and their presence is signaled by thunder and lightning around the magic circle. Their specific forms include an armed king riding a wolf; <note>1</note> an armed man; a woman holding a shield on her thigh; a female goat; a horse; a stag; a red garment; wool; and a cheeselip <note>original: "cheestip"—likely referring to "cheeselip," an old name for rennet or the plant Lady’s Bedstraw, used to curdle milk.</note>. "Wool of a choleric disposition" is perhaps a Goetic <term>Goetic: relating to Goetia, a system of magic focused on summoning demons or lower spirits.</term> form of gun-cotton <note>gun-cotton: an early explosive made of nitrocellulose; Waite is humorously suggesting that the "angry" wool in the grimoire acts like a modern explosive.</note>.

The Spirits of the Sun are usually large in body and limb, full-blooded, robust, and of a golden color tinged with blood—which recalls the pomegranates mentioned by Mrs. Browning <note>Elizabeth Barrett Browning (1806–1861), a prominent Victorian poet known for her vivid imagery.</note>. Their motion is like that of lightning; their sign is to cause the magician to break into a sweat—though this might simply be a natural property of any of these strange celestial beings of the underworld. Their specific forms include a king with a sceptre riding a lion; a crowned king; <note>2</note> a queen with a sceptre; a bird (not otherwise described, but likely anything except a bird of paradise); a lion; a rooster; a golden garment; a sceptre; and lastly, something that Robert Turner <note>Robert Turner (fl. 1650s) was an English translator of several important occult and botanical works.</note> wisely left untranslated: *caudatus*, which means "having a tail" <note>original: *caudatus*</note>.

The Spirits of Venus have bodies of medium height and pleasant faces, the upper part of which is golden and the lower part white or green. They move like a brilliant star. Their sign is the appearance of young women playing around the circle and trying to lure the Magician to join them. Their specific forms include a king with a sceptre riding a camel; a...

<meta>This page concludes the description of planetary spirits (specifically Venus, Mercury, and the Moon) and provides a critical evaluation of the source material, the pseudo-Agrippa "Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy."</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

...a naked maiden; a female goat; a camel—possibly the terrible demon described by Cazotte; <note>Jacques Cazotte (1719–1792) was a French author known for his occult-themed novel *The Devil in Love* (Le Diable amoureux).</note> a dove; a white or green garment; and the herb savine. <note>savine: *Juniperus sabina*, a type of juniper bush traditionally associated with magic and folk medicine.</note> <u>3</u>

The Spirits of Mercury commonly appear with a body of medium height. They are described as cold, liquid, and moist—qualities that sound redundant, but the reference is to the properties of quicksilver. <term>quicksilver: the metal mercury, which is liquid at room temperature.</term> That is to say, the "Mercury of the philosophers" <note>An alchemical term for a purified or symbolic form of the element.</note> was supposed not to wet the hand, but the mercurial spirits of the sorcerer apparently did. They are, moreover, beautiful and polite in speech, taking human shape and looking like armed knights. In their movement, they are comparable to silver-colored clouds. Their sign is that they cause horror and fear in the practitioner. Their specific forms include a king riding on a bear; <u>1</u> a handsome youth; a woman holding a distaff <term>distaff: a tool used in spinning wool or flax, often associated with domesticity or the Fates.</term>—it is difficult to understand how such an apparition can look like an armed knight, a criticism which, if it were worthwhile, might apply to all the remaining modes of manifestation; a dog; a female bear; a magpie; a garment of many shifting colors; a rod; and a little staff.

Finally, the Spirits of the Moon have large, soft, phlegmatic bodies <term>phlegmatic: in old medicine, this meant cold, moist, and sluggish, associated with the element of water.</term>, colored like a dark cloud. Their faces are swollen, their heads bald, their eyes are red and watery <note>original: "rheumy"</note>, and their teeth are like those of a wild boar. They move like a great storm sweeping across the sea. Their sign is a heavy shower of rain falling around the magic circle. Their particular forms are a king resembling an archer riding on a doe; a little boy; a huntress with bow and arrows; a cow; a small doe; a goose; a green or silver-colored garment; an arrow; and a many-footed creature—perhaps a centipede.

The sheer stupidity of this confused list makes the forged *Fourth Book* <note>The *Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy* was a famous 16th-century grimoire falsely attributed to the philosopher Henry Cornelius Agrippa.</note> look even more ridiculous than it otherwise deserves. Yet, it mimics the style of Agrippa's genuine treatises with a skill that is not entirely lacking, and it has just as much claim to be taken seriously as any of the other patchwork magic manuals. It is difficult to say whether the book is more aligned with the devil or with sheer folly, except for the fact that these two are often one and the same.

## § 6. The Heptameron

The *Fourth Book* of Cornelius Agrippa <note>The "Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy" is a famous grimoire falsely attributed to the scholar Henry Cornelius Agrippa.</note> was much too informal and left too much to the discretion of the practitioner to be satisfactory for a science as exact as Ceremonial Magic. A procedure that left nothing to the imagination and required no skill other than the patient, exact following of basic rules was necessary for the weaknesses of the average sorcerer. *The Heptameron, or Magical Elements* <term>Heptameron: from the Greek for "Seven Days," referring here to rituals for the seven days of the week.</term> attributed to Peter de Abano is an attempt to fill this gap and offer the beginner a complete wizard's toolkit. 

The introduction says—which might, according to the hypothesis <note>original: "ex hypothesi"</note>, be written by a later author—that Cornelius Agrippa seems to have written for the learned and those well-experienced in this art; he does not treat specifically of the ceremonies but mentions them in a general way. Those who have not "tasted magical superstitions" may find them ready for use here. "In short, in this book are kept the principles of magical instructions." It can be admitted at once that this goal is strictly fulfilled. What the practitioner must do and how he should perform it, so as to "draw spirits into conversation," are matters explained so plainly that even a simple traveler could not lose his way. Assuming the practitioner takes on a priestly role, or has a priest as an accomplice, the process is so simple that failure could not easily be blamed on a mistake by the operator.

It would be unfair to assume that the *Heptameron* is any more authentic regarding its author than the work to which it is supposedly a sequel. Its real authorship is shrouded in much the same mystery as that of the "False-Agrippa" <note>original: "pseudo-Agrippa"</note>. There are several serious reasons why the pupil of Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was a German abbot and famous occultist who taught Cornelius Agrippa.</note> should not have written the forged *Fourth Book*, but Peter of Abano is not an unlikely person to associate with the *Magical Elements*—if it were not for a minor chronological gap of about three hundred years. 

<margin>p. 90</margin>
It is true that Agrippa supposedly wrote on Magic, and Abano on Astrology and Geomancy <term>geomancy: a method of divination using patterns of points made in the earth or sand.</term> (unless his attributed works in those areas are also forgeries); however, the *Heptameron* was never mentioned for three centuries after the death of its reputed author in 1316. Its tone is too clearly modern, and it is too obviously a sequel <gloss>1</gloss> to a much more recent work, for it to possibly be a record from the 1300s.

Peter of Abano, from a town near Padua, was born in 1250. He was a learned physician of his era who attempted to reconcile <note>original: "conciliate"</note> different medical systems and is believed to have been the first European to quote Averroes <note>Averroes (Ibn Rushd) was a 12th-century Islamic philosopher from Spain known for his influential commentaries on Aristotle.</note>. He established himself at...

<summary>This page concludes descriptions of Lunar spirits and introduces the Heptameron, a practical magic manual attributed to Peter of Abano. Waite argues the book is likely a later forgery designed to provide simpler, more rigid instructions for sorcerers than Agrippa’s works.</summary>
<keywords>Heptameron, Peter de Abano, Lunar Spirits, Cornelius Agrippa, Ceremonial Magic, Occult History, pseudo-Agrippa, Averroes</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Heptameron and provides a biographical sketch of its traditional author, Peter of Abano, highlighting his persecution by the Inquisition.</meta>

Paris, but at the incitement of jealous colleagues, he was accused of heresy and fled to his birthplace. At Padua, a professorship in medicine was created for him, but the accusations followed their victim. Some charged him with denying the existence of demons, while others claimed he obtained his knowledge from seven imps whom he kept in a bottle. However this may be, the Inquisition began a legal process, but the intended victim was delivered by death—as some say, on the eve of his execution. 

This intervention infuriated the Tribunal, even though the testament Peter of Abano left behind affirmed his belief in the orthodox faith. The city magistrates were ordered, under threat of excommunication, to exhume his body, but it was removed by a faithful servant and buried secretly in another church. The Inquisition demanded the punishment of the offender but was ultimately content to burn the dead physician in effigy <note>Burning in effigy: a practice where a model or dummy of a person was burned when the person themselves could not be reached or was already dead.</note>. As a counterweight, a century later, a bust of him was placed in the Padua town hall. His genuine works, which are honestly unreadable, show no knowledge of the occult sciences beyond a belief in astrology, which in those days was as universal as Rome and as powerful as the Holy Tribunal. He remains, therefore, one of the moral martyrs of Magic, "falsely accused" <note>original: *faussement accusé*</note>, as the scholar Gabriel Naudé <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653) was a French librarian and scholar known for defending famous people accused of magic.</note> put it. His accusation and the way it was prosecuted also remain among the lesser "glories" of the Holy Office <note>The Holy Office was the department of the Roman Curia responsible for the Inquisition.</note>.

If we accept the *Heptameron* as a work belonging to the period of its first publication, it is placed here among the "Rituals of a Composite Character." This is not because it openly deals with devils, but because the nature of its angels and spirits is revealed by the way they are summoned; in short, they are described as angels but threatened as if they were demons.

The procedure is divided into two parts: a general method for summoning the "Spirits of the Air"—who are undoubtedly demons—and a set of angelic conjurations specific to each day of the week. The second section supposedly belongs to the category of White Magic—if I may use this grand distinction in a moment of mockery—since the spirits involved are said to be good and great. However, their duties are a confusing mix, including finding treasure, discovering secrets, starting wars, opening locks and bolts, winning the love of women, making men lustful, and spreading hatred and evil thoughts. Clearly, "White Magic" of this kind is much darker than it is described. 

Although the entire *Heptameron* is attributed to one person, only the first part is credited to Peter of Abano in the text. In that section, the magician’s personal preparation matches what is described in the Second Part of this current book. The ceremony itself—which, if cited at all, would have to be printed in its entirety <note>original: *in extenso*</note> because it has no parts that can be easily separated—is much too elaborate to be included here. This is especially true since the *Lemegeton* <note>The *Lemegeton*, or *Lesser Key of Solomon*, is one of the most famous and influential grimoires of the 17th century.</note> will provide a fairly complete idea of the goal and purpose of these "Composite Rituals" in a broad sense and will show that all traditional distinctions between types of magic dissolve within them.

<keywords>Peter of Abano, Inquisition, Heptameron, White Magic, Spirits of the Air, Lemegeton, Gabriel Naudé, Composite Rituals</keywords>

90:1 A sequel, moreover, which contains several direct references, as, for example: "But how they appear has been described already in the former book of magical ceremonies"—*The Conjuration of the Lord's Day*. This statement repeats with slight variations throughout the *Heptameron*. It may be advisable to add that Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535) was a famous German polymath and occult writer.</note> lived in the sixteenth century.

## § 7. The Sacred Magic of Abramelin the Mage

The existence of this text had been known to scholars for a long time before it was made available in an English translation by the editor of the *Key of Solomon* <note>The editor mentioned is S.L. MacGregor Mathers, who published his translation in 1898.</note>. Hobbyists of occult science—specifically those interested in its more questionable practical branches—came into possession of this "pearl of tradition" in 1898, and I assume it is now quite familiar in certain circles. I am sorry that it seems necessary to say a few words concerning it here. Mr. Mathers provided an introduction that does honor to his particular brand of talent; that is to say, it serves as an eloquent warning of what an expert should avoid when expressing his views or writing English prose.

The text is preserved in the Arsenal Library <note>original: *Bibliothèque de l'Arsenal*</note> in Paris and is a French manuscript dating to the early eighteenth century. It is also the only copy definitely known to collectors, though there is a rumor of another in Holland. According to its own claims, the work dates to the year 1458, written by a man named Abraham (the son of Simon and the father of Lamech), for whose benefit it was specifically designed. The text claims the original was in Hebrew, a statement naturally accepted by the translator. This claim is perhaps made more 

<margin>p. 93</margin>

believable from his perspective by the overwhelming internal evidence that the work was actually written by a Christian. The text is full of Christian references and allusions to later grimoires <term>grimoires: textbooks of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects like talismans and amulets, and how to perform magical spells</term>. 

The point is not worth debating on its own merits, but certain details provide unmistakable proof for those who understand textual evidence: (a) references to the "Jewish Sabbath," (b) the custom of a father’s blessing, (c) mentions of those who leave Christianity for Judaism, (d) references to the festival of Easter, (e) allusions to the New Testament, (f) the use of the Vulgate <note>The Vulgate is the late-4th-century Latin translation of the Bible.</note>, and (g) its theories concerning Guardian Angels.

In conclusion, the work dates to roughly the same time as the known manuscript [early 1700s]; it was never written in Hebrew, nor by anyone who knew Hebrew. The claim that the author was a Jew is just as valid as the translator's "brilliant" speculation that this supposed Abraham was a descendant of the *other* "Abraham the Jew," whose mysterious alchemical book allegedly came into the hands of Nicholas Flamel <note>Nicholas Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a French scribe who, centuries after his death, gained a reputation as an alchemist who discovered the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, his supposed contemporary.

<summary>Waite critiques the origins of "The Sacred Magic of Abramelin the Mage," arguing that the text is an 18th-century Christian forgery rather than a 15th-century Hebrew original as claimed.</summary>
<keywords>Abramelin the Mage, S.L. MacGregor Mathers, Arsenal Library, forgery, Nicholas Flamel, Jewish authorship, Guardian Angels, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of "The Sacred Magic of Abramelin the Mage," moving from its manuscript history to a summary of its unique claims and internal contradictions.</meta>

Abramelin the Mage was the teacher of Abraham the magician, who recorded the knowledge he received from this source and added an account of his own life and occult adventures. In several ways, this text differs significantly from the typical magical rituals of the time. For example, it mocks the practice of timing magical acts according to the stars or seasons. It argues that such formalities have no power over spirits and no value in supernatural matters. Based on this, it logically concludes—as explained in a specific chapter—that most magical books are fake and useless. It also claims they are evil, not just because of their superstitious focus on celestial signs, but because they use gibberish words in the processes of conjuration and similar acts. According to the text, these words were invented by the devil or by wicked men. It also emphasizes that rituals of this kind fail to call upon God, though this is clearly a false accusation against the existing literature.

While the highly critical Abraham is careful to call for divine help, his incantations are few and simple. Instead, he relies on the protection of those Guardian Angels which Christian tradition and doctrine attribute to every human soul. Conversely, he refuses to use magical words, symbols, or pentacles <note>A pentacle is a talisman or symbol used in magical rituals, often a five-pointed star</note>, which he condemns as abominations created by diabolical sorcerers. He therefore promotes a highly simplified version of "Art Magic," though what he removes in ritual complexity, he adds in the dramatic setup of the surroundings <note>original: *mise en scène*</note>. 

At first glance, his claims might make one think we are in the presence of new methods or even better intentions regarding "magical vanity." Surely, this work is on God's side; even the title is encouraging, claiming the book contains the "Holy Magic" that God gave to Moses, Aaron, David, Solomon, and the other saints, patriarchs, and prophets. The text repeatedly insists that it represents true Divine Wisdom, all for the glory and high honor of God, the benefit of the pious practitioner, and the good of the human race.

However, when we look at the actual dealings with spirits, we find that the "superior princes" are none other than Lucifer, Leviathan, Satan, and Belial. The lesser powers include Astaroth, Asmodeus, Beelzebub, and all others of that kind <note>original: *et hoc genus omne*</note>. Furthermore, these demons can be summoned for either good or evil purposes. The goals are the same old interests and "glorious" goals of White and Black Magic found throughout the history of all such rituals. These include finding stolen goods, discovering buried treasure, inciting hatred and conflict, casting spells, and the usual sexual magic <note>original: *venereal experiments*</note>. It even includes methods for Necromancy <note>The practice of communicating with the dead</note>, an art rejected by all but the most extreme forms of Black Magic.

<summary>This page describes the "Sacred Magic of Abramelin the Mage," noting its rejection of traditional astrological timing and symbols in favor of simple prayers and Guardian Angels. However, it points out the hypocrisy of the text, which claims to be holy while providing instructions for summoning major demons for worldly and even malevolent ends.</summary>
<keywords>Abramelin the Mage, Abraham the Magician, Guardian Angels, Holy Magic, Lucifer, Leviathan, Satan, Belial, White Magic, Black Magic, Necromancy, Rituals</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's critique of "The Sacred Magic of Abramelin the Mage" and begins a detailed catalog of the most famous rituals of Black Magic.</meta>

Now that the text has been made available, we can leave it here. No doubt the translator will continue to view it as a work of great "importance" from an occult perspective, and its existence in an English version as "a real benefit" to students. I leave that judgment to him.

->THE SUN-GOD RA SLAYING THE DRAGON OF DARKNESS.<-
->From the Papyrus of Hunefer, around 1370 B.C.<-

# CHAPTER IV

## The Rituals Of Black Magic

### § 1. The True Grimoire <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note>

There are four specific and blatant handbooks of Black Magic. All are written in the French language, though in three cases—as is common with ceremonial literature—they claim Italian connections, whether real or invented. They are:

I. THE TRUE GRIMOIRE <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note>, or the Most Approved Keys of Solomon the Hebrew Rabbi, in which the most hidden secrets, both natural and supernatural, are immediately revealed; however, it is necessary that the demons should be satisfied on their part. <gloss>1</gloss> Translated from the Hebrew by Plaingière, a Dominican Jesuit, <note>The term "Dominican Jesuit" is a contradiction, as these are two separate and often rival Catholic religious orders. Such titles were frequently invented by printers to give these books an air of forbidden ecclesiastical authority.</note> <gloss>2</gloss> along with a collection of curious secrets. Published by Alibeck the Egyptian in 1517. <gloss>3</gloss>

II. TRUE BLACK MAGIC, or the Secret of Secrets, a manuscript found at Jerusalem in the Tomb of Solomon, containing: 1. Forty-five talismans with their illustrations, as well as the instructions for using them and their marvelous properties. 2. All magical symbols <note>original: *characters*</note> known to this day. <gloss>1</gloss> Translated from the Hebrew of the magician <note>original: *Magus*</note> Iroë-Grego. <gloss>2</gloss> Rome. In the year of grace, 1750.

III. THE GREAT GRIMOIRE <note>original: *Grand Grimoire*</note>, featuring the powerful Key <note>original: *Clavicle*</note> of Solomon and of Black Magic; or the Infernal Devices of the Great Agrippa for the discovery of all hidden treasures and the subduing of every class of spirits, together with a summary of all the magical arts. (In its earliest edition, published without a location or date.)

<summary>This page introduces Chapter IV by listing the primary historical handbooks of Black Magic, starting with the "Grimorium Verum" and "The Grand Grimoire," noting their dubious claims of ancient or ecclesiastical origins.</summary>
<keywords>Black Magic, Grimorium Verum, Grand Grimoire, Keys of Solomon, Ra, Dragon of Darkness, Agrippa, occult bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the four major grimoires of Black Magic, specifically detailing the deceptive origins and corrupted contents of the True Grimoire (Grimorium Verum) and the Constitution of Pope Honorius.</meta>

# IV. THE CONSTITUTION OF POPE HONORIUS THE GREAT, wherein may be found the Arcane Conjurations which must be used against the Spirits of Darkness. With a Collection of the Most Rare Secrets. Rome, 1670.

These are all tiny volumes, technically called "duodecimo" <note>A printing term for a small book size, about 5 by 7 inches, made by folding a sheet of paper into 12 leaves.</note>, but they are much smaller according to modern measurements.

The date specified in the title of the *True Grimoire* <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note> is undeniably fake. The work actually dates to the middle of the eighteenth century, and the "Memphis" listed as its place of publication is actually Rome. The *Grand Grimoire* is not any older. The *Grimoire of Honorius* is said to have first appeared in 1629, but it has sometimes been wrongly attributed to the same period in the previous century.

As indicated by the author to whom it is attributed, the *True Grimoire* is based to some extent upon the *Key of Solomon*. The main points of resemblance are in the description of the magical instruments and in some of the forms of prayer. It clearly identifies the powers it intends

<page-num>98</page-num>

to invoke by the name of "Devils." <gloss>1</gloss> At the same time, it refers to them nominally as belonging to the four elements—which would connect them with spirits like Sylphs (air) and Salamanders (fire)—but this classification, mentioned only occasionally, does not actually hold true throughout the text. There is an account of the Hierarchy of Spirits, with Lucifer, Beelzebuth, and Astaroth as the chief rulers. <gloss>2</gloss> A portion of this account is taken from the *Lemegeton* <note>A famous 17th-century grimoire also known as the Lesser Key of Solomon.</note>, perhaps by way of the *Pseudo-monarchy of Demons* <note>original: *Pseudo-monarchia*</note> by Johann Weyer <note>original: Wierus</note>. The work claims to be divided into three sections, containing:

(a.) The Characters and Seals of the Demons, with the formulas for calling them forth and dismissing them.
(b.) A description of the Natural and Supernatural Secrets which can be performed by the power of the Demons, without any deception.
(c.) The Key of the work and its proper application.

Setting aside typographical errors, the manuscript from which this was printed must have been in a state of extreme confusion. In reality, there are no distinct divisions, and the little volume is full of Latin passages that often defy translation. For example: "let the will stand for reason" <note>original: *sic pro ratione voluntas*</note>; "so that it be included in this work, the least among the learned clergy" <note>original: *ut illud sit hoe in opere inclusum minimo clerum in doctis*</note>; and "because he will become a chief friend, he will become an enemy" <note>original: *quia amicus fiet capitalis, fiet inimicus*</note>. Likewise, the text uses "Holy of Kings" <note>original: *Sanctum Regum*</note> throughout instead of "Holy Kingdom" <note>original: *Sanctum Regnum*</note>. There are two folding illustrations of symbols and seals, many of which have no connection to the text. Meanwhile, other seals essential to the rituals are missing. These gaps are filled in modern Italian versions, which likely follow a different Italian edition that was also the source of

<page-num>99</page-num>

the French translation; however, that specific edition is unknown to me, and indeed to most experts on rare books. The work, as it currently exists, consists of only two parts: the *True Grimoire* <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note> itself and certain "Rare and Astounding Magical Secrets." The first part can be analyzed as follows:

(a.) Directions for preparing the practitioner, all of which are personal in nature and similar to those found in the *Key of Solomon* <note>original: Clavicle</note>.
(b.) Instructions for making the magical...

<summary>This page discusses the bibliographical history and contents of the *Grimorium Verum* and the *Grimoire of Honorius*, noting their fraudulent dates and corrupted Latin text.</summary>
<keywords>Grimorium Verum, Grimoire of Honorius, Black Magic, Demonology, Lucifer, Beelzebuth, Astaroth, Key of Solomon, Hierarchy of Spirits, book history</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the True Grimoire (Grimorium Verum), comparing its contents—specifically its tools, materials, and rituals—to the Key of Solomon and other later magical handbooks.</meta>

the tools required for the work, which are also similar to the *Key of Solomon* <note>original: "Clavicle"</note>. (c) The preparation of the virgin parchment on which the symbols and seals are to be written, showing distinct differences from the *Key of Solomon*. (d) The methods for summoning <note>original: "evocation"</note> and dismissing <note>original: "discharge"</note> spirits. Aside from the fact that the summoned spirits are Lucifer, Beelzebuth, Astaroth, and their subordinates and ministers, this first and most important section is no more offensive—and indeed, hardly more foolish—than most of the procedures in the original work it imitates.

The second part contains the typical strange recipes common to all the later magical handbooks <note>original: "Grimoires"</note>, including the *Admirable Secrets* of the supposed Albertus Magnus, the *Little Albert*, and others. Where it shows any significant variations, those changes usually lean toward Black Magic. Some are sexual <note>original: "venereal"</note> in the more offensive sense of the word; others are simply revolting; while still others, such as the "Magic Garters," are derived from the *Key of Solomon*. Finally, there are certain rituals that might claim to be those of White Magic <gloss>1</gloss> and are concerned with the ceremonial practice of inducing basic clairvoyance.

We may therefore conclude that the *True Grimoire* <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note> itself is no more demonic than the first part of the *Lemegeton* <note>Also known as the *Lesser Key of Solomon*.</note>, which actually contains the rituals for summoning exactly the same spirits.

### § 2. True Black Magic

The magical handbook <note>original: "Grimoire"</note> titled *True Black Magic* is simply an adapted version of the *Key of Solomon*, featuring the same introductory sections <note>original: "preambles"</note>, the same ritual, the same talismans, and symbols <note>original: "characters"</note> which are similar when they are not identical. Compared to the reasonably clear and religiously careful presentation of Mr. Mathers <note>Samuel Liddell MacGregor Mathers, a prominent occultist who published an English edition of the Key of Solomon in 1889.</note>, it naturally compares very poorly. Like the *True Grimoire* <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note>, it is exceedingly confused and is rendered almost meaningless by the omission of all the practical instructions. Its malicious or demonic <note>original: "diabolical"</note> element, however, consists more specifically in its introduction of the chapter on works of hatred and destruction. As we have already seen, and as will be more fully established later on, this chapter should undoubtedly be regarded as an essential <note>original: "integral"</note> part of the original work.

### § 3. The Grand Grimoire

The *Grand Grimoire* is the most extravagant <note>original: "fantastic"</note> of the cycle and is introduced with great ceremony by its alleged editor, Antonio Venitiana del Rabina—a person whose name suggests the work's Italian origin. Because of its rarity and the high demand for it, we are informed that it must be regarded as the true *Great Work* <note>original: *Magnum Opus*</note>—a view which may seem illogical <note>original: "inconsequential"</note>, but for which the authority of Rabbinical writers is cited. It is to these authors that we supposedly owe this priceless treasure, which countless frauds <note>original: "charlatans"</note> have tried to forge but have never succeeded in discovering. The copy used by Antonio in preparing his edition was transcribed from the authentic writings of the mighty King Solomon, which were obtained by pure chance. 

> "In truth, what other man, except for this invincible genius, would have had the boldness <note>original: "hardihood"</note> to reveal the devastating <note>original: "withering"</note> words which God uses to strike terror into the rebellious angels and force them into obedience? Having risen into the heavenly heights <note>original: "celestial altitudes"</note> so that he might master the secrets and learn the all-powerful words which constitute all the power of a terrifying and revered <note>original: "venerable"</note> Deity, the essence of those innermost mysteries <note>original: "arcana"</note>, used by an infinite Divinity, was extracted by this great king. He passed all the days of his life in the most difficult research and in pursuit of the most obscure and hopeless secrets. He succeeded ultimately in all his projects, penetrating into the most remote dens <note>original: "haunts"</note> of spirits, whom he bound, one and all, and forced to obey him by the power of his Talisman or Key <note>original: "Clavicle"</note>. In this work, he has revealed to us the planetary <note>original: "stellar"</note> influences, the..."

<summary>Waite analyzes the "True Black Magic" and "Grand Grimoire," identifying the former as a corrupted version of the Key of Solomon and the latter as a legendary, highly sought-after work of Italian origin attributed to Solomon.</summary>
<keywords>True Black Magic, Grand Grimoire, King Solomon, Antonio Venitiana del Rabina, Key of Solomon, magical handbooks, demonic magic, S.L. MacGregor Mathers</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Grand Grimoire," specifically quoting its dramatic claims regarding the power of the "Blasting Rod" and beginning an analysis of the book's contents and reputation.</meta>

"...the alignment <note>original: "constellation"</note> of the planets and the method for summoning <note>original: "evocation"</note> all ranks <note>original: "hierarchies"</note> of spirits by reciting certain divine names <note>original: "sublime Appellations"</note>, as they are set down for you later in this book. It also describes the true construction <note>original: "composition"</note> and effects of the terrifying Blasting Rod, which makes spirits tremble. This is the same rod God used to arm His Angel when Adam and Eve were expelled from the Garden of Eden <note>original: "Earthly Paradise"</note>. With this rod, He finally struck down the rebellious angels, casting their ambitions into the most horrific depths by its power—this Rod which gathers clouds, scatters storms, turns away lightning, or hurls any of these upon any part of the earth at the will of the person wielding it."

This is the introduction <note>original: "preamble"</note> to the *Grand Grimoire*. The work is divided into two parts. The first contains the summoning of Lucifuge <gloss>1</gloss> Rofocale <note>In this tradition, Lucifuge Rofocale is depicted as the prime minister of Hell; the name is likely a play on "Lucifer."</note> using the Blasting Rod. The second

<page-num>102</page-num>

is what Antonio <note>Antonio del Rabina, the supposed editor of the text.</note> mysteriously <note>original: "inscrutably"</note> considers to be the *Sacred Kingdom* <note>original: "Sanctum Regnum"</note>—namely, the ritual for making pacts. However, one of the most striking features of all these magical handbooks <note>original: "Grimoires"</note> is not their wicked intent <note>original: "diabolical malice"</note>, but their unintentional simple-mindedness <note>original: "unconscious ingenuousness"</note>. The pious, almost respectable character of all these operations seems to have been held quite sincerely.

Nevertheless, the *Grand Grimoire* is considered one of the most atrocious books of its kind. It contains a ritual for communicating with the dead <note>original: "Necromancy"</note> that some occult writers—in a rare moment of clarity—say is only possible for a dangerous madman or a hopeless criminal. It must be admitted that the ritual is highly irrational, but when dealing with this type of literature, it is hard to use that as an objection, as it applies to almost everything in the field. As for its criminal nature, this focuses on causing a disruption during midnight Mass on Christmas Eve. 

There is also a description of a poison titled "The Composition of Death, or Philosophical Stone," which is supposed to represent an advanced level of wickedness <note>original: "diabolism"</note>. Éliphas Lévi <note>A famous 19th-century French occultist whose writings shaped modern ceremonial magic.</note> claims that it pretends to provide the "Powder of Projection"—the great mystery of the alchemical sages—but in reality, it gives the "Powder of Consecution." The meaning of this latter term is quite vague and can only be guessed at. Regardless, it cannot really be either of those things, since the mixture is a liquid. Otherwise, it is simply a foolish recipe. Since no illegal use is suggested, it is not demonic <note>original: "diabolical"</note> at all, unless toxicology <note>the scientific study of poisons</note> is inherently Satanic simply because it does not deal in painkillers <note>original: "anodynes"</note>.

There is, of course, no question that the *Grand Grimoire* is a book of Black Magic, and it is natural that a book of Black Magic should be wicked. The most objectionable works are not those which openly announce they are evil, but those which teach evil while pretending to be good. The notable point regarding

<page-num>103</page-num>

the *Grand Grimoire*, other books like it, and the "White" rituals, is that the evil in supposedly evil books is often very superficial <note>original: "thin"</note>. Conversely, the "angelic" elements in rituals claimed to be holy often come dangerously close to being Satanic. The first part of the *Grand Grimoire*, like the *True Grimoire* <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note>, is simply a...

<summary>This page analyzes the "Grand Grimoire," focusing on its legendary "Blasting Rod," its reputation for being "atrocious" due to irrational rituals and poisons, and the blurred line between Black and White magic.</summary>
<keywords>Grand Grimoire, Blasting Rod, Lucifuge Rofocale, Sanctum Regnum, Antonio del Rabina, Necromancy, Éliphas Lévi, Black Magic, Grimorium Verum</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the "Grand Grimoire," focusing on its unique requirement for a soul-selling pact, and introduces the "Grimoire of Honorius," defending it against common occultist misconceptions.</meta>

the process for summoning <note>original: "evocation"</note> evil spirits to force them to surrender hidden treasure. In the second part, the magician is indeed expected to give himself, body and soul, to the demon who serves him in the meantime. There can be no hesitation in admitting that this creates a sharp distinction, not only between the *Grand Grimoire* and all the "Composite Rituals" <note>Books that mix elements of various magical traditions.</note>, but also between the *Grand Grimoire* and other rituals <note>original: "Liturgies"</note> of Black Magic. It is only a weak defense <note>original: "palliation"</note> to say that the contract <note>original: "compact"</note> is phrased as a trick <note>original: "subterfuge"</note> and actually gives nothing to the demon, who here—as is so common in folklore—is fooled, receiving only a shadow instead of something real. 1

### Footnotes

101:1 This change of the fallen "Light-Bearer" into "Fly-the-Light" <note>Lucifer means "Light-Bearer"; Lucifuge is a play on this, meaning "one who flees the light."</note> does not seem to appear in magical literature written before the *Grand Grimoire*. It was later adopted by the occultist Éliphas Lévi, who made it popular among practitioners today. Most occultists are entirely unaware of where the name actually came from.

103:1 Compare the amusing <note>original: "droll"</note> history of the "Devil and his Mother" <note>original: "Dam"</note>, and the story regarding the course on Black Magic taught by the Prince of Darkness at the University of Salamanca <note>In Spanish folklore, the devil was said to teach magic in a cave in Salamanca.</note>.

### § 4. The Grimoire of Honorius

Despite the wickedness of the *Agreements Made with Demons* <note>original: "Pacta Conventa Dæmonum"</note>, the *Grand Grimoire* has not, however, received as much harsh criticism as the *Grimoire of Honorius the Great* (also known as *Honorius the Third*). It is fair to say that almost every accusation made against this remarkable work is false, both in general and in detail. The main difference between these accusations is that some are the result of ignorance, while others are biased misinterpretations. The French occultist, Papus, is the only one who seems to take a middle ground, though he is somewhat vague when 

<margin>p. 104</margin>
he states that the sorcery in this handbook is more dangerous for weak practitioners than for the sorcerer’s enemies. Éliphas Lévi notes that the work is significant for students of occult science. 1 At first glance, it seems to be nothing but a collection of repulsive absurdities. However, for those initiated in the signs and secrets of the Kabbalah <note>A tradition of Jewish mysticism often adapted by Western magicians.</note>, it is claimed to be a true monument to human wickedness. There was rarely a statement with less factual basis; there was never a magical work less connected to Kabbalism. What little connection exists is limited to a few words used in the Conjurations, which are common to all ceremonial magic. In fact, this is the one handbook <note>original: "Grimoire"</note> most filled with Christian elements. However, those who are familiar with the way Lévi interprets things will not take an accusation seriously when it relies on the meaning of Kabbalistic words or signs. It is well known that the French occultist changed their meanings based on his mood, and that he did not truly understand his subject.

<summary>This page discusses the unique soul-selling pact of the "Grand Grimoire" and begins a defense of the "Grimoire of Honorius," arguing that it is more Christian than Kabbalistic despite its dark reputation.</summary>
<keywords>Grand Grimoire, Grimoire of Honorius, Lucifuge Rofocale, Éliphas Lévi, Papus, Kabbalah, Black Magic, pacts, Salamanca, demonology</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of the "Grimoire of Honorius," specifically refuting the sensationalist claims made by later occultists like Éliphas Lévi and clarifying the historical facts of its authorship.</meta>

But Éliphas Lévi was not satisfied with a general accusation; in a later work, <gloss>2</gloss> he elaborated on a more specific charge. Like the *Key of Solomon* and all the Grimoires <note>Historical books of magic spells and instructions.</note>, the work of Honorius requires the sacrifice of a young virgin goat <note>original: "virgin kid"</note> to ensure the practitioner has "virgin parchment" <note>Parchment made from the skin of an animal that had not mated, traditionally used in ritual magic.</note> to write on. However, Lévi claims that when this "abominable author" mentions a kid, he actually means a human child. In making this interpretation, Lévi cannot even use the excuse of the humorous slang term for children used in English, because his knowledge of our language was extremely limited. There is not a shred of evidence for this charge. The sacrifice mentioned in the *Grimoire of Honorius* means exactly the same thing as it does in the *Key of Solomon*. There was a specific ritual purpose for killing the animal, and it was identical in both books.

This covers the misinterpretations of writers who claim to have first-hand knowledge of the work. Others who have mentioned it in recent years have been content to follow the French authority <note>Referring to Lévi.</note> without checking for themselves. For example, Mr. J. H. Slater, in a paper read before the Bibliographical Society and printed in its records, <gloss>1</gloss> describes this Grimoire as an advocate of murder and all kinds of crimes. Furthermore, he confuses it entirely with the *Grand Grimoire*.

When examining the work directly, the first question is its authorship. Based on what we know of magical literature—not to mention the dignity of the papacy—it is highly unlikely that this is the work of a Roman bishop, especially a Pope like Honorius. Éliphas Lévi, who rightly sought to defend the Church of his childhood against ignorant accusations aimed at its leaders, unfortunately defended it poorly as usual. He looked back at history and found that during the time of Honorius, there was an anti-pope <note>A person who claims the papacy in opposition to the one recognized by the Church.</note> set up by Henry IV of Germany who lived an immoral life. Lévi immediately guessed that this man was the likely author of the offensive Grimoire. Again, there is no evidence at all for this guess. It is a very nasty trick <note>original: "un bien vilain procédé"</note>, as the occultist Papus might say, to increase the blame placed on the memory of that unfortunate clergyman without a good reason.

<page-num>106</page-num>

If we look at the facts, they are as follows. The first edition of the Grimoire is said to have appeared in 1629. It is unlikely to have been forged much earlier than the end of the sixteenth century—roughly nine hundred years after the death of its supposed author. The historical Pope Honorius was, it must be admitted, a prolific writer. His sermons and his vast collection of letters have been published in two large volumes in Paris <gloss>1</gloss> under leadership that was unlikely to even mention the forged decree <note>original: "constitution"</note> with anything but contempt. There is no mention of it, which is unfortunate from the perspective of book history <note>original: "bibliography"</note>. However, the editor also excluded another work that is much more reasonably attributed to Honorius and carries no social stigma <note>original: "no odium"</note>. This work is also important to our investigation; it is titled *Pope Honorius, Against the Powers of Darkness* <note>original: "Honorii Papæ, adversus tenebrarum"</note>

<summary>Waite debunks the claim that the Grimoire of Honorius calls for human sacrifice, arguing that such interpretations are based on poor scholarship and a confusion with other texts. He also dismisses the theory that it was written by a medieval anti-pope, dating the forgery instead to the late 16th or early 17th century.</summary>

<keywords>Éliphas Lévi, Grimoire of Honorius, Key of Solomon, ritual sacrifice, anti-pope, Papus, J. H. Slater, forgery, papal authorship</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page transitions from debunking the false accusations made against the Grimoire of Honorius to a direct examination of its contents, specifically focusing on its bold claim of papal authorization.</meta>

*Conjurations of the Prince and his Angels, from the original preserved at Rome*, Rome, 1529. <note>original: "Principem et ejus Angelos Conjurationes ex originale Romæ servato"</note> The authenticity of this work is clearly questioned because it was excluded from official collections. It is impossible to speak with certainty about it because it is so rare; few public libraries—and apparently none in England—possess a copy. However, it is clearly based on the "formularies of exorcism" <note>Standardized prayers and rituals used by the Church to drive out demons.</note>, an official rite of the Church which has a large body of literature behind it. It is not entirely unthinkable that a Pope from the past might have contributed to such literature. Regardless, the association of the Papacy with legitimate exorcism explains why this book was attributed to a Pope. This book of Black Magic may simply be a twisted version of orthodox prayers, or perhaps a form of retaliation—Sorcery’s way of getting revenge on a Pope who cast out devils by portraying him as the leader of those who summon them.

Having said this to defend the Grimoire against unfair criticism, I must now add—based on the book's own evidence—that it is a mean-spirited and fairly clever fake. It was undeniably designed to trick ignorant people of that time who were interested in magic, especially uneducated priests. It does this by pretending to have the official approval of the Apostolic See <note>The office and authority of the Pope.</note> for the practice of Hellish Magic and Necromancy <note>The practice of communicating with the dead.</note>. This entire claim is presented in a very strange way at the beginning of this fake "constitution," and it must be quoted at length to show its full effect.

> The Holy Apostolic See, to which the keys of the Kingdom of Heaven were given by the words Christ Jesus spoke to St. Peter: "I give unto you the Keys of the Kingdom of Heaven, and to you alone the Power of commanding the Prince of Darkness and his angels." These spirits, as slaves of their Master, owe him honor, glory, and obedience, according to those other words of Christ Jesus: "You shall worship the Lord your God, and Him only shall you serve." Therefore, by the Power of these Keys, the Head of the Church has been made the Lord of Hell. But seeing as until now the Sovereign Pontiffs <note>The Popes.</note> alone have possessed the power to use these invocations and command Spirits, His Holiness Honorius III, moved by his duty to care for his flock, has kindly desired to share the methods and the ability to summon and control Spirits with his respected Brothers in Jesus Christ. He has also added the Conjurations which must be used in such cases, all of which are contained in the Bull <note>An official document or decree issued by a Pope.</note> that follows.
>
> ->HONORIUS,<-
>
> Servant of the Servants of God <note>original: "Servus Servorum Dei," a traditional title for the Pope.</note>, to all and each of our respected Brothers of the Holy Roman Church: Cardinals, Archbishops, Bishops, and Abbots; and to all and each of our sons in Jesus Christ: Priests, Deacons, Subdeacons, Acolytes, Exorcists, Singers, Pastors, and Clergy both Secular and Regular <note>"Secular" clergy live in the world (like parish priests), while "Regular" clergy live under a monastic rule (like monks).</note>, Greetings and Apostolic Blessing. In those days when the Son of God, Savior of the World—who was begotten when the time was right and born, in human form, from the House of David—lived on this earth, Whose Most Holy...

<summary>This page argues that the "Grimoire of Honorius" is a clever forgery designed to trick the clergy by claiming the Pope has divine authority over Hell. It quotes the book's preamble, which asserts that the "Keys of Heaven" also grant the Papacy total command over demons.</summary>
<keywords>Grimoire of Honorius, Honorius III, Papal Bull, exorcism, Necromancy, Apostolic See, Keys of Heaven, religious forgery, Black Magic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the text of a fake "papal constitution" attributed to Pope Honorius, which claims to grant priests the power to summon and command demons. It then moves into a bibliographical survey of famous grimoires and their various editions.</meta>

*The Name is Jesus, before which the heavens, earth, and hell bend their knees. We have seen with what power He commanded demons, a power that was also transmitted to Saint Peter through the words: "Upon this rock I will build my Church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it." These words were addressed to Saint Peter as the Head and Foundation of the Church. We, then, who by the mercy of God—and despite our lack of merit—have succeeded to the Supreme Apostolic Office <note>original: Sovereign Apostolate; the Papacy</note>, and as the lawful successor of Saint Peter, hold the Keys to the Kingdom of Heaven in our hands. We desire to share the power of invoking and commanding spirits, which has been reserved for us and our predecessors alone. Wishing, I repeat, by Divine inspiration, to share it with our venerable brethren <note>A traditional address for bishops</note> and dear sons in Jesus Christ, and fearing that during the exorcism of the possessed <gloss>1</gloss>, they might otherwise be terrified by the frightening forms of those rebellious angels who were cast into the abyss for their sins; and fearing also that they might be insufficiently trained in the things that must be performed and observed, and so that those redeemed by the blood of Jesus Christ may not be tormented by any witchcraft or possessed by demons, we have included the method of their invocation in this Bull <note>A formal decree issued by a Pope</note>, which must be strictly observed. And because it is appropriate that the ministers of the Altar should have authority over the rebellious spirits, we hereby delegate to them all*

*powers which we possess, by virtue of the Holy Apostolic Chair, and we require them, by our Apostolic authority, to observe what follows strictly, lest by some negligence unworthy of their status they should bring down upon themselves the wrath of the Most High.*

The *True Grimoire* <note>original: *Grimorium Verum*</note> has been reprinted once in the French language. <gloss>1</gloss> Regarding the Italian version, there have been two modern editions, both poorly produced. <gloss>2</gloss> The book of *True Black Magic* is known only by the edition of 1750. The *Great Grimoire* <note>original: *Grand Grimoire*</note> reappeared at Nîmes in 1823 and is, in all respects, identical to the work entitled the *Red Dragon*, of which there are several examples. <gloss>3</gloss> The *Grimoire of Honorius* is exceedingly rare in its original form, but it is better known by the reprints of 1660 and 1670, though these are also scarce. There is, finally, an edition dated 1760, and this even commands a high price among collectors. It remains to be said that around the middle of the 18th century, Abbé d'Artigny <note>Antoine Gachet d'Artigny (1706–1778), a French clergyman and writer known for his historical and literary research.</note> was presented with a manuscript copy of this Grimoire that was much more complete than the printed editions. He gives no satisfactory account of it, nor can it be traced today. Possibly it represented the transition of the medieval *Sworn Book of Honorius* <note>The *Liber Juratus*, a much older 13th-century magical text</note> into the *Fake Papal Constitution*, which certainly reproduces the themes and moves in the atmosphere of its predecessor.

### **§ 5. Minor and Spurious Rituals of Black Magic**

At first glance, attempting to distinguish certain books as more "spurious" <term>spurious: fake, forged, or not what it claims to be</term> than others in a collection of forged literature seems like a pointless exercise in criticism. However, there are some Rituals of Black Magic that are merely the dishonest schemes of profit-seeking publishers, while there are others—predating that period and originating outside the usual centers of the book-peddling trade <term>colportage: the distribution of cheap, sensational books by traveling peddlers</term>—which have never had any real influence and are, in fact, generally unknown. Neither class has a significant history or has contributed anything meaningful to the subject. Yet, it does not follow that they offer no points of curiosity or interest, and some account of them must be provided here.

The *True Little Book of the Jesuits* <note>original: "Verus Jesuitarum Libellus"</note>—or "True Magical Work of the Jesuits, containing most powerful conjurations for all evil spirits of whatever state, condition, and office they are, and a most powerful and approved conjuration of the Spirit Uriel; <gloss>1</gloss> to which is added Cyprian's Invocation of Angels, and his Conjuration of the Spirits guarding Hidden Treasures, together with a form for their dismissal"—claims to have been published at Paris in the Latin language in the year 1508. It was reprinted by Scheible at Stuttgart in 1845, forming part of the curious collection of Faust documents mentioned earlier. Finally, in 1875, the late Major Herbert Irwin made, or privately obtained, an English translation which remains in manuscript. The date placed on the title page of the...

<summary>This page concludes a bibliography of various editions of the Key of Solomon and the Red Dragon before introducing Section 5, which examines minor and forged magical texts like the "True Little Book of the Jesuits."</summary>
<keywords>Grimoire, Black Magic, Key of Solomon, Red Dragon, Jesuits, Uriel, Saint Cyprian, bibliography, occult history</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the evaluation of forged or misattributed magical texts, specifically focusing on the "True Little Book of the Jesuits" and the "Magical Practice of Faust," noting their historical inaccuracies.</meta>

The original edition immediately reveals the fraud. It is almost unnecessary to point out that in the first decade of 

<page-num>111</page-num>

the sixteenth century, the Jesuits did not exist. The Society of Jesus was founded by Saint Ignatius, who died in 1556—two years after the Society was officially confirmed by Pope Paul III. The conjurations in this book are extremely strange. The first is addressed to a nameless spirit who is supposedly obedient to the biblical patriarchs Abraham and Isaac. This spirit is ordered to bring the magician millions of coins of the finest Spanish gold from the depths of the sea. The exact number is not specified and depends entirely on the greed <note>original: cupidity</note> of the practitioner. If the spirit fails, the conjuration threatens: "I will condemn your body and your soul." In the second ritual formula, the spirit is summoned by the knowledge and exorcising power of Agrippa. <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535) was a famous occultist; his mention here proves the book could not have been written before his rise to fame.</note> This again sets a clear limit on how old the collection could possibly be, if further proof were even needed. 

The third invocation is addressed to the spirit *Zayariel*, who is summoned using the names *Agla Scheffert* and the great *Jehovah Podashocheia*. The remaining rituals, totaling seven in all, are nearly identical in style and exact in their purpose: the demon is always required to bring the practitioner's desires from the depths of the sea, the watery abyss, or the spiritual abyss. The "Discharge" or "Absolution" <note>A ritual closing used to dismiss the spirit and release them from the magician's service</note> that ends the series is actually just another conjuration.

The *Summoning of Saint Cyprian* is presumably an experiment in what is called "White Magic," since it is addressed to an angel who was a guest of the biblical figures Lot and Abraham. Its goal is simply "help in time of need." Because of its simplicity, it is apparently suitable for every difficulty in life. It should be noted for reference by those who might find it useful, though it is too long and tedious to include in these pages. A similar observation applies only too well to the *Process for the Magical Acquisition of Hidden Treasures*. However, it is 

<page-num>112</page-num>

much more complete than the others and is so closely connected to the "highest good" <note>original: *Summum Bonum*</note>—the ultimate goal of all ceremonial magic—that it is necessary to include it. Nevertheless, it is an operation of necromancy <note>Magic involving the dead or spirits of the underworld</note> and will be found in its proper place in the second part of this book. <gloss>1</gloss> *The True Little Book of the Jesuits* <note>original: *Verus Jesuitarum Libellus*</note> ends with a dark and smoky <note>original: fuliginous; meaning soot-covered or hellish</note> conjuration of the entire hierarchy of Hell <note>original: *Infernus*</note>. This ritual continues for many pages and contains more nonsensical words than several other grimoires combined. Since nothing is known about its original edition, it is impossible to explain the origins of this strange fraud.

The *Magical Practice of Faust* <note>original: *Praxis Magica Fausti*</note>, or the "Magical Elements of Dr. John Faust, Practitioner of Medicine," claims to have been printed from the original manuscript <note>original: MS.</note> in the Municipal Library of Weimar. It is dated 1571, a time when, it must be respectfully stated, no such municipal library existed in the city where Goethe was born. Furthermore, the library's actual collection does not include this manuscript. It cannot be said for certain whether the original edition was backdated, as it is extremely rare. I only know of it through a reprint by Scheible and an unpublished transcript by Major Irwin. The work consists of a few strange illustrations in the style of the seventeenth century and some nonsensical...

He describes rituals for summoning gold from the sea and mentions a process for finding hidden treasures that he classifies as necromancy.</summary>

conjurations, all exceedingly brief. The third of these exhorts the Evil Spirit on the curious <note>original: quaint</note> grounds that it is now the time of the Great Name *Tetragrammaton* <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, usually transcribed as YHVH</note>. The purpose of the summoning is not indicated; the formulas are Christian, broken up by innumerable crosses and by names and terms which defy any guess as to their meaning. The hierarchy of the spirit is determined by the closing words: "I command you, O Spirit Rumoar, even by Lucifer, your mighty sovereign."

### § 6. The Black Pullet

*The Black Pullet* <note>original: La Poule Noire</note>, the *Druid of Menapienne*, *Red Magic*, or the *Cream of the Occult Sciences*, along with works derived from the first of these, such as the *Queen of the Hairy Flies*, the *Green Butterfly*, etc., form a class by themselves. With one exception, they are quite unserious publications which can scarcely be called forgeries <note>original: spurious</note>, as they make almost no pretense of being genuine ancient texts.<gloss>1</gloss> They belong to the late eighteenth century. Dr. Encausse <note>Gérard Encausse (1865–1916), better known as "Papus," a famous French occultist</note>, the head of the French Martinists <note>A mystical order following the teachings of Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin</note>, suggests that they were all fabricated at Rome. He infers—as seen previously—that we owe them to the industry of priests. This conclusion seems to follow somewhat loosely from the evidence; it is characteristic of him and his school of thought, and is indeed of much the same value as the statement in the book *Isis Unveiled* <note>An influential occult book by H.P. Blavatsky</note> that the habitual practice of *Black Magic* at the Vatican could be "easily proved."

The *Black Pullet* is by far the most curious of its class, and there is indeed enough individuality in its narrative to lift it much above the petty impostures with which it is grouped. Its chief occult interest centers on the series of talismanic rings which it incorporates into the text, which is itself a type of magical romance. It makes no claim to antiquity, except to say that it embodies ancient wisdom, and it does not appeal to the authority of Solomon. In a book of *Black Magic*—which it certainly is, though its Goetic <note>original: Goëtic; referring to the practice of conjuring demons or dark spirits</note> intention is disguised—such modesty is a virtue. Many of the talismans seem to be original designs; at least, they do not connect with any occult symbolism known to the present writer. At the same time, they are constructed according to the rules laid down by the *Fourth Book* attributed to Cornelius Agrippa regarding "infernal signatures" <note>Symbolic "autographs" or marks associated with specific demons</note>.
