# CHAPTER IX

## Concerning Infernal Necromancy

<summary>This page describes a ritual from the Grimorium Verum for summoning spirits using the "Three Rings of Solomon," involving specific incense, an elder rod, and the recitation of the 50th Psalm. It concludes by introducing the topic of Infernal Necromancy.</summary>

<keywords>Three Rings of Solomon, Grimorium Verum, ritual magic, magic circle, elder rod, Miserere mei, pentacles, spirit evocation, incense, Infernal Necromancy</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Three Rings of Solomon" and transitions into Chapter IX, "Infernal Necromancy," contrasting ancient practices with 19th-century spiritualism.</meta>

It is only within recent times that the attempt to communicate with the dead has been elevated to the status <note>original: "dignity"</note> of White Magic. At this point, it is necessary to state clearly that the phenomena of Modern Spiritualism <note>A 19th-century movement focused on communicating with the dead through mediums, distinct from ritual magic</note> must be distinguished from the practices of ancient Necromancy <note>The practice of communicating with the dead to predict the future or perform magic</note>. While they share a similar purpose, their methods are fundamentally different. To compare them would be almost the same as saying that the art of physical Alchemy is similar to modern business because both aim to acquire wealth. To fully evaluate the claims of Modern Spiritualism would exceed the scope of this book; it is mentioned here only to set it completely apart.

It should be added, however, that occult writers—with the lack of discrimination common to their kind—have sometimes ambitiously tried to represent communication with departed souls through Ceremonial Magic as something much more noble than mere Spiritualism. In reality, the opposite is closer to the truth. Ancient Necromancy involved barbaric and horrific rites; it was only under the influence of Éliphas Lévi and Pierre Christian <note>Two influential 19th-century French occultists who sought to "civilize" magical traditions</note> that it was "cleaned up" and civilized. Yet, in the hands of these sophisticated magicians, it has become simply a process of self-hallucination <note>original: "auto-hallucination"</note>, having no scientific value whatsoever.

The secret of true evocation belongs to the hidden occult sanctuaries, according to those who speak for them. This secret is not found in the practices of Spiritualism, and even less—as far as can be gathered—is it found in the magical rituals. Furthermore, this secret would likely not be valued by those who truly possess it, because the higher soul of humanity is beyond the reach of summoning <note>original: "evocation"</note>, and whatever *does* respond to such a call would necessarily be a spirit far lower than the magician <note>original: "initiate"</note>. The claim of such power is naturally one of delusion mixed with fraud.

In any case, the Necromancy found in these rituals is, strictly speaking, a branch of Black Magic. For this reason, it was undoubtedly excluded from the "theurgic" <note>Theurgy: "God-working," or magic aimed at divine connection</note> system of the *Arbatel*. Even composite works like the two *Keys of Solomon* and the *Magical Elements* do not contain any account of a practice that was always viewed with horror <note>original: "held in execration"</note>. It was apparently considered lawful for the Magician <note>original: "Magus"</note> to conjure and command demons, and to torture the hierarchy of Hell <note>original: "Infernus"</note> using the power of Divine Names, but he was required to leave the dead to their rest.

Where the process *is* described, as in the *Fourth Book* attributed to Cornelius Agrippa <note>This "Fourth Book" is widely considered "pseudo-Agrippa," a work falsely attributed to the famous occultist after his death</note>, it is limited to the summoning of souls who might reasonably be assumed to be damned, and it involves disgusting <note>original: "revolting"</note> rites. It assumes that a person who lived an evil life carries the depraved desires that corrupted them on earth into the next world, and the ritual lures them back through their lingering connection to their discarded body. For this reason, the use of blood was seen as essential, because blood was believed to be the carrier of physical life. Likewise, a portion of the body itself—whether flesh or bone—was required for the rite. It goes without saying that summonings involving such materials belong to Black Magic, and they offer no redeeming qualities for an informed student to consider.

"It is also to be understood," says the author of the pseudo-Agrippa, "that...

<summary>Waite distinguishes ancient Necromancy from modern Spiritualism, arguing that the former is a gruesome branch of Black Magic involving blood and remains, which was excluded from more "divine" magical texts.</summary>
<keywords>Necromancy, Modern Spiritualism, Black Magic, Éliphas Lévi, Arbatel, Keys of Solomon, pseudo-Agrippa, evocation, theurgy</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Necromancy, moving from the general theory of communicating with the dead to the specific requirements for locations and the actual rituals used to summon spirits.</meta>

"...those who are proposing to raise up the souls of any deceased persons must do so in places with which it is known that they were familiar, in which some special connection <note>original: "alliance"</note> between soul and body may be assumed, or some species of lingering emotional attachment <note>original: "attracting affection"</note> still draws the soul to such places. . . . Therefore, the locations most suited for the purpose are churchyards, and, better still, those which have been the scene of the execution of criminal judgments"—in plain words, the immediate neighborhood of a gallows <note>original: "gibbet"</note>. A battlefield or other place of public slaughter is still more favorable, but best of all is the scene of a murder before the corpse <note>original: "carcase"</note> has been removed.

The ritual of Necromantic Evocation <note>The practice of summoning the spirits of the dead</note> is indicated but not given by the authority just cited; we must seek it in the works of Ebenezer Sibley <note>An 18th-century English physician and astrologer who wrote on the occult</note> and in the supplementary portions of the *Grand Grimoire* and the *Red Dragon*. The astrologer Sibley does not give an account of his sources, but they were evidently not in printed books. The Sloane Manuscript <note>A collection of historic documents held in the British Library</note> numbered 3884 in the Library of the British Museum would appear to have been one. In any case, it is not an invented process; it develops the principles laid down in pseudo-Agrippa <note>Works falsely attributed to Henry Cornelius Agrippa, a famous 16th-century occultist</note> and is quite in harmony with the malevolent <note>original: "baleful"</note> nature of Black Magic. It is provided here word-for-word.

But if, instead of hellish <note>original: "infernal"</note> or familiar spirits, the ghost or apparition of a departed person is to be summoned <note>original: "exorcised"</note>, the Magician, with his assistant, must go to the churchyard or tomb where the deceased was buried, exactly at midnight, as the ceremony can only be performed in the night between the hours of twelve and one. The grave is first to be opened, or an opening made by which access may be had to the naked body. The magician, having described the circle and holding a magic wand in his right hand, while his companion or assistant carries a consecrated torch, turns himself to all the four winds, and, touching the dead body three times with the magical wand, repeats as follows:

—*By the virtue of the Holy Resurrection, and the torments of the damned, I conjure and command you, Spirit of [Name of the deceased], to answer my rightful <note>original: "liege"</note> demands, being obedient to these sacred ceremonies, on pain of everlasting torment and distress. . . . BERALD, BEROALD, BALBIN, GAB, GABOR, AGABA. Arise, arise, I charge and command you.* 

After these forms and ceremonies, the ghost or apparition will become visible and will answer any questions put to it by the magician. But if it is desired to question the spirit of any corpse that has hanged, drowned, or otherwise committed suicide <note>original: "made away with itself"</note>, the conjuration must be performed while the body lies on the spot where it is first found after the suicide has been committed, and before it is touched or removed. The ceremony is as follows: The magician binds upon the top of his wand a bundle of St. John’s wort or *Hypericum perforatum* <note>original: "Millies perforatum," a likely OCR error or archaic variation of the botanical name for St. John's wort</note>, with the head of an owl; and having gone to the spot where the corpse lies, at twelve o'clock at night, he draws the circle and solemnly repeats these words:

—*By the mysteries of the deep, by the flames of Banal, by the Power of the East and the silence of the night, by the Holy Rites of Hecate <note>The ancient Greek goddess of magic, crossroads, and ghosts</note>, I conjure and command you, you distressed spirit, to present yourself here and reveal to me the cause of your calamity, why you did violence to your own life, where you are now existing, and where you will be hereafter.*

<summary>This page details the grim requirements for necromancy, specifying that rituals should occur in places of death or execution, and provides specific incantations for summoning the ghosts of the buried or of those who committed suicide.</summary>
<keywords>Necromancy, Ebenezer Sibley, Black Magic, conjuration, Hecate, Sloane MS 3884, funeral rites, ritual magic</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for a necromantic ritual, detailing the final conjurations used to compel a spirit to re-enter its body and the subsequent questioning of the deceased.</meta>

Then gently striking <note>original: "smiting"</note> the corpse <note>original: "carcase"</note> nine times with the rod, he adds:

> *I conjure you, Spirit of this [Name] <note>The letter "N." stands for the Latin "Nomen," indicating where the magician would insert the name of the deceased.</note> who has passed away, to answer the demands that I propose to you, as you ever hope for the rest of the holy ones and relief from all your misery; by the*

<page-num>327</page-num>

> *Blood of Jesus which He shed for your soul, I conjure and bind you to speak to me what I shall ask you.*

Then, cutting down the corpse from the tree, they shall lay its head toward the east. While the following conjuration is being repeated, they shall set a brazier <note>original: "chafing-dish"</note> of fire at its right hand, into which they shall pour a little wine, some mastic <note>A resin obtained from the mastic tree, often used in incense.</note>, and some aromatic gum, and lastly the contents of a vial full of the finest oil. They shall also have a pair of bellows and some unlit charcoal to make the fire burn brightly when the corpse rises. The conjuration is this:

> *I conjure you, Spirit of [Name], that you immediately enter into your former body again and answer my demands; by the virtue of the Holy Resurrection, and by the posture of the body of the Saviour of the world, I charge you, I conjure you, I command you, on pain of the torments and wandering of twenty-one years <note>original: "thrice seven years"</note> which I, by the force of sacred magic rites, have power to inflict upon you; by your sighs and groans I conjure you to speak. So help you God and the prayers of the Holy Church. Amen.*

This ceremony being repeated three times while the fire is burning with mastic and aromatic gum, the body will begin to rise, and at last will stand upright before the exorcist. It will answer with a faint and hollow voice the questions proposed to it: why it destroyed itself, where its dwelling is, what its food and life are, how long it will be before it enters into rest, and by what means the magician may help it to find rest; also concerning the treasures of this world and where they are hidden. Moreover, it can answer very precisely <note>original: "punctually"</note> concerning the places where ghosts reside and the manner of communicating with them, teaching the nature of Astral Spirits <note>In occultism, these are spirits belonging to the "astral plane," a dimension between the physical and the purely spiritual.</note> and hellish beings as far as its capacity allows.

<page-num>328</page-num>

When the ghost has fully answered all of this, the magician—out of compassion and reverence for the deceased—ought to use whatever means can possibly be used to procure rest for the spirit. To this effect, he must dig a grave and, filling it half full of quicklime <note>A caustic chemical used historically to speed the decomposition of bodies.</note> with a little salt and common sulfur, must put the naked corpse into it. Next to burning the body into ashes, this is of great power to quiet and end the disturbance of the Astral Spirit. 

But in this and in all cases where the ghosts or apparitions of deceased persons are raised up and consulted, the magician must observe great caution to stay strictly within the magic circle. For if, by the alignment and position of the stars at his birth <note>original: "nativity"</note>, he is among those who follow the Black Art for wicked <note>original: "iniquitous"</note> purposes, it is very dangerous to conjure any spirits without drawing the form of the circle and wearing upon the heart, or holding in the hand, the Pentacle of Solomon <note>A powerful protective talisman or symbol used to constrain spirits.</note>. For the ghosts of deceased men can easily cause sudden death to...

<summary>This page describes the final steps of a necromantic ritual where a corpse is magically reanimated to answer questions about the afterlife and hidden treasures, followed by instructions for its ritual burial.</summary>
<keywords>necromancy, ritual magic, conjuration, Astral Spirits, Pentacle of Solomon, reanimation, quicklime, Ebenezer Sibley</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of necromantic rituals, moving from the physical reanimation of a corpse to more symbolic and extreme methods of spirit communication, specifically focusing on the pursuit of buried treasure.</meta>

the magician born under such a planetary alignment, even while the act of exorcism is taking place.

It must be admitted that this process is grim and depressing, and the student of the occult will not envy the sorcerer at the first sign of life in the corpse. Yet this rite is methodical, and even restrained, when compared with the monstrous alternative found in the *Grand Grimoire* <note>A famous and notorious book of black magic, also known as "The Red Dragon."</note>. This alternative must be given on the authority of Eliphas Lévi <note>A prominent 19th-century French occultist whose writings heavily influenced modern magic.</note>; for no available editions of the work in question, nor the *Red Dragon*, nor indeed any ritual I am familiar with, contains it. There is a reasonable probability that Lévi invented it to support his argument at the time.

> "There are also necromantic processes involving digging up earth from graves with your fingernails, dragging out some of the bones, and setting them in the shape of a cross on your chest. One must then attend midnight mass on Christmas Eve and fly out of the church at the moment of consecration, shouting: 'Let the dead rise from their tombs!' You then return to the graveyard, take a handful of earth nearest to a coffin, and run back to the church door (which has been alerted by the noise). After placing the two bones in a cross shape, you must again shout: 'Let the dead rise from their tombs!' Then, if you escape being captured and committed to a madhouse, you must walk away at a slow pace, counting four thousand five hundred steps in a straight line—which involves either following a main road or climbing over walls. Finally, after traveling this distance, you must lie flat on the ground as if in a coffin, repeating in mournful tones: 'Let the dead rise from their tombs!' and calling three times for the person whose spirit you wish to appear."

The goal of necromantic evocations <note>The act of calling forth the spirits of the dead.</note> was much the same as the other operations found in the Grimoires. If the sorcerer of old—like the modern magician—had ever disturbed the spirit of Apollonius <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a famous Greek philosopher and reputed magician of the 1st century AD.</note> from his eternal rest, it would have been to ask about money. The final process in Necromancy will therefore be a fitting conclusion to our entire inquiry, as it is designed to summon and dismiss a human spirit who is believed to guard a hidden treasure. It is taken from *The True Little Book of the Jesuits* <note>original: *Verus Jesuitarum Libellus*</note>, and it represents the ultimate peak <note>original: *ne plus ultra*</note> of Ceremonial Magic, regardless of how it is categorized. The end goal of everything is money, says the sorcerer; if asked to define Occult Science, he would answer that it was the method for obtaining hidden wealth. The evidence of this entire body of literature agrees with that definition.

Giovanni Pico della Mirandola <note>original: "Picus de Mirandola," a famous 15th-century Italian Renaissance philosopher</note> claimed that the most primitive and incomprehensible words are the most powerful in Black Magic. This claim applies everywhere in this study, for the whole art is characterized by an uneducated <note>original: "illiterate"</note> delight in unintelligible imagery. Since the examples cited here from *The True Little Book of the Jesuits* <note>original: "Verus Jesuitarum Libellus"</note> seem in most cases to be random <note>original: "fortuitous"</note> jumbles of letters, they are undoubtedly everything a magician could desire. However, if the spirit of the deceased miser still refuses to appear, the reader will easily think of additional threats and summons <note>original: "constraints and conjurations"</note>.

## Footnotes

324:1 The more personal knowledge the magician possessed about the deceased person, the more easily he was supposedly able to summon them.

326:1 The process by which the soul was called back into the dead body before its burial constituted <term>necromancy</term> <note>divination by communicating with the dead</note> in its literal sense. Summoning only the "shadow" or ghost was sometimes called <term>sciomancy</term> <note>divination by shades or spirits</note>.

# CONCLUSION

The ceremonial literature of White and Black Magic has now spoken for itself in the universal <note>original: "catholic"</note> sense of the words. We see exactly what the rituals claimed to offer to those who followed their alleged <note>original: "putative"</note> practical instructions. I assume once more that it is unnecessary to debate whether the Olympic Spirits <note>Seven spirits mentioned in the Arbatel of Magic who were said to rule the various regions of the world</note> will actually...

<summary>Waite concludes his survey of Black Magic rituals by discussing the use of "barbarous names" and begins a broader conclusion on the reality versus the claims of ceremonial magic.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Verus Jesuitarum Libellus, Apophis, necromancy, sciomancy, Olympic Spirits, barbarous names, Grimoire</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the book's conclusion, moving from a discussion of specific black magic rituals to an overall critique of the motivations behind ceremonial magic and the nature of the "composite" rituals that claim to be "white" or "transcendental" magic.</meta>

To make gold in an instant through Magic, to transport precious stones, to prolong life for several hundred years, to teach all arts, and to provide the practitioner with serving spirits in visible and physical form—these claims merely express the greed <note>original: "cupidity"</note> and other desires of the magician. This is the realm of occult work that sometimes claims the title of "transcendental" <note>meaning supernatural or surpassing ordinary limits</note>. However, its entire purpose and scope are limited to low material gain and pleasure. The ambition of the Magus <note>a master of magic</note> was to secure these advantages—firstly—through the trickery and shortcuts of the occult world instead of through his own hard work, and—secondly—on a much larger scale than was normally possible.

When we look at the Rituals I have classified as "composite" <note>rituals that mix elements of both "white" and "black" magic</note>, we find that we are dealing with a much more valued and popular series of handbooks. In the polite opinion of occult circles, the crown jewel of these is considered "white" <note>good or holy</note> by its very nature, and only <term>Goëtic</term> <note>relating to low magic or the summoning of demons</note> in its later additions. In reality, it is Goëtic in both intent and essence. It is "white" only in the sense that some of its confused processes might be called 

<page-num>335</page-num>

harmless, if one ignores their bloodthirsty nature. It is obvious from the text, however, that the spirits <note>original: "Intelligences"</note> being summoned are fallen spirits. Indeed, one of the main concerns during their appearance is the hideousness of their natural forms. The primary goals of these experiments are: (1) the recovery of stolen goods; (2) the power to become invisible (for reasons that are obvious even if left unsaid); (3) the discovery of buried treasure; and (4) the seeking of love and favor. The remaining goals belong more properly to the realm of folklore and Fairyland. 

This is the reality of the work that is accepted as the very source and foundation of the Art. While the **Infernal Hierarchy** of the ***Lemegeton*** <note>also known as *The Lesser Key of Solomon*, a famous 17th-century book of magic</note> occasionally promises more important things, the "diabolism" <note>devil-worship or demonic magic</note> of practical magic was mostly of a low-brow, popular variety. Those documents that aimed too high—for example, at logic, philosophy, the liberal sciences, eloquence, and true understanding—had very few followers <note>original: "votaries"</note>. To grant riches, to ignite love and lust, and to discover treasures—as these were the peak of human ambition, they were also the primary skills demanded from the spirits. The people attracted to these goals were clearly those who could not find satisfaction through normal means: the outcasts, the incompetent, the ignorant, the lonely, the deformed, the hideous, the powerless, and those to whom both Nature and God <note>original: "Grace"</note> had denied success.

This is the same category into which the modern "psychic" mind would unknowingly fall, if I could believe for a moment that there has been any real revival of Ceremonial Magic in the nineteenth or twentieth century (outside of specific circles in places like Paris). The typical student of the occult is foolish enough in his interests, but when he takes these **Grimoires** <note>textbooks of magic</note> seriously, he usually assumes there is a deeper meaning behind them. He does

<page-num>336</page-num>

not necessarily view them as allegories, but rather as the final, corrupted version of something that he imagines was once real knowledge. This trend has been encouraged by the dishonest <note>original: "knavish"</note> theory that occult...

<summary>Waite argues that ceremonial magic is driven by greed and a desire for material shortcuts, appealing primarily to social outcasts. He dismisses the idea that these "composite" rituals are truly "white" or high magic, viewing them instead as low-brow attempts to summon fallen spirits for selfish gains.</summary>

<keywords>Ceremonial Magic, Magus, Goëtic magic, Lemegeton, Grimoires, Infernal Hierarchy, material gain, occultism, Arthur Edward Waite</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page concludes the book’s analysis, arguing that ceremonial magic is a corruption of true spiritual tradition. It connects these rituals to a debased side of historical Jewish mysticism, contrasting them with higher works like the Zohar.</meta>

<page-num>337</page-num>

the sanctuaries I mentioned much earlier in this work, and to which I must refer again for a moment in these final words.

As part of the foundation from which this false art of spirits arises, there is the supposed effectiveness of adjuration <note>the act of commanding or charging someone solemnly</note>, prayer, and ceremonial acts of worship performed in connection with it. But in Magic, that effectiveness can only be shown in matters that are trivial or disgusting. It is obvious that for any higher purpose, we should turn to those things through the usual channels of religion. If the theory of prayer is true, Magic is irrelevant regarding holy things because there is a more excellent way of obtaining great, good, and eternal gifts. But if the theory of prayer is not true, Magic is also irrelevant because it depends on and falls to the earth with that same false assumption at its core.

In reality, Magic—whether White or Black—is an attempt to direct this accepted effectiveness into evil channels. It tries to force the infernal ranks to obey with the assistance of the blessed angels <note>original: "hierarchies"</note>, and to enlist the sympathies of the angels for foolish or shameful tasks based on the authority of prayers intended to please them. I should add that I define the very essence of evil as the desire for power that gives the owner an advantage over those around them through hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> interventions, against which it would generally be impossible to protect oneself. I define the acquisition of knowledge without study or effort in the same way and for the same reasons. Other ambitions, desires, and greeds do not need me to explicitly condemn them, for they speak clearly enough for themselves. Based on its own assumptions, this fake art or science belongs to the abyss. In most cases, however, it remains merely an abyss of evil intent; the practitioner is left with their low, greedy, pathetic, or foolish ambition, but without actually achieving it.

It may seem at first glance that I have been "breaking ugly worms on wheels" <note>a variation of the phrase "breaking a butterfly on a wheel," meaning to use excessive force or analysis on a small or insignificant problem</note> larger than would have been required for giants like Gargantua, Behemoth, or Leviathan. But my primary concern is to keep the Secret Tradition <note>the supposed hidden line of spiritual wisdom and mysticism</note> pure and to separate it from the diseased and monstrous growths that have come to cover it. The magic found in these Ceremonial Rituals is no part of the real tradition; in any form we know, it is not one of its hidden layers, nor is it even a degraded version of it. 

At the same time—as we have sufficiently seen—these rituals do represent, at a very great distance, a "contrary" <note>original: "averse"</note> tradition. It is the side of Jewish tradition produced during their long and centuries-old exile. There is no question that the Jewish mind, which produced the great mystical testimony of the Zoharic <note>referring to the Zohar, the foundational work of Jewish Kabbalah</note> literature on one hand, also focused on the types of preoccupations that resulted in Ceremonial Magic as their final evolution in the dark pools of thought. This is their only point of importance. It is this alone which has justified this current study and the length to which it has extended.

<summary>Waite concludes that ceremonial magic is an attempt to misuse religious prayer for selfish or evil ends, labeling it a "pseudo-art." He distinguishes these rituals from the "Secret Tradition" and connects their origin to a debased side of historical Jewish mysticism.</summary>

<keywords>Secret Tradition, Ceremonial Magic, Black Magic, White Magic, Jewish Mysticism, Zohar, ethics of magic, occult power</keywords>
